## F001

**Question:** In Figure 1B-1, what happens immediately after FHWA approves a request to experiment?

**Grade:** C=1.00, Comp=1.00, F=1.00, Cit=1.00, Strict full credit=Yes

**Descriptive mean:** 100.0% (Full credit)

**Evaluation confidence:** 88%

**Rationale:** The response received full credit because the audit accepted the substantive answer, coverage of required elements, grounding, and evidence localization without a deduction. The locked benchmark marks the question as answerable, so the response needed to state and support the required MUTCD answer. Correctness rationale: The response states the locked conclusion, rule, value, or interpretation correctly. Completeness rationale: The audit accepted the response as covering all required answer elements. Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 1B.05, Figure 1B-1, page 7, p.7, p.8, p.6. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: The requesting jurisdiction installs the experimental traffic control device. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=1.000 and Context Precision@6=1.000. The visual-routing decision matched the locked modality requirement. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer. The question required 1 locked visual entity or entities. Exact visual hit=1.00, visual recall=1.00, and visual precision=1.00.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: According to Figure 1B-1, once the FHWA answers "Yes" at the "Approved?" decision point, the requesting jurisdiction installs the experimental traffic control device. From there the process branches into two parallel activities: evaluating the experimental device and providing semi-annual reports to FHWA Division & HQ, both of which ultimately lead to the jurisdiction providing FHWA a copy of the final report. (If not approved, the jurisdiction instead responds to questions raised by FHWA and the request returns to FHWA Review.) Standards (mandatory): - Approval itself is a prerequisite: "A traffic control device or application that does not comply with the provisions of this Manual shall not be used on any street, highway, bikeway, or site roadway open to public travel... without first receiving official approval to experiment from the FHWA's Office of Transportation Operations." Visual evidence: [Image 1] shows the "Approved?" diamond with the "Yes" arrow leading directly to the box "Requesting jurisdiction installs experimental traffic control device," which then branches to "Evaluate experimental traffic control device" and "Requesting jurisdiction provides semi-annual reports to FHWA Division & HQ." [Image 2] presents the same Figure 1B-1 flowchart in full-page context on page 7 of the Manual, confirming the same sequence following the "Yes" branch. Citations: - Figure 1B-1 (p.7) - Section 1B.05 Support §9 (p.8) - Section 1B.05 Standard §2 (p.6) - Page 48

**Gold answer:** The requesting jurisdiction installs the experimental traffic control device.

## F002

**Question:** In Figure 1B-2, if a proposed change is accepted for Federal rulemaking, what process follows before a Final Rule is published?

**Grade:** C=1.00, Comp=1.00, F=1.00, Cit=1.00, Strict full credit=Yes

**Descriptive mean:** 100.0% (Full credit)

**Evaluation confidence:** 88%

**Rationale:** The response received full credit because the audit accepted the substantive answer, coverage of required elements, grounding, and evidence localization without a deduction. The locked benchmark marks the question as answerable, so the response needed to state and support the required MUTCD answer. Correctness rationale: The response states the locked conclusion, rule, value, or interpretation correctly. Completeness rationale: The audit accepted the response as covering all required answer elements. Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 1B.07, Section 1B.06, Section 1B.02, Figure 1B-2, Page 52, p.9. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: FHWA develops provisions for a Notice of Proposed Amendments, publishes the notice in the Federal Register, holds a docket comment period, reviews comments, prepares the Final Rul… Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=0.944 and Context Precision@6=0.667. The visual-routing decision matched the locked modality requirement. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer. The question required 1 locked visual entity or entities. Exact visual hit=0.00, visual recall=0.00, and visual precision=0.00.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: According to Figure 1B-2, once a proposed change is accepted for Federal rulemaking, FHWA develops device provisions for inclusion in the next Notice of Proposed Amendments, publishes that Notice in the Federal Register, holds a docket comment period, reviews the comments received, and then prepares the Final Rule before publishing it. In parallel, the change may also be evaluated for interim approval, which allows optional use of the device under specified conditions until the official rulemaking is complete. After the Final Rule is published, State manuals must come into substantial conformance with the National MUTCD within 2 years. Standards (mandatory): - "States or other Federal agencies that have their own MUTCDs or Supplements shall revise these MUTCDs or Supplements to be in substantial conformance with changes to the National MUTCD within 2 years of the effective date of the Final Rule for the changes [23 CFR 655.603(b)(3)]." Options (permitted): - Per the interim approval branch of the process, "Interim approval allows for the optional use of a traffic control device or application and does not create a new mandate or recommendation for its use," subject to conditions that jurisdictions, toll facility operators, or owners of site roadways open to public travel agree to comply with "until an official rulemaking action has occurred." Visual evidence: [Image 1] Figure 1B-2 shows that a "YES" answer at the "Accepted for Federal rulemaking?" decision leads down the rulemaking track — "FHWA develops device provisions for inclusion in the next Notice of Proposed Amendments" → "FHWA publishes Notice of Proposed Amendments in Federal Register" → "Docket comment period" → "FHWA reviews comments" → "FHWA prepares Final Rule" → "FHWA publishes Final Rule" — with a parallel "Interim approval?" branch and a post-rule box requiring State manual conformance within 2 years per 23 CFR 655.603(a). [Image 3] Page 52 provides context that a diagram indicating the process for incorporating new traffic control devices into the Manual is shown in Figure 1B-2, and that interim approval allows optional provisional use pending official rulemaking. Citations: - Figure 1B-2 (p.9) - Section 1B.07 Support §3 (p.11) - Section 1B.06 Support §4 (p.9) - Section 1B.07 Support §4 (p.11) - Section 1B.02 Standard §2 (p.4)

**Gold answer:** FHWA develops provisions for a Notice of Proposed Amendments, publishes the notice in the Federal Register, holds a docket comment period, reviews comments, prepares the Final Rule, and publishes it.

## F004

**Question:** In Figure 2A-2, what minimum mounting height and lateral offset are shown for a basic roadside sign in a rural area without a wider shoulder?

**Grade:** C=1.00, Comp=1.00, F=1.00, Cit=1.00, Strict full credit=Yes

**Descriptive mean:** 100.0% (Full credit)

**Evaluation confidence:** 88%

**Rationale:** The response received full credit because the audit accepted the substantive answer, coverage of required elements, grounding, and evidence localization without a deduction. The locked benchmark marks the question as answerable, so the response needed to state and support the required MUTCD answer. Correctness rationale: The response states the locked conclusion, rule, value, or interpretation correctly. Completeness rationale: The audit accepted the response as covering all required answer elements. Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 2A.15, Section 2A.16, Section 2A.13, Figure 2A-2, Figure 2A-3, p.58. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: A 5-foot minimum mounting height and a 12-foot minimum lateral offset from the edge of the traveled way. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=1.000 and Context Precision@6=0.667. The visual-routing decision matched the locked modality requirement. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer. The question required 1 locked visual entity or entities. Exact visual hit=1.00, visual recall=1.00, and visual precision=1.00.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: For a basic roadside sign in a rural area without a shoulder wider than 6 feet, Figure 2A-2 (Panel A) shows a minimum mounting height of 5 feet, measured from the bottom of the sign to the elevation of the near edge of the pavement, and a minimum lateral offset of 12 feet from the edge of the traveled way. (Where a shoulder wider than 6 feet exists, Panel B shows the alternative 6-foot offset from the edge of the shoulder.) Standards (mandatory): - "In rural areas, the minimum height, measured vertically from the bottom of the sign to the elevation of the near edge of the pavement, of signs installed at the side of the road shall be 5 feet (see Figure 2A-2)." Guidance (recommended): - For post-mounted signs, the minimum lateral offset should be 12 feet from the edge of the traveled way; only if a shoulder wider than 6 feet exists should the minimum lateral offset be 6 feet from the edge of the shoulder. - Signs should be individually installed on separate posts or mountings except in specified cases (e.g., where one sign supplements another), consistent with the standardized locations illustrated in Figure 2A-2. Visual evidence: - [Image 1] Figure 2A-2, Panel A ("Roadside sign in rural area") depicts a STOP sign with "12 ft MIN." lateral offset from the edge of the traveled way and "5 ft MIN." mounting height, while Panel B shows the 6 ft MIN. offset case that applies only when a shoulder wider than 6 ft exists. - [Image 2] The full page view of Figure 2A-2 confirms the same Panel A dimensions (12 ft MIN. lateral offset, 5 ft MIN. height) for a rural roadside sign. - [Image 5] Figure 2A-3's note reinforces that the lateral offset is a minimum of 12 feet measured from the edge of the traveled way (or 6 feet from the edge of the shoulder). Citations: - Section 2A.15 Standard §4 (p.58) - Section 2A.16 Guidance §3 (p.60) - Section 2A.13 Guidance §6 (p.57) - Figure 2A-2 (p.51) - Page 92 - Page 93

**Gold answer:** A 5-foot minimum mounting height and a 12-foot minimum lateral offset from the edge of the traveled way.

Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: In the separate-intersections case, the paths of opposing left-turning vehicles cross Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=1.000 and Context Precision@6=0.333. The visual-routing decision matched the locked modality requirement. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer. The question required 1 locked visual entity or entities. Exact visual hit=1.00, visual recall=1.00, and visual precision=1.00.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: The distinguishing visual feature in Figure 2A-5 is the interaction of the opposing left-turn vehicle paths (shown as blue arrows). In Panel A ("Separate intersections"), the divided highway has a wide median and the paths of opposing left-turning vehicles cross each other within the median area; in Panel B ("Single intersection"), the median is narrow and the opposing left-turn paths curve past one another without crossing. Guidance (recommended): - A divided highway crossing should be signed and marked as separate intersections when both of the following conditions are present: "A. The paths of opposing left turns from the divided highway cross each other (see Figure 2A-5), and B. There is adequate storage in the interior approaches for the design vehicles expected to cross the divided highway." - "At the crossing of two divided highways, engineering judgment should be used to determine the number of separate intersections." Visual evidence: [Image 1] Figure 2A-5 shows Panel A labeled "Separate intersections (paths of opposing left-turning vehicles cross)" with a wide median where the two blue left-turn arrows intersect each other, and Panel B labeled "Single intersection (paths of opposing left-turning vehicles do not cross)" with a narrow median where the blue left-turn arrows sweep around each other without intersecting. [Image 2] Page 105 shows the same Figure 2A-5 in full-page context, confirming the panel labels and the crossing versus non-crossing left-turn path geometry as the distinguishing feature. Citations: - Figure 2A-5 (p.64) - Section 2A.23 Guidance §1 (p.63) - Section 2A.23 Guidance §3 (p.64) - Page 105

**Gold answer:** In the separate-intersections case, the paths of opposing left-turning vehicles cross; in the single-intersection case, those paths do not cross.

The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=0.983 and Context Precision@6=0.667. The visual-routing decision matched the locked modality requirement. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer. The question required 1 locked visual entity or entities. Exact visual hit=1.00, visual recall=1.00, and visual precision=1.00.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: In Figure 2B-1, the STOP (R1-1) sign is an octagon (red background with white legend and border), and the YIELD (R1-2) sign is a downward-pointing equilateral triangle (red band with white interior and red YIELD legend). The ALL-WAY (R1-3P) plaque has a white legend and border on a red background. Standards (mandatory): - "At intersections where all approaches are controlled by STOP signs (see Section 2B.12), an ALL-WAY (R1-3P) supplemental plaque (see Figure 2B-1) shall be mounted below each STOP sign. The ALL-WAY plaque shall have a white legend and border on a red background." - "When it is determined that a full stop is always required on an approach to an intersection, a STOP (R1-1) sign (see Figure 2B-1) shall be used." - "The YIELD (R1-2) sign (see Figure 2B-1) shall not be displayed using a changeable message sign." Visual evidence: - [Image 1] Figure 2B-1 shows the STOP (R1-1) sign as a red octagon with white legend, the YIELD (R1-2) sign as a red-and-white downward-pointing triangle, and the ALL-WAY (R1-3P) plaque as a small horizontal rectangle with white "ALL WAY" legend and border on a red background. - [Image 2] Page 115 (Section 2B.04/2B.05 text with Figure 2B-1) confirms the same shapes and colors: octagonal STOP sign, triangular YIELD sign, and red ALL-WAY plaque with white legend. - [Image 3] Page 1015 (Figure 7B-1) shows the same octagonal STOP and triangular YIELD symbols reproduced within school crossing signs, consistent with Figure 2B-1. - [Image 4] Page 835 (Figure 6G-1) again depicts the R1-1 as a red octagon and the R1-2 as a red-and-white triangle in temporary traffic control applications. - [Image 5] Page 159 (Figure 2B-20) shows octagonal STOP signs in an intersection signing layout, consistent with the R1-1 shape shown in Figure 2B-1. Citations: - Section 2B.04 Standard §4 (p.74) - Section 2B.04 Standard §1 (p.74) - Section 2B.05 Standard §2 (p.75) - Figure 2B-1 (p.74) - Page 115

**Gold answer:** STOP is an octagon; YIELD is a downward-pointing equilateral triangle; the ALL-WAY plaque has white legend and border on a red background.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: Figure 4I-4 shows the pedestrian intervals in this order: the Walk Interval (steady WALKING PERSON), followed by the Pedestrian Change Interval (flashing UPRAISED HAND, with optional countdown), followed by the Buffer Interval (steady UPRAISED HAND). The figure labels the first interval (Walk Interval) as "7 seconds MIN." (which may be reduced under some conditions) and the last interval (Buffer Interval) as "2 seconds MIN." Standards (mandatory): - "A pedestrian change interval consisting of a flashing UPRAISED HAND (symbolizing DONT WALK) signal indication shall begin immediately following the WALKING PERSON (symbolizing WALK) signal indication. Following the pedestrian change interval, a buffer interval consisting of a steady UPRAISED HAND (symbolizing DONT WALK) signal indication shall be displayed for at least 2 seconds prior to the release of any conflicting vehicular movement." - "The sum of the time of the pedestrian change interval and the buffer interval shall not be less than the calculated pedestrian clearance time... The buffer interval shall not begin later than the beginning of the red clearance interval, if used." Options (permitted): - The countdown display is optional for Pedestrian Change Intervals of 7 seconds or less; the Walk Interval may be reduced under some conditions; and the Buffer Interval, which shall always be provided and displayed, may be used to help satisfy the calculated pedestrian clearance time, or may begin after the calculated pedestrian clearance time has ended. - The additional time provided by an extended push button press to satisfy pedestrian clearance time needs may be added to either the walk interval or the pedestrian change interval. Visual evidence: [Image 1] Figure 4I-4 shows the interval sequence Walk Interval → Pedestrian Change Interval → Buffer Interval, with "7 seconds MIN.**" labeled under the Walk Interval and "2 seconds MIN." labeled at the Buffer Interval, along with the "Zero" point of the countdown display at the end of the pedestrian change interval. [Image 2] The full page 724 view confirms Figure 4I-4's layout and footnotes, including the pedestrian clearance time span covering the pedestrian change interval (and optionally the buffer interval). Citations: - Figure 4I-4 (p.724) - Section 4I.06 Standard §4 (p.724) - Section 4I.06 Support §6 (p.724) - Section 4I.06 Option §9 (p.725)

**Gold answer:** Walk interval, pedestrian change interval, then buffer interval. The walk interval is labeled 7 seconds minimum and the buffer interval 2 seconds minimum, subject to the stated walk-interval exception.

Options (permitted): - Per the note in Figure 4J-3, an optional steady red clearance interval may be added after Interval 3, and an optional short buffer interval (alternating flashing red while the pedestrian signal heads are displaying a steady UPRAISED HAND) may be added after Interval 5. Support: - "The optimum method of displaying a STOP or SLOW message is to place the STOP/SLOW paddle on a rigid staff that is tall enough that when the end of the staff is resting on the ground, the message is high enough to be seen by approaching or stopped traffic." - "The use of paddles and flags by flaggers is illustrated in Figure 6D-1." Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: The pathway crossing is on a separate pathway alignment outside the roadway, whereas the sidewalk crossing is part of the highway corridor alongside the roadway and crosses the tr… Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=0.906 and Context Precision@6=0.500. The visual-routing decision matched the locked modality requirement. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer. The question required 1 locked visual entity or entities. Exact visual hit=1.00, visual recall=1.00, and visual precision=1.00.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: The principal layout difference is the relationship to a roadway. In panel A, the pathway occupies its own independent right-of-way ("Pathway right-of-way") with no roadway present, whereas in panel B the sidewalk runs immediately alongside and parallel to a roadway within the highway right-of-way ("Highway right-of-way"). Because of this proximity, roadway grade crossing traffic control devices can often warn sidewalk users, but they cannot adequately warn pathway users on a separate alignment. Standards (mandatory): - "Detectable warnings (see Chapter 3C) shall be used at pathway grade crossings where pedestrian travel is permitted and at sidewalk grade crossings and shall extend across the full width of the pathway or sidewalk." (Both panels of the figure show detectable warnings on each approach.) Support (explanatory): - "A pathway is frequently placed in its own right-of-way on an alignment that is independent of any roadway. If a pathway is built parallel to a roadway, it is physically separated from the roadway by an open space or barrier such that the traffic control devices for the roadway grade crossing do not exert an influence over or provide adequate warning to pathway users. A sidewalk runs parallel to a roadway within the highway right-of-way and is close enough to the edge of the roadway’s traveled way that the traffic control devices for the roadway grade crossing can frequently exert an influence over or provide adequate warning to sidewalk users. Pathways are typically used by both pedestrians and bicyclists, whereas sidewalks are typically used only by pedestrians." Visual evidence: [Image 1] shows panel A with a standalone pathway crossing the tracks within its own "Pathway right-of-way," while panel B shows a sidewalk adjacent to a roadway (with yellow centerline) crossing the tracks within a shared "Highway right-of-way," both with detectable warnings on each side of the tracks. [Image 2] shows the full MUTCD page containing Section 8E.01 text and Figure 8E-1 confirming this distinction. Citations: - Section 8E.01 Support §4 (p.1035) - Section 8E.04 Standard §7 (p.1038) - Figure 8E-1 (p.1035) - Page 1076

**Gold answer:** The pathway crossing is on a separate pathway alignment outside the roadway, whereas the sidewalk crossing is part of the highway corridor alongside the roadway and crosses the tracks with the roadway.

## M001

**Question:** A provision appears under a Guidance heading and uses the word 'should.' Is it mandatory, and when may a deviation be appropriate?

**Grade:** C=1.00, Comp=1.00, F=1.00, Cit=1.00, Strict full credit=Yes

**Descriptive mean:** 100.0% (Full credit)

**Evaluation confidence:** 88%

**Rationale:** The response received full credit because the audit accepted the substantive answer, coverage of required elements, grounding, and evidence localization without a deduction. The locked benchmark marks the question as answerable, so the response needed to state and support the required MUTCD answer. Correctness rationale: The response states the locked conclusion, rule, value, or interpretation correctly. Completeness rationale: The audit accepted the response as covering all required answer elements. Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 1D.03, Section 6P.01, Section 1C.01, Section 2D.06, Section 2G.10, Table 2C-6. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: It is recommended practice, not a mandatory Standard. | A deviation may be appropriate when engineering judgment or an engineering study indicates that the deviation is suitable for the specific situation. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=1.000 and Context Precision@6=0.167. The visual-routing decision did not match the locked modality requirement for this question. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: No — a provision under a Guidance heading is a recommendation, not a mandate. Guidance statements describe recommended practice in typical situations, typically using the verb "should," and deviations are allowed when engineering judgment or an engineering study indicates the deviation is appropriate. Only Standard statements (using "shall") are mandatory. Standards (mandatory): - "Guidance—a statement of recommended practice in typical situations, with deviations allowed if engineering judgment or engineering study (see Section 1D.03) indicates the deviation to be appropriate. All Guidance statements are labeled, and the text appears in unbold italic type. The verb 'should' is typically used. The verbs 'shall' and 'may' are not used in Guidance statements. Guidance statements are sometimes modified by Option statements." - By contrast, "Standard—a statement of required, mandatory, or specifically prohibitive practice regarding a traffic control device," for which "the verb 'shall' is typically used." Guidance (recommended): - Typical examples of Guidance "should" statements include: "guide signs should be limited to no more than three lines of destinations," and preferential-lane guide signs on conventional roads, "if used, they should comply with the provisions for guide signs in Chapter 2D and any principles… that engineering judgment finds to be appropriate for the conditions" — both are recommendations subject to engineering judgment, not requirements. Support (context on deviations): - Section 1D.03 explains that the application of engineering study and engineering judgment is a fundamental principle of the use of traffic control devices; even for Standards, in limited site-specific cases a documented engineering study may support a deviation — for Guidance, the threshold is lower (engineering judgment or engineering study suffices). - Section 6P.01 notes that, except for notes clearly classified by headings, the information in typical applications "can generally be regarded as Guidance" — i.e., recommended rather than mandatory. Visual evidence: [Image 4] Table 2C-6 illustrates the normative gradation directly, distinguishing "Optional," "Recommended," and "Required" uses of the Advisory Speed plaque. [Image 5] shows Guidance statements in unbold italic type using "should" (e.g., signal-face positioning recommendations) alongside bold Standard text, illustrating the labeling convention. [Image 7] shows a Guidance statement in italic type using "should" for SCHOOL word-marking letter height. [Image 8] shows a typical-application notes page where Standard, Guidance, and Option headings clearly separate mandatory "shall" statements from recommended "should" statements. Citations: - Section 1C.01 Standard §1 (p.13) - Section 1D.03 Support §2 (p.32) - Section 6P.01 Support §2 (p.857) - Section 2D.06 Guidance §2 (p.209) - Section 2G.10 Guidance §2 (p.420) - Table 2C-6 (p.196) - Page 54 - Page 971 - Page 859

**Gold answer:** It is recommended practice, not a mandatory Standard. A deviation may be appropriate when engineering judgment or an engineering study indicates that the deviation is suitable for the specific situation.

Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 2A.11, Section 2A.21, Section 8B.04, Figure 2A-1, Figure 8B-2, Page 48. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: The strip must be at least 2 inches wide, extend from the sign down the full support to within 2 feet above the near edge of the roadway, match the sign background color except th… Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=0.885 and Context Precision@6=0.500. The visual-routing decision matched the locked modality requirement. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer. The question required 1 locked visual entity or entities. Exact visual hit=1.00, visual recall=1.00, and visual precision=1.00.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: A retroreflective strip on a sign support is a permitted method of enhancing sign conspicuity, but once used it must meet mandatory dimensional and color requirements. The strip must be at least 2 inches wide, run the full length of the support from the sign to within 2 feet above the near edge of the roadway, and match the sign's background color (red for YIELD and DO NOT ENTER signs), with no legend or other information displayed on it. A parallel requirement applies to Crossbuck supports at passive grade crossings, where a white strip of the same dimensions is mandatory on the back of the support. Standards (mandatory): - "If a strip of retroreflective material is used on the sign support, it shall be at least 2 inches in width, it shall be placed for the full length of the support from the sign to within 2 feet above the near edge of the roadway, and its color shall match the background color of the sign, except that the color of the strip for the YIELD and DO NOT ENTER signs shall be red. The retroreflective strip shall not display any legend or other information." - For grade crossings: "A vertical strip of retroreflective white material, not less than 2 inches in width, shall be used on each Crossbuck support at passive grade crossings for the full length of the back of the support from the Crossbuck sign or Number of Tracks plaque to within 2 feet above the near edge of the roadway," with a wrapped strip on a round support of at least 2 inches outside diameter satisfying the requirement. Options (permitted): - Based upon engineering judgment, where improvement of the conspicuity of a standard regulatory, warning, or guide sign is desired, adding a strip of retroreflective material to the sign support is one of the methods that may be used (see Figure 2A-1). Visual evidence: - [Image 1] Figure 2A-1, item E, illustrates a vertical retroreflective strip on a sign support (matching the fluorescent yellow-green background of the pedestrian warning sign) extending down the support and ending "2 ft MAX." above the near edge of the roadway. - [Image 2] shows the same Figure 2A-1 in its page context (Page 48), including item E with the 2-foot maximum dimension above the roadway edge. - [Image 4] shows Section 2A.11 Standard Paragraph 5 stating the 2-inch minimum width, full-length placement, background-matching color (red for YIELD and DO NOT ENTER), and prohibition on any legend, along with the list of permitted conspicuity methods including item L (retroreflective strip on the sign support). - [Image 3] shows Section 2A.21/2A.22, whose Support text notes that information on retroreflective material on sign supports is contained in Section 2A.11. - [Image 5] shows Figure 8B-2, depicting the 2-inch white retroreflective strip on the back of the Crossbuck support (and an optional 2-inch red or white strip on the front) terminating within 2 feet of the roadway edge. Citations: - Section 2A.11 Standard §5 (p.49) - Section 2A.11 Option §1 (p.49) - Section 8B.04 Standard §17 (p.1000) - Section 2A.21 Support §8 (p.62) - Figure 2A-1 (p.48)

**Gold answer:** The strip must be at least 2 inches wide, extend from the sign down the full support to within 2 feet above the near edge of the roadway, match the sign background color except that YIELD and DO NOT ENTER strips are red, and contain no legend or other information.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: For a basic roadside sign in a rural area, the normal minimum mounting height is 5 feet, measured vertically from the bottom of the sign to the elevation of the near edge of the pavement. The normal minimum lateral offset shown in Figure 2A-2 is 12 feet from the edge of the traveled way, or 6 feet from the edge of the shoulder where the shoulder is wider than 6 feet. Where roadside features make these offsets impractical, Section 2A.16 permits a reduced lateral offset of as little as 2 feet on certain roads. Standards (mandatory): - "In rural areas, the minimum height, measured vertically from the bottom of the sign to the elevation of the near edge of the pavement, of signs installed at the side of the road shall be 5 feet (see Figure 2A-2)." - For contrast, in business, commercial, or residential areas where parking, bicyclist, or pedestrian movements are likely, "the minimum height … of signs installed at the side of the road shall be 7 feet (see Figure 2A-2)." Guidance (recommended): - The figure-related guidance notes that "Lateral offset is a minimum of 6 feet measured from the edge of the shoulder, or 12 feet measured from the edge of the traveled way," and refers the reader to Section 2A.16 for lower minimums that may be used where lateral offset space is limited. Options (permitted): - "On conventional, low-volume rural, and special-purpose roads in areas where it is impractical to locate a sign with the lateral offset prescribed by this Section because of roadside features such as terrain or vegetation, a lateral offset of at least 2 feet may be used." Visual evidence: - [Image 1] Figure 2A-2, drawings A and B ("Roadside sign in rural area"), shows a 5 ft MIN. height with a 12 ft MIN. lateral offset from the edge of the traveled way, or a 6 ft MIN. offset from the edge of a shoulder wider than 6 feet. - [Image 2] The full-page view of Figure 2A-2 confirms the same 5 ft MIN. height and 12 ft/6 ft MIN. lateral offsets for rural roadside signs, with a note directing readers to Section 2A.16 for reduced offsets. - [Image 4] Section 2A.15/2A.16 text confirms mounting height provisions and introduces the Lateral Offset standards, including the note that Figure 2A-2 illustrates the mounting height requirements. - [Image 5] Figure 2A-3's note reiterates that lateral offset is a minimum of 6 feet from the edge of the shoulder or 12 feet from the edge of the traveled way, with lower minimums per Section 2A.16. - [Image 3] Figure 2M-3 shows the same rural offset conventions (12 ft MIN. from the traveled way, 6 ft MIN. from a paved shoulder, 5 ft MIN. height) applied to recreational-area sign assemblies, consistent with Figure 2A-2. Citations: - Section 2A.15 Standard §4 (p.58) - Section 2A.15 Standard §5 (p.58) - Section 2A.13 Guidance §6 (p.57) - Section 2A.16 Option §9 (p.60) - Figure 2A-2 (p.51) - Page 92 - Page 93 - Page 100 - Page 566

**Gold answer:** The minimum mounting height is 5 feet. The normal minimum lateral offset is 12 feet from the edge of the traveled way, or 6 feet from the edge of a shoulder wider than 6 feet.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: No, "DLY" is not acceptable. Table 1D-3 explicitly lists DLY (intended word "Delay") as an unacceptable abbreviation because road users commonly misinterpret it as "Daily," and Section 1D.08 mandates that Table 1D-3 abbreviations shall not be used on any traffic control device, including a portable CMS. Standards (mandatory): - "The abbreviations shown in Table 1D-3 shall not be used in connection with traffic control devices because of their potential to be misinterpreted by road users." - "Abbreviations displayed on CMS shall comply with the provisions of Section 1D.08." - "When the word messages shown in Table 1D-2 need to be abbreviated on a portable changeable message sign, the abbreviations shown in Table 1D-2 shall be used. Unless indicated by an asterisk, these abbreviations shall only be used on portable changeable message signs." (The designer must use the Table 1D-2 abbreviation for the intended word rather than an invented one such as DLY.) Guidance (recommended): - If a chosen word is one whose Table 1D-2 abbreviation shows a prompt word, that abbreviation "should not be used on a portable changeable message sign ... unless the prompt word shown in Table 1D-2 either precedes or follows the abbreviation, as applicable." - If more than one allowable abbreviation exists for a word, "the same abbreviation should be used throughout a single jurisdiction." Visual evidence: [Image 1] shows Table 1D-3, "Unacceptable Abbreviations," in which the row "DLY — Delay — Daily" identifies DLY as prohibited due to misinterpretation as "Daily." Citations: - Section 1D.08 Standard §4 (p.34) - Section 2L.05 Standard §6 (p.516) - Section 1D.08 Standard §2 (p.34) - Section 1D.08 Guidance §3 (p.34) - Section 1D.08 Guidance §5 (p.34) - Table 1D-3 (p.37)

**Gold answer:** No. DLY is explicitly prohibited because it can be misinterpreted as DAILY.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: At an all-way stop, an ALL-WAY (R1-3P) supplemental plaque must be mounted below each STOP sign, and the plaque must have a white legend and border on a red background. A 4-WAY plaque (or any numbered-way plaque such as 2-WAY or 3-WAY) may not be substituted — such plaques are expressly prohibited with STOP signs. Standards (mandatory): - "At intersections where all approaches are controlled by STOP signs (see Section 2B.12), an ALL-WAY (R1-3P) supplemental plaque (see Figure 2B-1) shall be mounted below each STOP sign. The ALL-WAY plaque shall have a white legend and border on a red background." - "Supplemental plaques with legends such as 2-WAY, 3-WAY, 4-WAY, or other numbers of ways shall not be used with STOP signs." - "When it is determined that a full stop is always required on an approach to an intersection, a STOP (R1-1) sign (see Figure 2B-1) shall be used." Visual evidence: [Image 1] Figure 2B-1 shows the STOP (R1-1) sign together with the ALL-WAY (R1-3P) plaque, depicted with white legend and border on a red background, along with related plaques (R1-2aP, R1-2bP, R1-2cP, R1-10P). Citations: - Section 2B.04 Standard §4 (p.74) - Section 2B.04 Standard §5 (p.74) - Section 2B.04 Standard §1 (p.74) - Figure 2B-1 (p.74)

**Gold answer:** An ALL-WAY (R1-3P) plaque must be mounted below each STOP sign. It has a white legend and border on a red background. A 4-WAY or other numeric-way plaque may not be substituted.

Completeness rationale: The audit found incomplete coverage even though the automated clause comparison did not isolate a single missing sentence. Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 2B.12, Section 2B.13, Section 2B.16, p.79, Page 120, Page 119. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: The crash warrant may apply with at least 5 correctable crashes in 12 months or 6 in 36 months. | The volume warrant may apply when, for the same 8 hours, major-street entering volume is at least 300 units/hour and minor-street entering volume at least 200 units/hour, with a p… Adjudication note: Correct crash and eight-hour volume thresholds; omits the 70-percent high-speed adjustment. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 2, which produced MRR=0.500. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=0.624 and Context Precision@6=0.333. The visual-routing decision did not match the locked modality requirement for this question. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: The two all-way stop warrants use different metrics: the crash warrant is triggered by crash frequency, while the 8-hour volume warrant is triggered by multimodal entering volumes. For a four-leg intersection, the crash warrant is met by five or more reported crashes in 12 months or six or more in 36 months (of a type correctable by all-way stop control), whereas the 8-hour volume warrant requires at least 300 combined vehicle/bicycle/pedestrian units per hour from the major-street approaches and at least 200 units per hour from the minor-street approaches for each of the same 8 hours of a typical day. Both are Option (permissive) provisions, so meeting either threshold permits — but does not require — all-way stop control based on an engineering study. Options (permitted): - Crash warrant (four-leg trigger): "All-way stop control may be installed at an intersection where an engineering study indicates that: A. For a four-leg intersection, there are five or more reported crashes in a 12-month period or six or more reported crashes in a 36-month period that were of a type susceptible to correction by the installation of all-way stop control." - 8-hour volume warrant: "All-way stop control may be installed at an intersection where an engineering study indicates: A. The combined motor vehicle, bicycle, and pedestrian volume entering the intersection from the major-street approaches is at least 300 units per hour for each of any 8 hours of a typical day; and B. The combined motor vehicle, bicycle, and pedestrian volume entering the intersection from the minor-street approaches is at least 200 units per hour for each of any of the same 8 hours." - Key contrasts: (1) metric — crash counts (5/yr or 6/3 yr, four legs) versus hourly volumes (300 major / 200 minor units per hour); (2) time frame — 12- or 36-month crash history versus any 8 hours of a single typical day; (3) mode coverage — the volume warrant explicitly combines motor vehicles, bicycles, and pedestrians, while the crash warrant counts reported crashes of a correctable type; (4) the volume warrant's two thresholds must both be satisfied concurrently (same 8 hours), whereas the crash warrant offers alternative time windows (12-month OR 36-month). Visual evidence: [Image 3] shows Sections 2B.13 and 2B.16 side by side, including the four-leg crash thresholds (five in 12 months / six in 36 months), the 300/200 units-per-hour criteria, and the provision that the volume thresholds may be reduced to 70 percent when the major-street 85th-percentile speed exceeds 40 mph. [Image 5] shows Section 2B.12, which lists both warrants (Warrant A: Crash Experience and Warrant D: 8-Hour Volume) among the all-way stop control warrants and states that satisfaction of a warrant does not in itself require installation. Citations: - Section 2B.13 Option §1 (p.79) - Section 2B.16 Option §1 (p.79) - Page 120 - Page 119

**Gold answer:** The crash warrant may apply with at least 5 correctable crashes in 12 months or 6 in 36 months. The volume warrant may apply when, for the same 8 hours, major-street entering volume is at least 300 units/hour and minor-street entering volume at least 200 units/hour, with a possible 70-percent adjustment if major-street 85th-percentile speed exceeds 40 mph.

## M007

**Grade:** C=1.00, Comp=1.00, F=1.00, Cit=1.00, Strict full credit=Yes

**Descriptive mean:** 100.0% (Full credit)

**Evaluation confidence:** 88%

**Rationale:** The response received full credit because the audit accepted the substantive answer, coverage of required elements, grounding, and evidence localization without a deduction. The locked benchmark marks the question as answerable, so the response needed to state and support the required MUTCD answer. Correctness rationale: The response states the locked conclusion, rule, value, or interpretation correctly. Completeness rationale: The audit accepted the response as covering all required answer elements. Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 2A.22, Table 2A-5, p.62, p.63. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: For white on red, the table gives white at least 35, red at least 7 cd/lx/m², and at least a 3:1 white-to-red contrast ratio. | The agency complies by having and using a method designed to maintain the minimums, even if some individual signs fall below them at a particular moment. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=0.975 and Context Precision@6=0.833. The visual-routing decision matched the locked modality requirement. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer. The question required 1 locked visual entity or entities. Exact visual hit=1.00, visual recall=1.00, and visual precision=1.00.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: No. Compliance with the retroreflectivity Standard is based on having and using an assessment or management method — not on the instantaneous condition of every individual sign. The MUTCD explicitly explains that an agency using such a method remains in compliance even if some individual signs are temporarily below the Table 2A-5 minimums, although signs identified as deficient should be replaced. For white-on-red signs such as STOP signs, Table 2A-5 requires a minimum maintained retroreflectivity of W ≥ 35 and R ≥ 7 (cd/lx/m²), with a minimum white-to-red contrast ratio of at least 3:1. Standards (mandatory): - "Public agencies or officials having jurisdiction shall use an assessment or management method that is designed to maintain sign retroreflectivity at or above the minimum levels in Table 2A-5." - Table 2A-5 sets the minimum maintained levels for White on Red at W ≥ 35; R ≥ 7 (in cd/lx/m² at an observation angle of 0.2° and an entrance angle of −4.0°), with footnote 4 requiring a "Minimum sign contrast ratio ≥ 3:1 (white retroreflectivity ÷ red retroreflectivity)." Guidance (recommended): - Except for signs identified in Paragraph 5, one or more of the methods described in "Maintaining Traffic Sign Retroreflectivity" (FHWA-SA-07-020, Revised 2013), or a method developed based on an engineering study, should be used to maintain sign retroreflectivity at or above the Table 2A-5 minimums; "Signs that are identified through the agency’s method as being below the minimum levels should be replaced." Options (permitted): - Highway agencies may exclude certain signs from the retroreflectivity maintenance guidelines: Parking, Standing, and Stopping (R7 and R8 series) signs; Walking/Hitchhiking/Crossing (R9 series, R10-1 through R10-4b) signs; Acknowledgment signs; and bikeway signs intended for exclusive use by bicyclists or pedestrians. (STOP signs are not among these exclusions.) Support (informational): - "Compliance with the Standard in Paragraph 2 of this Section is achieved by having a method in place and using the method to maintain the minimum levels established in Table 2A-5. Provided that an assessment or management method is being used, an agency or official having jurisdiction would be in compliance with the Standard in Paragraph 2 of this Section even if there are some individual signs that do not meet the minimum retroreflectivity levels at a particular point in time." Visual evidence: [Image 1] shows Table 2A-5, "Minimum Maintained Retroreflectivity Levels," which lists the White on Red row (W ≥ 35; R ≥ 7) with the footnote requiring a minimum white-to-red contrast ratio of 3:1, applicable to STOP-type signs. Citations: - Section 2A.22 Standard §2 (p.62) - Section 2A.22 Support §3 (p.62) - Section 2A.22 Guidance §4 (p.62) - Section 2A.22 Option §5 (p.63) - Table 2A-5 (p.62)

**Gold answer:** Not automatically. The agency complies by having and using a method designed to maintain the minimums, even if some individual signs fall below them at a particular moment. For white on red, the table gives white at least 35, red at least 7 cd/lx/m², and at least a 3:1 white-to-red contrast ratio.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: No. Under the MUTCD's general dimensioning rule, the reduced "Minimum" sign sizes may only be used on low-speed roadways with operating speeds of 30 mph or less; a multi-lane roadway posted at 35 mph does not qualify, so the smaller (minimum/single-lane-minimum) sizes may not be used and the full prescribed (Conventional Road) dimensions apply. However, the specific numeric dimensions for the STOP (R1-1) sign itself (Table 2B-1) are not included in the evidence provided, so I cannot state the exact STOP sign size — only that it must be no smaller than the prescribed Conventional Road size for that context. Standards (mandatory): - "The sign dimensions prescribed in the sign size tables in the various Parts and Chapters in this Manual and in the 'Standard Highway Signs' publication (see Section 1A.05) shall be used unless engineering judgment determines that other sizes are appropriate in accordance with the following." - "The sizes shown in the Minimum columns that are smaller than the sizes shown in the Conventional Road columns in the various sign size tables in this Manual shall only be used on low-speed roadways, alleys, site roadways open to public travel, and on low-volume rural roads with operating speeds of 30 mph or less; and only where the reduced legend size would be adequate for the regulation or warning or where physical conditions preclude the use of larger sizes." — A 35-mph multi-lane roadway exceeds this 30-mph threshold, so reduced sizes are not permitted. - Note 3 to the school-area sign size table states that minimum sign sizes for multi-lane conventional roads shall be as shown in the Conventional Road column (i.e., the smaller Minimum column values cannot be used on multi-lane roads). Guidance (recommended): - For school-area signs, "The sizes in the Oversized column should be used on roadways that have four or more lanes with posted speed limits of 40 mph or higher" — showing that larger, not smaller, sizes are the recommended direction on higher-speed multi-lane roads. - For guide-sign lettering, Table 2D-2 distinguishes Single-Lane and Multi-Lane columns by speed range; on multi-lane roads in the 30–40 mph range, the larger Multi-Lane column values apply rather than the Single-Lane values. Visual evidence: - [Image 1] Table 2D-2 (Sheet 1, Post-Mounted Signs) shows separate Single-Lane and Multi-Lane letter/numeral size columns, with the Multi-Lane 30–40 mph column generally requiring larger sizes than the Single-Lane column for the same speed range. - [Image 2] Table 7B-1 lists Conventional Road, Minimum, and Oversized sizes for school-area regulatory signs, and its Note 3 states that minimum sign sizes for multi-lane conventional roads shall be as shown in the Conventional Road column. - [Image 3] Shows the Section 2A.07 Standard text restricting Minimum-column sizes to low-speed roadways with operating speeds of 30 mph or less. Insufficient evidence note: The regulatory sign size table containing the STOP (R1-1) sign dimensions (e.g., Table 2B-1) is not among the evidence supplied, so the exact required STOP sign dimension (in inches) for a multi-lane 35-mph roadway cannot be quoted from this evidence. Citations: - Section 2A.07 Standard §2 (p.45) - Section 7B.01 Guidance §3 (p.972) - Section 2D.07 Guidance §5 (p.210) - Table 2D-2 (p.210) - Table 7B-1 (p.971)

**Gold answer:** For regulatory signs other than STOP, the single-lane size may be used at 35 mph or less. The STOP-sign exception remains: the multi-lane STOP size is 36 x 36 inches.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: For a 55-mph posted or 85th-percentile speed, Table 2C-3 lists 495 feet for the potential stop condition (Condition B, deceleration to 0 mph — the column used for Stop Ahead, Yield Ahead, and Signal Ahead signs). This distance is a guideline, not an inflexible mandate: Table 2C-3 is presented as "Guidelines for Advance Placement of Warning Signs" and the distances should be applied with engineering judgment and adjusted as needed (e.g., a minimum of 100 feet should be added for signs with less than 6-inch legend or more than four words). However, in certain contexts (e.g., at passive grade crossings) the MUTCD does mandate locating the Stop Ahead/Yield Ahead sign in accordance with Table 2C-3. Standards (mandatory): - "The Stop Ahead (W3-1), Yield Ahead (W3-2), and Signal Ahead (W3-3) Advance Traffic Control signs (see Figure 2C-8) shall be installed on an approach to a primary traffic control device that is not visible for a sufficient distance to permit the road user to respond to the device (see Table 2C-3)." - At passive grade crossings with a YIELD or STOP sign: "The Yield Ahead or Stop Ahead sign shall be located in accordance with Table 2C-3. The minimum distance between the signs shall be in accordance with Section 2C.04 and Table 2C-3." Guidance (recommended): - Table 2C-3 shows, for 55 mph, 495 ft under the Condition B "0 mph" column (the potential stop condition); the table is titled "Guidelines for Advance Placement of Warning Signs," indicating recommended rather than mandatory distances. - "For Conditions A and B, warning signs with less than 6-inch legend or more than four words, a minimum of 100 feet should be added to the advance placement distance to provide adequate legibility of the warning sign." - "The effectiveness of the placement of warning signs should be periodically evaluated under both day and night conditions." Visual evidence: - [Image 1] Table 2C-3 shows the 55 mph row with 495 ft in the "0³" column, and footnote 3 identifies this column as the potential stop condition (typical signs: Stop Ahead, Yield Ahead, Signal Ahead, and Intersection Warning), based on 2018 AASHTO stopping sight distance with a 2.5-second PRT and deceleration of 11.2 feet/second². - [Image 2] Page 152 shows Section 2C.04, whose Guidance states that the distances in Table 2C-3 "should be applied with engineering judgment" and that the distances "can be adjusted for roadway features, other signing, and to improve visibility," confirming the table is not an inflexible mandate. - [Image 3] Page 189 (Figure 2C-13, Sheet 5) illustrates use of the table with the annotation "See Table 2C-3 to determine the advance placement distance" for a lane-merge warning sign. - [Image 4] Page 185 (Figure 2C-13, Sheet 1) similarly annotates "See Table 2C-3 to determine the advance placement distance" for a W4-2R lane ends sign. - [Image 5] Page 156 (Figure 2C-2) notes "See Table 2C-3 for advance placement distance guidelines," reinforcing the guideline character of the table. Citations: - Table 2C-3 (p.152) - Section 2C.04 Guidance §5 (p.152) - Section 2C.04 Guidance §804 (p.153) - Section 2C.35 Standard §1 (p.177) - Section 8B.06 Standard §3 (p.1001)

**Gold answer:** The table shows 495 feet. It is a guideline to be applied with engineering judgment and may be adjusted for roadway features, other signing, and visibility; it is not an inflexible universal mandate.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: On a roadway with center line markings, a no-passing zone must be marked at a horizontal or vertical curve whenever the passing sight distance falls below the minimum in Table 3B-1 — which for a 55-mph 85th-percentile speed or speed limit is 900 feet. If the next no-passing zone begins less than 400 feet downstream, the no-passing markings should be connected so the two zones become continuous. Standards (mandatory): - "On roadways with center line markings, no-passing zone markings shall be used at horizontal or vertical curves where the passing sight distance is less than the minimum shown in Table 3B-1 for the 85th-percentile speed or the speed limit." - "On two-way, two-lane or three-lane roadways where center line markings are installed, no-passing zones shall be established at vertical and horizontal curves and other locations where an engineering study indicates that passing must be prohibited because of inadequate sight distances or other special conditions." - "No-passing zones shall be marked by either the one-direction no-passing zone pavement markings or the two-direction no-passing zone pavement markings described in Section 3B.01 and shown in Figures 3B-1 and 3B-3." Guidance (recommended): - "Where the distance between successive no-passing zones is less than 400 feet, no-passing zone markings should connect the zones." Visual evidence: - [Image 1] Table 3B-1 shows that for a 55-mph 85th-percentile speed or speed limit, the minimum passing sight distance is 900 feet. - [Image 2] Figure 3B-4 illustrates how the no-passing zone limits are located: the zone begins at point "a," where sight distance (measured between points 3.5 feet above the pavement) first becomes less than the Table 3B-1 minimum, and ends at point "b," where sight distance again exceeds the minimum, for both vertical and horizontal curves. - [Image 3] shows the full page containing Figure 3B-4 with the profile and plan views of no-passing zones at curves. - [Image 4] shows the full page containing Section 3B.03 provisions and Table 3B-1. Citations: - Section 3B.03 Standard §4 (p.543) - Section 3B.03 Standard §3 (p.543) - Section 3B.03 Standard §1 (p.542) - Section 3B.03 Guidance §7 (p.543) - Table 3B-1 (p.543) - Figure 3B-4 (p.544) - Page 585 - Page 584

**Gold answer:** The no-passing zone must be marked when available passing sight distance is less than 900 feet. If successive no-passing zones are separated by less than 400 feet, the markings should connect them.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: For Condition A with 2 or more major-street lanes and 1 minor-street lane, Table 4C-1 (100% columns) requires 600 vehicles per hour on the major street (total of both approaches) together with 150 vehicles per hour on the more critical minor-street approach (one direction only), existing for each of any 8 hours of an average day. Meeting these volumes does not compel signal installation: the provision is Guidance stating only that the need for a signal "should be considered" based on an engineering study. Standards (mandatory): - "These major-street and minor-street volumes shall be for the same 8 hours for each condition; however, the 8 hours that are selected for the Condition A analysis shall not be required to be the same 8 hours that are selected for the Condition B analysis." - "These major-street and minor-street volumes shall be for the same 8 hours for each condition; however, the 8 hours satisfied in Condition A shall not be required to be the same 8 hours satisfied in Condition B." Guidance (recommended): - "The need for a traffic control signal should be considered if an engineering study finds that one of the following conditions exist for each of any 8 hours of an average day: A. The vehicles per hour given in both of the 100 percent columns of Condition A in Table 4C-1 exist on the major street and the more critical minor-street approach, respectively, to the intersection; or B. The vehicles per hour given in both of the 100 percent columns of Condition B in Table 4C-1 exist..." - For the combination of Conditions A and B (after adequate trial of other remedial measures), the 80 percent columns apply: for this lane configuration, 480 vph on the major street and 120 vph on the minor-street approach for Condition A, in combination with the Condition B 80 percent values, for each of any 8 hours of an average day. Options (permitted): - "Any four consecutive 15-minute periods may be considered as 1 hour if the separate 1-hour periods used in the warrant analysis do not overlap each other and both the major-street volume and the minor-street volume are for the same specific 1-hour periods." - Per the table footnotes, the 70 percent columns (420 vph major / 105 vph minor for this configuration) may be used when the major-street speed exceeds 40 mph or in an isolated community with a population of less than 10,000, and the 56 percent columns may be used for the combination of Conditions A and B under the same speed/population conditions after adequate trial of other remedial measures. Visual evidence: [Image 1] Table 4C-1, Condition A, row "2 or more / 1" shows major-street volumes of 600 (100%), 480 (80%), 420 (70%), and 336 (56%) and minor-street volumes of 150, 120, 105, and 84, with footnotes explaining when the reduced-percentage columns apply. Citations: - Section 4C.02 Guidance §4 (p.653) - Section 4C.02 Standard §5 (p.653) - Section 4C.02 Guidance §9 (p.654) - Section 4C.02 Standard §10 (p.654) - Section 4C.01 Option §15 (p.652) - Table 4C-1 (p.653)

**Gold answer:** For each of the same 8 selected hours, 600 vph must exist on the major street (both approaches total) and 150 vph on the critical minor approach (one direction) at the 100-percent thresholds. Meeting the warrant does not compel installation; an engineering study must determine whether a signal is justified.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: Under the one-year Crash Experience warrant (Warrant 7), a four-leg intersection meets the crash-history condition if reported angle and pedestrian crashes in a 1-year period equal or exceed 5 (all severities) or 3 (fatal-and-injury) per Table 4C-2; where the major street exceeds 40 mph or the community has under 10,000 population, Table 4C-4 thresholds may be used instead (4 or 10 all-severity, and 3 or 6 fatal-and-injury, depending on the number of through lanes). In addition to the crash-history condition, two further criteria must also be met: (A) an adequate trial of alternatives has failed to reduce crash frequency, and (C) traffic volumes reach 80 percent of the Warrant 1 Condition A or Condition B values (or pedestrian volume reaches 80 percent of the Pedestrian Volume warrant) for each of any 8 hours of an average day. Guidance (recommended): - "The need for a traffic control signal should be considered if an engineering study finds that all of the following criteria are met: A. Adequate trial of alternatives with satisfactory observance and enforcement has failed to reduce the crash frequency; and B. At least one of the following conditions applies to the reported crash history... 1. The number of reported angle crashes and pedestrian crashes within a 1-year period equals or exceeds the threshold number in Table 4C-2 for total angle crashes and pedestrian crashes (all severities); or 2. The number of reported fatal-and-injury angle crashes and pedestrian crashes within a 1-year period equals or exceeds the threshold number in Table 4C-2 for total fatal-and-injury angle crashes and pedestrian crashes... and C. For each of any 8 hours of an average day, the ve[hicles per hour given in both of the 80 percent columns of Condition A or Condition B in Table 4C-1 exist, or the pedestrian volume is not less than 80 percent of the Pedestrian Volume warrant requirements]." Options (permitted): - "If the posted or statutory speed limit or the 85th-percentile speed on the major street exceeds 40 mph, or if the intersection lies within the built-up area of an isolated community having a population of less than 10,000: A. The traffic volumes in the 56 percent columns in Table 4C-1 may be used in place of the 80 percent columns. B. Tables 4C-4 and 4C-5 may be used in place of Tables 4C-2 and 4C-3, respectively." - Agencies may use documented agency-specific crash frequency values (from calibrated or agency-developed HSM safety performance functions) in place of the values in Tables 4C-2 through 4C-5. Visual evidence: - [Image 1] Table 4C-2 shows that for four-leg intersections the one-year thresholds are 5 total angle and pedestrian crashes (all severities) and 3 fatal-and-injury angle and pedestrian crashes for all lane configurations. - [Image 2] Table 4C-4 (community less than 10,000 population or above 40 mph on the major street) shows four-leg one-year thresholds of 4 or 10 all-severity crashes and 3 or 6 fatal-and-injury crashes, depending on the number of through lanes on the major and minor streets. - [Image 3] Page 702 shows Section 4C.08 in full, including the Guidance criteria A, B, and C and Table 4C-2 with the four-leg thresholds of 5 and 3. Citations: - Section 4C.08 Guidance §2 (p.661) - Section 4C.08 Option §5 (p.662) - Section 4C.08 Option §6 (p.663) - Section 4C.08 Support §1 (p.661) - Section 4C.08 Support §7 (p.663) - Table 4C-2 (p.661) - Table 4C-4 (p.662) - Page 702

**Gold answer:** The thresholds are at least 5 all-severity angle/pedestrian crashes or at least 3 fatal-and-injury angle/pedestrian crashes. In addition, an adequate trial of alternatives must have failed, and the applicable 80-percent vehicular-volume or pedestrian-volume condition must be met for the required hours.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: For an 85th-percentile approach speed of 45 mph, Table 4D-2 specifies a minimum sight distance of 460 feet, and the two primary signal faces should be continuously visible from at least that distance in advance of (measured to) the stop line. If approaching traffic does not have a continuous view of at least two signal faces for that distance, a warning sign (per Section 2C.35) should be installed to warn approaching traffic of the signal. In addition, because the speed is 45 mph or higher, backplates should be used on all signal faces facing the approach. Guidance (recommended): - "The two primary signal faces required as a minimum for each approach should be continuously visible to traffic approaching the traffic control signal, from a point at least the minimum sight distance provided in Table 4D-2 in advance of and measured to the stop line. This range of continuous visibility should be provided unless precluded by a physical obstruction or unless another signalized location is within this range." - "If approaching traffic does not have a continuous view of at least two signal faces for at least the minimum sight distance shown in Table 4D-2, a sign (see Section 2C.35) should be installed to warn approaching traffic of the traffic control signal." - "If the posted or statutory speed limit or the 85th-percentile speed on an approach to a signalized location is 45 mph or higher, signal backplates should be used on all of the signal faces that face the approach." - For approaches with speeds of 45 mph or higher, signal faces should be provided per Table 4D-1 and Figure 4D-1, with primary faces on the far side of the intersection, overhead faces over through and mandatory turn lanes, supplemental faces considered for visibility behind large vehicles, and backplates on all signal faces. Visual evidence: [Image 1] Table 4D-2 shows that a 45 mph 85th-percentile speed corresponds to a minimum sight distance of 460 feet, with a note that distances are derived from stopping sight distance plus an assumed queue length for shorter cycle lengths (60 to 75 seconds). [Image 2] shows Section 4D.06 in full, including the Guidance that the two primary signal faces should be continuously visible for the Table 4D-2 distance and that a warning sign should be installed if that view is unavailable. [Image 3] shows Figure 4D-2 illustrating the lateral and longitudinal placement areas for primary signal faces. [Image 4] shows Figure 4D-1 and Table 4D-1 with the recommended signal face configurations for approaches with speeds of 45 mph or higher. [Image 5] shows the continuation of Section 4D.06, including the Guidance that backplates should be used on all signal faces facing approaches with speeds of 45 mph or higher. Citations: - Section 4D.06 Guidance §5 (p.670) - Section 4D.06 Guidance §6 (p.670) - Section 4D.06 Guidance §17 (p.671) - Section 4D.05 Guidance §7 (p.668) - Table 4D-2 (p.670) - Page 711 - Page 712 - Page 713 - Page 710

**Gold answer:** They should be continuously visible from at least 460 feet in advance of the stop line. If at least two faces are not continuously visible for that distance, a warning sign should be installed for approaching traffic.

Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 4I.06, Figure 4I-4, Page 765, Page 764, Page 767, Page 771. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: The order is WALK, flashing DONT WALK pedestrian change interval, then steady DONT WALK buffer interval. | The buffer interval is at least 2 seconds before conflicting traffic is released, and clearance is normally evaluated using 3.5 feet per second. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=0.927 and Context Precision@6=0.667. The visual-routing decision matched the locked modality requirement. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer. The question required 1 locked visual entity or entities. Exact visual hit=1.00, visual recall=1.00, and visual precision=1.00.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: The pedestrian intervals occur in a fixed order: a Walk Interval (steady WALKING PERSON, 7 seconds minimum), followed immediately by the Pedestrian Change Interval (flashing UPRAISED HAND, optionally with countdown), followed by a Buffer Interval (steady UPRAISED HAND) before conflicting traffic is released. The buffer interval must be at least 2 seconds and must always be provided and displayed. Pedestrian clearance time is normally calculated using a walking speed of 3.5 feet per second, measured from the end of the WALK indication to at least the far side of the traveled way or a median of sufficient width. Standards (mandatory): - Per the figure notes accompanying Figure 4I-4: "The Buffer Interval, which shall always be provided and displayed, may be used to help satisfy the calculated pedestrian clearance time, or may begin after the calculated pedestrian clearance time has ended." - As shown on the page containing Section 4I.06's standard text [Image 3], "a buffer interval consisting of a steady UPRAISED HAND (symbolizing DONT WALK) signal indication shall be displayed for at least 2 seconds prior to the release of any conflicting vehicular movement," and "the buffer interval shall not begin later than the beginning of the red clearance interval, if used." Guidance (recommended): - "The pedestrian clearance time should be sufficient to allow a pedestrian crossing in the crosswalk who left the curb or edge of pavement at the end of the WALKING PERSON (symbolizing WALK) signal indication to travel at a walking speed of 3.5 feet per second to at least the far side of the traveled way or to a median of sufficient width for pedestrians to wait." - "Where pedestrians who walk slower than 3.5 feet per second, or pedestrians who use wheelchairs, routinely use the crosswalk, a walking speed of less than 3.5 feet per second should be considered in determining the pedestrian clearance time." Options (permitted): - "The additional time provided by an extended push button press to satisfy pedestrian clearance time needs may be added to either the walk interval or the pedestrian change interval." - Per the figure notes: the countdown display is optional for Pedestrian Change Intervals of 7 seconds or less, and the Buffer Interval may be used to help satisfy the calculated pedestrian clearance time or may begin after it has ended. Visual evidence: [Image 1] Figure 4I-4 shows the interval sequence — steady WALK (Walk Interval, "7 seconds MIN."), flashing UPRAISED HAND with countdown (Pedestrian Change Interval spanning the calculated pedestrian clearance time), then steady UPRAISED HAND (Buffer Interval, "2 seconds MIN.") — plus five possible relationships between the buffer interval and the vehicular yellow/red clearance intervals. [Image 2] Page 765 reproduces Figure 4I-4 in context along with the Guidance on 3.5 feet per second walking speed and the Options for 4 feet per second with extended push button press. [Image 3] Page 764 contains the Standard requiring the buffer interval (steady UPRAISED HAND) for at least 2 seconds prior to release of conflicting traffic and requiring that the change interval plus buffer not be less than the calculated pedestrian clearance time. [Image 4] Page 767 provides related guidance on WALK timing where leading pedestrian intervals are used, supporting the interval-order context. [Image 5] Page 771 addresses pedestrian hybrid beacon operation and is not directly relevant to the interval order or timing values asked here. Citations: - Section 4I.06 Support §6 (p.724) - Section 4I.06 Option §9 (p.725) - Section 4I.06 Guidance §7 (p.724) - Section 4I.06 Guidance §10 (p.725) - Figure 4I-4 (p.724) - Page 764 - Page 765

**Gold answer:** The order is WALK, flashing DONT WALK pedestrian change interval, then steady DONT WALK buffer interval. The buffer interval is at least 2 seconds before conflicting traffic is released, and clearance is normally evaluated using 3.5 feet per second.

Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: Dark, flashing yellow, steady yellow, both steady red, alternating flashing red, then dark. | The both-steady-red stage corresponds to the pedestrian walk interval. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 2, which produced MRR=0.500. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=0.733 and Context Precision@6=0.500. The visual-routing decision matched the locked modality requirement. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer. The question required 1 locked visual entity or entities. Exact visual hit=1.00, visual recall=1.00, and visual precision=1.00.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: After a pedestrian actuation, a pedestrian hybrid beacon proceeds through the following sequence: (1) dark until activated, (2) flashing circular yellow upon activation, (3) steady circular yellow, (4) both steady circular red indications during the pedestrian walk interval, (5) alternating flashing circular red during the pedestrian change interval, and (6) dark again until the next actuation. The pedestrian walk interval corresponds to Stage 4 — the display of both steady CIRCULAR RED signal indications. Standards (mandatory): - "Following an actuation by a pedestrian, a pedestrian hybrid beacon face shall display a flashing CIRCULAR yellow signal indication, followed by a steady CIRCULAR yellow signal indication, followed by both steady CIRCULAR RED signal indications during the pedestrian walk interval, followed by alternating flashing CIRCULAR RED signal indications during the pedestrian change interval (see Figure 4J-3). Upon ... termination of the pedestrian change interval, the pedestrian hybrid beacon faces shall revert to a dark (not illuminated) condition." - "A pedestrian hybrid beacon face shall consist of three signal sections, with a CIRCULAR YELLOW signal indication centered below two horizontally-aligned CIRCULAR RED signal indications (see Figure 4J-3)." Options (permitted): - Per the note in Figure 4J-3, "An optional steady red clearance interval may be added after Interval 3, and an optional short buffer interval (alternating flashing red while the pedestrian signal heads are displaying a steady UPRAISED HAND) may be added after Interval 5." Visual evidence: [Image 1] shows Figure 4J-3 with the six-stage sequence — (1) Dark Until Activated, (2) Flashing Yellow Upon Activation, (3) Steady Yellow, (4) Steady Red During Pedestrian Walk Interval, (5) Alternating Flashing Red During Pedestrian Change Interval, (6) Dark Again Until Activated — with the walk interval explicitly labeled as Stage 4 (both red lenses steady, "SR"). [Image 2] shows the same Figure 4J-3 in the full-page context of page 729 along with the legend (SY steady yellow, FY flashing yellow, SR steady red, FR flashing red). Citations: - Section 4J.03 Standard §2 (p.730) - Section 4J.02 Standard §2 (p.728) - Figure 4J-3 (p.729)

**Gold answer:** Dark, flashing yellow, steady yellow, both steady red, alternating flashing red, then dark. The both-steady-red stage corresponds to the pedestrian walk interval.
