**Grade:** C=1.00, Comp=1.00, F=1.00, Cit=1.00, Strict full credit=Yes

**Descriptive mean:** 100.0% (Full credit)

**Evaluation confidence:** 88%

**Rationale:** The response received full credit because the audit accepted the substantive answer, coverage of required elements, grounding, and evidence localization without a deduction. The locked benchmark marks the question as answerable, so the response needed to state and support the required MUTCD answer. Correctness rationale: The response states the locked conclusion, rule, value, or interpretation correctly. Completeness rationale: The audit accepted the response as covering all required answer elements. Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 1C.03, Section 5A.03, Section 5A.02, Table 5A-1, p.760, p.31. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: Level 3 performs all aspects within its ODD, subject to a human fallback request. | An ADS performs the entire dynamic driving task on a sustained basis and the term applies to Levels 3-5. | Level 2 performs steering and acceleration/deceleration but still expects the human to perform all remaining aspects of the task, so it is ADAS rather than ADS. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=1.000 and Context Precision@6=0.167. The visual-routing decision matched the locked modality requirement. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer. The question required 1 locked visual entity or entities. Exact visual hit=1.00, visual recall=1.00, and visual precision=1.00.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: The MUTCD defines an Automated Driving System (ADS) as hardware and software capable of performing the *entire* Dynamic Driving Task (DDT) on a sustained basis, a term used specifically for Level 3, 4, or 5 systems. At Level 2, the system executes only steering and acceleration/deceleration while "the human driver performs all remaining aspects of the Dynamic Driving Task," so it does not perform the entire DDT and Table 5A-1 categorizes it as ADAS. At Level 3, the system provides "sustained performance by an ADS of all aspects of the Dynamic Driving Task within a given ODD," meeting the full-DDT definition, so Table 5A-1 places it in the Automated Driving System (ADS) category. Standards (mandatory): - Per the acronym list, when used in this Manual "ADS" shall mean "Automated Driving System" (Section 1C.03). Support (informational, from the definitions and table): - "Automated Driving System (ADS) – The hardware and software that are collectively capable of performing the entire Dynamic Driving Task (DDT) on a sustained basis, regardless of whether it is limited to a specific Operational Design Domain (ODD); this term is used specifically to describe a Level 3, 4, or 5 driving automation system." - "Automation Levels – The levels of automation that are described in Table 5A-1." - Table 5A-1, Level 2: "The driving mode specific execution by one or more sustained driver assistance systems of both steering and acceleration/deceleration … with the expectation that the human driver performs all remaining aspects of the Dynamic Driving Task" — Automation Category: Advanced Driver Assistance Systems (ADAS). - Table 5A-1, Level 3: "The driving mode specific sustained performance by an ADS of all aspects of the Dynamic Driving Task within a given ODD with the expectation that the human driver will respond appropriately to a request to intervene" — Automation Category: Automated Driving System (ADS). Visual evidence: [Image 1] shows Table 5A-1, where the "Automation Category" column groups Levels 1–2 under "Advanced Driver Assistance Systems (ADAS)" and Levels 3–5 under "Automated Driving System (ADS)," with all of Levels 1–5 sharing the "Driving Automation System" automation type. [Image 2] shows the Section 5A.03 definition of ADS (Item B), which restricts the term to systems performing the entire DDT and to Levels 3, 4, or 5. [Image 4] shows the Chapter 5A general text, including the Section 5A.03 lead-in stating the definitions come from SAE J3016 and the ADAS definition (Item A) describing systems that merely aid the driver with one or more driving tasks. Citations: - Section 5A.03 Support §1 (p.760) - Table 5A-1 (p.760) - Section 1C.03 Standard §1 (p.31) - Section 5A.02 Support §1 (p.759) - Page 801 - Page 800

**Gold answer:** An ADS performs the entire dynamic driving task on a sustained basis and the term applies to Levels 3-5. Level 2 performs steering and acceleration/deceleration but still expects the human to perform all remaining aspects of the task, so it is ADAS rather than ADS. Level 3 performs all aspects within its ODD, subject to a human fallback request.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: A TTC zone is typically divided into four areas that road users encounter in this order: (1) the advance warning area, (2) the transition area, (3) the activity area, and (4) the termination area. The activity area — where the work takes place — is comprised of three components: the work space, the traffic space, and the buffer space. All of this is descriptive (Support) material rather than mandatory, recommended, or permitted practice. Standards (mandatory): (none — the cited provisions are Support/informational only) Visual evidence: [Image 1] Figure 6B-1 depicts the four areas in travel order (advance warning area, transition area, activity area, termination area) and labels the activity area's components — the work space set aside for workers and equipment, the traffic space allowing traffic to pass through, and the lateral and longitudinal buffer spaces providing protection. [Image 2] shows the Section 6B.06 text defining the activity area as comprised of the work space, traffic space, and buffer space. [Image 3] shows Section 6B.03, which lists the four areas of a TTC zone and refers to Figure 6B-1. [Image 4] and [Image 5] show related taper and worker-safety text not directly needed for this question. Citations: - Section 6B.03 Support §1 (p.771) - Section 6B.06 Support §1 (p.773) - Section 6B.06 Support §5 (p.773) - Figure 6B-1 (p.772) - Page 814 - Page 812

**Gold answer:** Advance warning, transition, activity, and termination. The activity area consists of the work space, traffic space, and buffer space.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: For a speed of 35 mph (40 mph or less), Table 6B-4 gives L = WS²/60 = (12 × 35²)/60 = 14,700/60 = **245 feet**. From Table 6B-3, the minimum merging taper is at least L = **245 ft**, the minimum shifting taper is at least 0.5 L ≈ **123 ft**, and the minimum shoulder taper is at least 0.33 L ≈ **81 ft**. Guidance (recommended): - "The appropriate taper length (L) should be determined using the criteria shown in Tables 6B-3 and 6B-4." - "A shifting taper should have a length of approximately ½ L (see Tables 6B-3 and 6B-4)." - "If used, shoulder tapers should have a length of approximately ¹⁄3 L (see Tables 6B-3 and 6B-4). If a shoulder is used as a travel lane, either through practice or during a TTC activity, a normal merging or shifting taper should be used." - Consistent with pavement-marking taper practice: "For roadways where the speed limit is less than 45 mph, the formula L = WS²/60 should be used to compute the taper length," where W is the width of offset in feet and S is the speed in mph. Options (permitted): - A lateral buffer space may be used to separate the traffic space from the work space, or between two travel lanes (especially opposing flows); Table 6B-4 provides the formulas for calculating taper length (L). Visual evidence: - [Image 1] Table 6B-4 shows that for speeds of "40 mph or less," L = WS²/60, with L = taper length in feet, W = width of offset in feet, and S = posted speed limit, off-peak 85th-percentile speed, or anticipated operating speed in mph. - [Image 2] Table 6B-3 lists minimum taper lengths: Merging Taper "at least L," Shifting Taper "at least 0.5 L," and Shoulder Taper "at least 0.33 L," with a note to use Table 6B-4 to calculate L. - [Image 3] Figure 6B-2 (p.774) illustrates the merging taper (L), shifting tapers (½ L), and shoulder taper (⅓ L) and refers to Table 6B-4 for formulas for calculating L. - [Image 4] Figure 6P-39 (TA-39) applies taper length L and ½ L segments in a median crossover layout, referring to Table 6B-4 for the formulas. - [Image 5] Figure 6P-38 (TA-38) shows L, ½ L, and shoulder taper dimensions in an interior lane closure, referring to Table 6B-4 for calculating L. - [Image 6] Figure 6P-42 (TA-42) shows taper lengths L and shoulder tapers near an exit ramp, again referring to Table 6B-4 for calculating L. Citations: - Section 6B.08 Guidance §4 (p.775) - Section 6B.08 Guidance §8 (p.776) - Section 6B.08 Guidance §10 (p.776) - Section 6B.08 Support §2 (p.775) - Section 6B.06 Option §14 (p.775) - Section 3B.13 Guidance §2 (p.568) - Table 6B-4 (p.775) - Table 6B-3 (p.775) - Page 815 - Page 978 - Page 976 - Page 984

**Gold answer:** For 40 mph or less, L = W*S²/60 = 12*35²/60 = 245 feet. Minimum merging taper: 245 feet; shifting taper: 122.5 feet; shoulder taper: about 80.9 feet (0.33L, commonly rounded appropriately in design).

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: The STOP/SLOW paddle is the primary and preferred hand-signaling device because it gives road users more positive guidance than red flags. To stop traffic, the flagger must face road users and hold the STOP paddle face toward them in a stationary position with the arm extended horizontally, while the free arm is raised with the palm above shoulder level toward approaching traffic. Standards (mandatory): - "To stop road users, the flagger shall face road users and aim the STOP paddle face toward road users in a stationary position with the arm extended horizontally away from the body. The free arm shall be held with the palm of the hand above shoulder level toward approaching traffic." Guidance (recommended): - "The STOP/SLOW paddle (see Figure 6D-1 and Table 6G-1) should be the primary and preferred hand-signaling device because the STOP/SLOW paddle gives road users more positive guidance than red flags." Visual evidence: [Image 1] Figure 6D-1 labels the STOP/SLOW paddle column as the "PREFERRED METHOD" (with the flag reserved for "EMERGENCY SITUATIONS ONLY") and illustrates the "TO STOP TRAFFIC" posture — the flagger holding the STOP (R1-1) paddle, at least 18 inches wide, on a staff with one arm extended and the free hand raised palm-out toward traffic. Support (informational): - The optimum method of displaying a STOP or SLOW message is to place the STOP/SLOW paddle on a rigid staff that is tall enough that, when the end of the staff rests on the ground, the message is high enough to be seen by approaching or stopped traffic. Citations: - Section 6D.02 Guidance §1 (p.783) - Section 6D.05 Standard §3 (p.785) - Section 6D.02 Support §5 (p.783) - Figure 6D-1 (p.784)

**Gold answer:** The STOP/SLOW paddle is the primary and preferred device. To stop traffic, the flagger faces traffic, displays the STOP face in a stationary position, and raises the free arm with the palm toward traffic.

The color presented by the elements shall be yellow." - "An arrow board shall be a sign with a matrix of elements capable of either flashing or sequential displays." **Model answer:** Direct Answer: Entering a reduced school speed limit zone, drivers must first encounter a School (S1-1) sign posted in advance, then a School Speed Limit assembly or School Speed Limit When Flashing (S5-1) sign placed at or near the point where the reduced zone begins. Where the speed reduction exceeds 10 mph, a Reduced School Speed Limit Ahead (S4-5/S4-5a) sign should precede the speed limit assembly. Leaving the zone, the downstream end must be marked with an END SCHOOL SPEED LIMIT (S5-3) sign. Standards (mandatory): - "A School Speed Limit assembly (see Figure 7B-1) or a School Speed Limit When Flashing (S5-1) sign (see Figure 7B-1) shall be used to indicate the speed limit where a reduced school speed limit zone has been established... The School Speed Limit assembly or School Speed Limit When Flashing sign shall be placed at or as near as practicable to the point where the reduced school speed limit zone begins." - "If a reduced school speed limit zone has been established, a School (S1-1) sign shall be installed in advance (see Table 2C-3 for advance placement guidelines) of the first School Speed Limit sign assembly or S5-1 sign that is encountered in each direction as traffic approaches the reduced school speed limit zone." - "If used, the Reduced School Speed Limit Ahead sign shall be followed by a School Speed Limit sign or a School Speed Limit assembly." - "Except as provided in Paragraph 4 of this Section, the downstream end of an authorized and posted reduced school speed limit zone shall be identified with an END SCHOOL SPEED LIMIT (S5-3) sign." - Where higher fines apply only to speeding: "a FINES HIGHER (R2-6P), FINES DOUBLE (R2-6aP), or $XX FINE (R2-6bP) plaque shall be posted with the School Speed Limit (S5-1) sign and shall not be posted beneath the School Zone (S1-1) sign." Guidance (recommended): - "A Reduced School Speed Limit Ahead (S4-5 or S4-5a) sign (see Figure 7B-1) should be used to inform road users of a reduced speed zone where the speed limit is being reduced by more than 10 mph, or where engineering judgment indicates that advance notice would be appropriate." Visual evidence: [Image 1] Figure 7B-2 illustrates the full sequence in each direction: an advance S1-1 sign (with optional SCHOOL S4-3P plaque), a Reduced School Speed Limit Ahead (S4-5) sign, the School Speed Limit assembly (S4-3P/R2-1/S4-1P), and at the downstream end an END SCHOOL SPEED LIMIT (S5-3) or END SCHOOL ZONE (S5-2) sign with an optional SPEED LIMIT 45 (R2-1) sign above. [Image 2] Figure 7B-1 shows the components of the School Speed Limit assembly (S4-3P SCHOOL plaque, R2-1, and S4-1P/S4-2P/S4-4P/S4-6P plaques), the S5-1 WHEN FLASHING sign with optional fine plaques, the S4-5/S4-5a advance signs, and the S5-2/S5-3 end-of-zone signs. [Image 3] This page contains the Section 7B.05 text requiring the assembly at the zone beginning, the advance S1-1 sign, and the S5-3 end sign. [Image 4] This page shows Section 7B.06 provisions on fine plaques accompanying the School Speed Limit sign. [Image 5] This full-page view of Figure 7B-2 confirms the entering/leaving sign layout along both approaches. [Image 6] Figure 7B-4 shows the analogous sequence with higher-fines plaques added to the School Speed Limit assembly. Citations: - Section 7B.05 Standard §1 (p.978) - Section 7B.05 Standard §2 (p.978) - Section 7B.05 Standard §3 (p.978) - Section 7B.05 Standard §14 (p.980) - Section 7B.05 Guidance §13 (p.980) - Section 7B.06 Standard §3 (p.980) - Figure 7B-2 (p.975) - Figure 7B-1 (p.973)

**Gold answer:** Approaching traffic first encounters an advance School (S1-1) sign, then the School Speed Limit assembly or S5-1 at the beginning of the reduced zone. The downstream end is normally marked by S5-3 END SCHOOL SPEED LIMIT; S5-2 END SCHOOL ZONE may be used when both zones end together, with a downstream R2-1 optionally mounted.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: From top to bottom, the assembly consists of the Crossbuck (R15-1) sign at the top, the inverted-T Number of Tracks (R15-2P) plaque mounted directly below the Crossbuck, and the YIELD (R1-2) or STOP (R1-1) sign below that on the same support (or on a separate support closer to the traveled way). The Number of Tracks plaque is mandatory whenever two or more tracks are present at the grade crossing. Standards (mandatory): - "A Crossbuck Assembly shall consist of a Crossbuck (R15-1) sign, and a Number of Tracks (R15-2P) plaque if two or more tracks are present, that complies with the provisions of Section 8B.03, and either a YIELD (R1-2) or STOP (R1-1) sign installed on the same support, except as provided in Paragraph 10 of this Section. YIELD or STOP signs used at passive grade crossings shall be installed in compliance with the provisions of Section 2B.18, and Figures 8B-2 and 8B-3." - "If there are two or more tracks at a grade crossing, the number of tracks shall be indicated on a supplemental Number of Tracks (R15-2P) plaque (see Figure 8B-1) of inverted T shape mounted below the Crossbuck sign in the manner shown in Figure 8B-2." - "If a Crossbuck sign support at a passive grade crossing does not include a YIELD or STOP sign (either because the YIELD or STOP sign is placed on a separate support or because a YIELD or STOP sign is not present on the approach), a vertical strip of retroreflective white material, not less than 2 inches in width, shall be used for the full length of the front of the support from the Crossbuck sign or Number of Tracks plaque to within 2 feet above the near edge of the roadway." Guidance (recommended): - The use of STOP signs at passive grade crossings should be limited to unusual conditions where requiring all motor vehicles to make a full stop is determined to be necessary by a Diagnostic Team; per the figure notes in Figure 8B-2, "YIELD or STOP signs are used only at passive crossings. A STOP sign is used only if an engineering study determines that it is appropriate for that particular approach." - If the YIELD or STOP sign is installed on a separate support than the Crossbuck sign, it should be placed in the same plane as the Crossbuck sign and closer to the traveled way, with a minimum 2-inch separation between the nearest points of the YIELD or STOP sign and the Crossbuck sign. Options (permitted): - A strip of retroreflective white material not less than 2 inches in width may be used on the back of each blade of each Crossbuck sign for the length of each blade at active grade crossings. Visual evidence: - [Image 4] (Figure 8B-2, Page 1080) shows the vertical order on a single support: Crossbuck at the top mounted at approximately 9 feet, the "3 TRACKS" inverted-T plaque immediately below it, and the YIELD (or STOP) sign below the plaque, with the retroreflective strip extending down the support to within 2 feet of the roadway edge. - [Image 2] (Figure 8B-3) shows the separate-support alternative, with the YIELD sign placed closer to the traveled way in the same plane as the Crossbuck and a 2-inch minimum separation between sign edges. - [Image 1] and [Image 3]/[Image 5] (Figure 8E-5) show the same vertical stacking — Crossbuck, "2 TRACKS" plaque, then YIELD or STOP sign — applied at a pathway or sidewalk grade crossing. - [Image 6] (Page 1036) shows Table 8B-1 sign sizes (R15-1 Crossbuck 48 x 9; R15-2P plaque 27 x 18) and the Section 8B.04 Standard defining the assembly composition. Citations: - Section 8B.04 Standard §1 (p.995) - Section 8B.03 Standard §6 (p.993) - Section 8B.04 Standard §20 (p.1000) - Section 8B.04 Guidance §6 (p.997) - Section 8B.04 Guidance §13 (p.1000) - Section 8B.03 Option §9 (p.995) - Figure 8B-3 (p.998) - Figure 8E-5 (p.1039) - Page 1080 - Page 1036

**Gold answer:** The Crossbuck is at the top, the inverted-T Number of Tracks plaque is directly below it, and the YIELD or STOP sign is below the plaque on the support. The Number of Tracks plaque is required when two or more tracks are present.

Standards (mandatory): - "If used, the distance from the upstream edge of the bicycle box that is nearest to the stop line for motor vehicles to the downstream edge of the bicycle box that is nearest the crosswalk or intersection shall be at least 10 feet. (Section 9E.12 Standard, shown on Page 1146) - "If used, green-colored pavement shall be used in the full limits of the bicycle box." ## T001

**Question:** What is the purpose of the MUTCD, and what four objectives are used to achieve that purpose?

**Grade:** C=1.00, Comp=1.00, F=1.00, Cit=1.00, Strict full credit=Yes

**Descriptive mean:** 100.0% (Full credit)

**Evaluation confidence:** 82%

**Rationale:** The response received full credit because the audit accepted the substantive answer, coverage of required elements, grounding, and evidence localization without a deduction. The locked benchmark marks the question as answerable, so the response needed to state and support the required MUTCD answer. Correctness rationale: The response states the locked conclusion, rule, value, or interpretation correctly. Completeness rationale: The audit accepted the response as covering all required answer elements. Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 1A.01, Section 1B.01, p.1, p.4, Page 42, 23 U.S.C. 109(d). Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: Its objectives are to promote safety, inclusion, and mobility | promote efficiency through national uniformity in device meaning and appearance | promote consistency in use, installation, and operation Adjudication note: Complete answer with the required purpose, scope, and four objectives. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=0.832 and Context Precision@6=0.333. The visual-routing decision did not match the locked modality requirement for this question. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: The purpose of the MUTCD is to establish uniform national criteria for the use of traffic control devices that meet the needs and expectancy of road users on all streets, highways, pedestrian and bicycle facilities, and site roadways open to public travel. This purpose is achieved through four objectives: promoting safety, inclusion, and mobility; promoting efficiency through national uniformity in device meaning and appearance; promoting national consistency in device use, installation, and operation; and providing basic principles for traffic engineers' decisions. Note that this purpose statement is Support (explanatory) material, though the MUTCD itself carries mandatory force as the national standard. Standards (mandatory): - "The Manual on Uniform Traffic Control Devices for Streets and Highways (MUTCD) is incorporated by reference in 23 Code of Federal Regulations (CFR), Part 655, Subpart F and shall be recognized as the national standard for all traffic control devices installed on any street, highway, bikeway, or site roadway open to public travel... in accordance with 23 U.S.C. 109(d) and 402(a)." Support (informational): - "The purpose of the MUTCD is to establish uniform national criteria for the use of traffic control devices that meet the needs and expectancy of road users on all streets, highways, pedestrian and bicycle facilities, and site roadways open to public travel." - "This purpose is achieved through the following objectives: A. Promote safety, inclusion, and mobility for all users of the road network; B. Promote efficiency through creating national uniformity in the meaning and appearance of traffic control devices; C. Promote national consistency in the use, installation, and operation of traffic control devices; and D. Provide basic principles for traffic engineers to use in making decisions regarding the use, installation, operation, maintenance, and removal of traffic control devices." Visual evidence: [Image 5] shows the full page of Chapter 1A, Section 1A.01 (Purpose of the MUTCD), displaying the purpose statement and the lettered objectives A through D as Support material. Citations: - Section 1A.01 Support §1 (p.1) - Section 1A.01 Support §2 (p.1) - Section 1B.01 Standard §1 (p.4) - Page 42

**Gold answer:** The MUTCD establishes uniform national criteria for traffic control devices on streets, highways, pedestrian and bicycle facilities, and site roadways open to public travel. Its objectives are to promote safety, inclusion, and mobility; promote efficiency through national uniformity in device meaning and appearance; promote consistency in use, installation, and operation; and provide basic decision-making principles for traffic engineers.

The locked benchmark marks the question as answerable, so the response needed to state and support the required MUTCD answer. Correctness rationale: The response states the locked conclusion, rule, value, or interpretation correctly. Completeness rationale: The audit accepted the response as covering all required answer elements. Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 1A.01, Section 1B.01, Section 1B.08, Section 1B.05, p.1, p.4. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: Applicability is independent of public or private ownership or jurisdiction and independent of whether funding is Federal, State, local, or private. Adjudication note: Correctly states independence from ownership, jurisdiction, and funding. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=0.877 and Context Precision@6=0.333. The visual-routing decision did not match the locked modality requirement for this question. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: No. The MUTCD's applicability to facilities open to public travel does not depend on ownership type (public or private) or the source of funding (Federal, State, local, or private). Instead, applicability turns on whether the facility is open to public travel: the MUTCD applies to publicly and privately owned streets, roadways, bikeways, site roadways, and toll roads open to the public, and does not apply to private gated roadways, private railroad grade crossings, or parking areas regardless of ownership. Standards (mandatory): - "In accordance with 23 CFR 655.603(a), the MUTCD shall apply to all of the following types of facilities: A. Any street, roadway, or bikeway open to public travel, either publicly or privately owned; B. Streets and roadways on sites that are off the public right-of-way that are open to public travel without full-time access restrictions... C. Publicly-owned toll roads, including those under the jurisdiction of a public agency, public authority, or public-private partnership; D. Privately-owned toll roads where the public is allowed to travel without access restriction... and E. Grade crossings of publicly-owned roadways with railroads or light rail transit." - "The MUTCD shall not apply to the following types of facilities: A. Roadways within private gated properties where access to the general public is restricted at all times; B. Grade crossings of privately-owned roadways with railroads; and C. Parking areas, including the driving aisles within those parking areas, that are either publicly or privately owned." Guidance (recommended): - A local jurisdiction, toll facility operator, or owner of a site roadway open to public travel requesting permission to experiment or to use a device under an existing interim approval should first check for any State laws, regulations, and/or directives covering the application of the MUTCD provisions that might apply. - Before requesting permission to experiment with a new device or application, an owner of a site roadway open to public travel should first check for any laws, regulations, and/or directives covering the application of the MUTCD that might apply. Supporting context (informational, not normative): Section 1A.01 states directly that "The applicability of the MUTCD to facilities open to public travel is independent of the type of ownership or jurisdiction (public or private) and the source of funding (Federal, State, local, or private)." Visual evidence: [Image 5] shows the Chapter 1A General page containing the Support statement that applicability is independent of ownership, jurisdiction, and funding source. [Image 6] shows the Chapter 1B page with Section 1B.01, listing the facility types to which the MUTCD shall and shall not apply, including both publicly and privately owned facilities. Citations: - Section 1A.01 Support §5 (p.1) - Section 1B.01 Standard §2 (p.4) - Section 1B.01 Standard §3 (p.4) - Section 1B.08 Guidance §1 (p.12) - Section 1B.05 Guidance §7 (p.7) - Page 42 - Page 45

**Gold answer:** No. Applicability is independent of public or private ownership or jurisdiction and independent of whether funding is Federal, State, local, or private.

## T003

**Descriptive mean:** 100.0% (Full credit)

The locked benchmark marks the question as answerable, so the response needed to state and support the required MUTCD answer. Correctness rationale: The response states the locked conclusion, rule, value, or interpretation correctly. Completeness rationale: The audit accepted the response as covering all required answer elements. Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 1A.02, Section 6A.03, Section 3I.01, p.1, p.768, p.632. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: Infrastructure elements that physically restrict travel paths or speeds, including islands, curbs, speed humps, other raised surfaces, and transverse or longitudinal rumble strips… Adjudication note: Correctly excludes the listed infrastructure elements and rumble strips. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=0.920 and Context Precision@6=0.333. The visual-routing decision did not match the locked modality requirement for this question. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: No. The MUTCD explicitly states that infrastructure elements that restrict travel paths or vehicle speeds—such as islands, curbs, speed humps, and other raised roadway surfaces—are not traffic control devices, and that transverse or longitudinal rumble strips are likewise not traffic control devices. This is consistent with the MUTCD's definition of traffic control devices, which covers only devices whose primary purpose is communicating a regulatory, warning, or guidance message to road users. Standards (mandatory): - "Traffic control devices shall be defined as all signs, signals, markings, channelizing devices, or other devices that use colors, shapes, symbols, words, sounds, or tactile information for the primary purpose of communicating a regulatory, warning, or guidance message to road users on a street, highway, pedestrian facility, bikeway, pathway, or site roadway open to public travel." Islands, curbs, speed humps, raised surfaces, and rumble strips do not fall within this definition. Support (informational): - "Infrastructure elements that restrict the road user's travel paths or vehicle speeds, such as islands, curbs, speed humps, and other raised roadway surfaces, are not traffic control devices. Transverse or longitudinal rumble strips are also not traffic control devices. Operational devices associated with the application of traffic control strategies such as fencing, roadway lighting, barriers, and attenuators are shown in this Manual for context, but their design, application, and usage are not specified since they are not traffic control devices." - "Although they are not considered to be traffic control devices, raised islands (see Chapter 3J) are also sometimes used to channelize traffic." Visual evidence: [Image 5] shows the full text of Section 1A.02, Paragraph 02, which states that islands, curbs, speed humps, other raised roadway surfaces, and transverse or longitudinal rumble strips are not traffic control devices. Citations: - Section 1A.02 Support §2 (p.1) - Section 6A.03 Standard §2 (p.768) - Section 3I.01 Support §2 (p.632) - Page 42

**Gold answer:** No. Infrastructure elements that physically restrict travel paths or speeds, including islands, curbs, speed humps, other raised surfaces, and transverse or longitudinal rumble strips, are not traffic control devices.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: When an MUTCD provision requires a decision for implementation, that decision must be made by an engineer, or by an individual working under the supervision of an engineer, who has the appropriate experience and expertise for traffic control device decisions. The decision must be based on engineering judgment or an engineering study, as the specific MUTCD provision requires. Supporting text clarifies that the final decision to implement the engineer's solution rests with the agency having jurisdiction over the roadway, after consultation with and based on the engineer's advice. Support (informational context): - MUTCD principles are developed for and used by individuals who are duly authorized and qualified to conduct traffic control device activities. - The MUTCD presumes the user has sufficient working knowledge, professional training and experience, and education in traffic engineering principles. - The MUTCD does not mandate that an engineer make the final decision to implement or execute the engineering-based solution in the field; the engineer (or individual under an engineer's supervision, or other individual duly authorized by State law) develops the solution with specifications for selection and placement of devices, but responsibility for the final implementation decision rests with the agency having jurisdiction over the roadway, after consultation with and based on advice from the engineer. Visual evidence: [Image 8] shows the full page containing Section 1A.04 (Use of the MUTCD), including the Standard paragraph requiring implementation decisions to be made by an engineer or a supervised individual using engineering judgment or engineering study. Citations: - Section 1A.04 Standard §2 (p.2) - Section 1A.04 Support §1 (p.2) - Section 1A.01 Support §6 (p.1) - Section 1D.03 Support §4 (p.32) - Page 43

**Gold answer:** The decision must be made by an engineer, or by an individual under the supervision of an engineer, who has the appropriate experience and expertise. The decision must use engineering judgment or an engineering study as required by the applicable MUTCD provision.

## T005

**Question:** How do the MUTCD headings Standard, Guidance, Option, and Support differ, including the verbs typically associated with them?

**Grade:** C=1.00, Comp=1.00, F=1.00, Cit=1.00, Strict full credit=Yes

**Descriptive mean:** 100.0% (Full credit)

**Evaluation confidence:** 82%

**Rationale:** The response received full credit because the audit accepted the substantive answer, coverage of required elements, grounding, and evidence localization without a deduction. The locked benchmark marks the question as answerable, so the response needed to state and support the required MUTCD answer. Correctness rationale: The response states the locked conclusion, rule, value, or interpretation correctly. Completeness rationale: The audit accepted the response as covering all required answer elements. Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 1D.03, Section 1C.01, Section 1A.04, Figure 3B-6, Figure 9E-5, Figure 3B-27. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: A Standard is mandatory or prohibitive and typically uses 'shall.' Guidance is recommended practice with justified deviations allowed and typically uses 'should.' An Option is per… Adjudication note: Correctly distinguishes Standard, Guidance, Option, and Support. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=1.000 and Context Precision@6=0.167. The visual-routing decision did not match the locked modality requirement for this question. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: The MUTCD uses four headings to classify the normative weight of its text. Standard statements are mandatory ("shall"), Guidance statements are recommended ("should"), Option statements are permissive ("may"), and Support statements are purely informational and use none of those verbs. Each category also has a distinct typography: Standards appear in bold, Guidance in unbold italics, and Options and Support in unbold type. Standards (mandatory): - "Standard—a statement of required, mandatory, or specifically prohibitive practice regarding a traffic control device. … All Standard statements are labeled, and the text appears in bold type. The verb 'shall' is typically used. The verbs 'should' and 'may' are not used in Standard statements. Standard statements are sometimes modified by Option statements." - "Guidance—a statement of recommended practice in typical situations, with deviations allowed if engineering judgment or engineering study (see Section 1D.03) indicates the deviation to be appropriate. All Guidance statements are labeled, and the text appears in unbold italic type. The verb 'should' is typically used. The verbs 'shall' and 'may' are not used in Guidance statements. Guidance statements are sometimes modified by Option statements." - "Option—a statement of practice that is a permissive condition and carries no requirement or recommendation. Option statements sometimes contain allowable modifications to a Standard or Guidance statement. All Option statements are labeled, and the text appears in unbold type. The verb 'may' is typically used. The verbs 'shall' and 'should' are not used in Option statements." - "Support—an informational statement that does not convey any degree of mandate, recommendation, authorization, prohibition, or enforceable condition. Support statements are labeled, and the text appears in unbold type. The verbs 'shall,' 'should,' and 'may' are not used in Support statements." - Deviations from Standards are narrowly limited: "In limited, location-specific cases, the results of a documented engineering study (see Section 1D.03) might indicate a deviation from one or more requirements of a Standard provision to be appropriate." Visual evidence: [Image 5] shows the full text of Section 1C.01 defining all four headings, including the Option and Support definitions and their associated verbs and typography. [Image 6] shows Section 1A.04, which explains that "the headings Standard, Guidance, Option, and Support … are used to classify the nature of the text that follows," and that figures and tables (including their notes) may constitute any of the four categories. [Image 1], [Image 2], [Image 3], and [Image 7] illustrate typical MUTCD pages where text is organized under labeled Standard (bold), Guidance (italic), Option, and Support headings following the figures. [Image 4] and [Image 8] likewise show the alternating labeled headings applied throughout the Manual's provisions. Citations: - Section 1C.01 Standard §1 (p.13) - Page 54 - Page 43 - Figure 3B-6 (p.546) - Figure 9E-5 (p.1094) - Figure 3B-27 (p.586) - Figure 8D-3 (p.1034) - Page 627 - Page 313

**Gold answer:** A Standard is mandatory or prohibitive and typically uses 'shall.' Guidance is recommended practice with justified deviations allowed and typically uses 'should.' An Option is permissive and typically uses 'may.' Support is informational and conveys no mandate, recommendation, permission, prohibition, or enforceable condition.

**Grade:** C=0.80, Comp=0.75, F=1.00, Cit=0.75, Strict full credit=No

**Descriptive mean:** 82.5% (Partial credit)

**Evaluation confidence:** 82%

**Rationale:** The response received 82.5% as the descriptive mean of the four official item scores. The official rubric did not award strict full credit because at least one dimension scored below 1.00. The locked benchmark marks the question as answerable, so the response needed to state and support the required MUTCD answer. Correctness rationale: The response gives the main conclusion but loses precision on one or more required details. Completeness rationale: The audit found incomplete coverage even though the automated clause comparison did not isolate a single missing sentence. Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The response provides some evidence localization, but the audit judged it incomplete, imprecise, or partly incorrect. The response cites: Section 1C.02, Section 1D.03, Section 1A.04, Section 1C.01, Section 4C.01, p.32. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: an engineering study must be documented in writing. | Engineering judgment does not require documentation Adjudication note: Correct documentation distinction, but the shared evaluation and engineering-principles definition is not stated as explicitly as the locked gold annotation. Retrieval context: The top five text chunks did not include a chunk that matched the locked page, section, table, or figure evidence. No relevant text chunk appeared in the six logged ranks, so MRR was 0. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=0.000 and Context Precision@6=0.000. The visual-routing decision did not match the locked modality requirement for this question. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: The key documentation difference is that engineering judgment does not have to be documented, while an engineering study must be documented in writing. Per the definitions shown in the glossary (Section 1C.02), both must be exercised/performed by a professional engineer (or an individual working under the supervision of such an engineer), but only the engineering study carries a written documentation requirement. This matters in practice: a deviation from a Standard provision is allowed only where the agency fully documents the engineering basis through an engineering study. Standards (mandatory): - From the glossary definition of Engineering Judgment: "Documentation of engineering judgment is not required." [Image 5] - From the glossary definition of Engineering Study: "An engineering study shall be performed by a professional engineer (see definition in this Section) with appropriate traffic engineering expertise, or by an individual working under the supervision of such an engineer... An engineering study shall be documented in writing." [Image 5] - "Where the content of this Manual requires a decision for implementation, such decisions shall be made by an engineer, or an individual under the supervision of an engineer... Those decisions shall be made using engineering judgment or engineering study, as required by the MUTCD provision." - "In limited, location-specific cases, the results of a documented engineering study (see Section 1D.03) might indicate a deviation from one or more requirements of a Standard provision to be appropriate." Support (informational): - "Definitions of professional engineer, engineering study, and engineering judgment are provided in Section 1C.02." - "Site-specific conditions might result in the determination that it is impossible or impracticable to comply with a Standard at that location... In such limited, specific cases, the deviation is allowed, provided that the agency or official having jurisdiction fully documents, through an engineering study, the engineering basis for the deviation." — i.e., undocumented engineering judgment is not sufficient to deviate from a Standard; a documented engineering study is required. - Similarly, agencies can install a traffic control signal where no warrants are met "but only after conducting an engineering study that documents the rationale" for the decision. Visual evidence: [Image 5] shows the glossary definitions (items 76 and 77), where engineering judgment expressly states "Documentation of engineering judgment is not required" and engineering study states "An engineering study shall be documented in writing." [Image 7] shows Section 1D.03, which explains that deviations from Standards are allowed only when the agency fully documents the engineering basis through an engineering study. Citations: - Section 1D.03 Support §1 (p.32) - Section 1D.03 Support §2 (p.32) - Section 1A.04 Standard §2 (p.2) - Section 1C.01 Standard §1 (p.13) - Section 4C.01 Support §20 (p.653) - Page 58 - Page 73

**Gold answer:** Both involve evaluating pertinent information and applying engineering principles by a professional engineer or a person under such an engineer's supervision. Engineering judgment does not require documentation; an engineering study must be documented in writing.

The response identifies: Section 1B.01, Section 1B.05, Section 1A.01, Section 6A.04, Figure 3B-23, Figure 9B-3. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: It applies to the shopping-center site roadways that are open to public travel without full-time access restrictions. | It does not apply to the parking area itself, including its driving aisles. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: Yes — roadways within a shopping center that are open to public travel without full-time access restrictions are "site roadways open to public travel," and the MUTCD applies to them by mandate. No — the MUTCD expressly does not apply to parking areas, including the driving aisles within those parking areas, regardless of public or private ownership. Standards (mandatory): - "In accordance with 23 CFR 655.603(a), the MUTCD shall apply to all of the following types of facilities: ... B. Streets and roadways on sites that are off the public right-of-way that are open to public travel without full-time access restrictions. Examples include roadways within shopping centers, office parks, airports, sports arenas, other similar business and/or recreation facilities... The above-described examples of streets and roadways are referred to in this Manual as site roadways open to public travel." - "The MUTCD shall not apply to the following types of facilities: ... C. Parking areas, including the driving aisles within those parking areas, that are either publicly or privately owned." Guidance (recommended): - Before requesting permission to experiment with a new device or application, an owner of a site roadway open to public travel should first check for any laws, regulations, and/or directives covering the application of the MUTCD that might apply. Support (informational): - The purpose of the MUTCD is to establish uniform national criteria for traffic control devices on all streets, highways, pedestrian and bicycle facilities, and site roadways open to public travel. - The applicability of the MUTCD to facilities open to public travel is independent of the type of ownership or jurisdiction (public or private) and the source of funding. - MUTCD crashworthiness provisions apply to all streets, highways, and site roadways open to public travel. Visual evidence: [Image 5] shows the full text of Section 1B.01, including the list of facilities where the MUTCD applies (item B naming shopping centers) and the exclusion of parking areas and their driving aisles. [Image 6] shows definition 247, "Site Roadways Open to Public Travel," which explicitly states that site roadways open to public travel "do not include parking areas..., including the driving aisles... within those parking areas." [Image 1] illustrates parking space markings for on-street (curbside) parking spaces along a roadway, which is within MUTCD scope, as distinct from off-street parking areas. [Image 4] similarly shows on-street back-in angle parking signing and markings along a public roadway. [Images 2 and 3] show Truck Parking Availability signs installed on the highway mainline directing drivers to parking facilities, not devices inside parking areas themselves. Citations: - Section 1B.01 Standard §2 (p.4) - Section 1B.01 Standard §3 (p.4) - Section 1B.05 Guidance §7 (p.7) - Section 1A.01 Support §1 (p.1) - Section 1A.01 Support §5 (p.1) - Section 6A.04 Support §1 (p.768) - Page 45 - Page 67 - Figure 3B-23 (p.582) - Figure 9B-3 (p.1057) - Figure 2I-9 (p.490) - Figure 2I-10 (p.491)

**Gold answer:** It applies to the shopping-center site roadways that are open to public travel without full-time access restrictions. It does not apply to the parking area itself, including its driving aisles.

Correctness rationale: The response states the locked conclusion, rule, value, or interpretation correctly. **Model answer:** Direct Answer: States or other Federal agencies with their own MUTCDs or Supplements must revise them to be in substantial conformance with changes to the National MUTCD within 2 years of the effective date of the Final Rule adopting those changes. This requirement also extends to agency policies, directives, specifications, or standard drawings that modify Standard, Guidance, or Option provisions, since those documents are considered supplements to the MUTCD. Standards (mandatory): - "States or other Federal agencies that have their own MUTCDs or Supplements shall revise these MUTCDs or Supplements to be in substantial conformance with changes to the National MUTCD within 2 years of the effective date of the Final Rule for the changes [23 CFR 655.603(b)(3)]. Substantial conformance of such State or other Federal agency MUTCDs or Supplements shall be as defined in 23 CFR 655.603(b)(1)." - "For the purposes of Paragraph 2 of this Section, policies, directives, specifications, standard drawings, or similar documents that are issued by an agency and that change or modify Standard, Guidance, or Option provisions in this Manual shall be considered as supplements to the MUTCD and shall also be revised to be in substantial conformance with the National MUTCD." - "After the effective date of a new edition of the MUTCD or a revision thereto, or after the adoption thereof by the State, whichever occurs later, new or reconstructed devices installed shall comply with the new edition or revision, as required by 23 CFR 655.603." Visual evidence: [Image 2] (Figure 1B-2) illustrates the rulemaking process in which the FHWA publishes a Final Rule, after which State manuals must be brought into substantial conformance with the National MUTCD. [Image 3] (Table 1B-1) shows examples of separate target compliance dates the FHWA has established for particular MUTCD provisions, which is a related but distinct mechanism from the 2-year conformance requirement. Citations: - Section 1B.02 Standard §2 (p.4) - Section 1B.02 Standard §3 (p.5) - Section 1B.03 Standard §3 (p.5) - Section 1B.03 Support §6 (p.5) - Figure 1B-2 (p.9) - Table 1B-1 (p.5)

**Gold answer:** They must revise their MUTCDs or supplements into substantial conformance within 2 years of the effective date of the Final Rule for the changes.

## T009

**Question:** When a non-compliant traffic control device is replaced because it is damaged, missing, or no longer serviceable, what is the general rule and the limited replacement-in-kind exception?

**Grade:** C=1.00, Comp=1.00, F=1.00, Cit=1.00, Strict full credit=Yes

**Descriptive mean:** 100.0% (Full credit)

**Evaluation confidence:** 82%

**Rationale:** The response received full credit because the audit accepted the substantive answer, coverage of required elements, grounding, and evidence localization without a deduction. The locked benchmark marks the question as answerable, so the response needed to state and support the required MUTCD answer. Correctness rationale: The response states the locked conclusion, rule, value, or interpretation correctly. Completeness rationale: The audit accepted the response as covering all required answer elements. Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 1C.02, Section 1B.03, Section 6K.01, Section 5A.04, Section 1D.10, p.6. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: A non-compliant device may be replaced in kind only when engineering judgment finds that a lone compliant device among adjacent non-compliant devices would confuse users and/or th… | The replacement must generally be a compliant device. Adjudication note: Correct general rule and both parts of the replacement-in-kind exception. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=1.000 and Context Precision@6=0.500. The visual-routing decision did not match the locked modality requirement for this question. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: The general rule is that when a non-compliant device is being replaced or refurbished because it is damaged, missing, or no longer serviceable, it must be replaced with a compliant device. The limited exception permits replacement in kind when engineering judgment shows it is more appropriate—either because a single compliant device amid a series of adjacent non-compliant devices would confuse road users, and/or because the scheduled replacement of the whole series will still achieve timely MUTCD compliance. Standards (mandatory): - "A non-compliant traffic control device that is being replaced or refurbished because it is damaged, missing, or no longer serviceable (see definition in Section 1C.02) for any reason shall be replaced with a compliant device, except as provided for in Paragraph 13 of this Section." - "Unless a particular device is no longer serviceable (see definition in Section 1C.02), non-compliant devices on existing highways and bikeways shall be brought into compliance with the current edition of the National MUTCD as part of the systematic upgrading of substandard traffic control devices (and installation of new required traffic control devices) required pursuant to the Highway Safety Program, 23 U.S.C. §402(a)." - In the temporary traffic control context: "Channelizing devices that are no longer serviceable (see definition in Section 1C.02) shall be replaced." Guidance (recommended): - Agencies should establish "maintenance policies that incorporate effective practices to identify and then fix or replace in a timely manner any traffic control device that is reaching the end of its useful life, or that is damaged or otherwise no longer serviceable." - Functional maintenance of traffic control devices should be used to determine if certain devices need to be changed to meet current traffic conditions. Options (permitted): - A non-compliant traffic control device may be replaced in kind when engineering judgment indicates it is more appropriate because: (A) one compliant device in the midst of a series of adjacent non-compliant devices would be confusing to road users, and/or (B) the schedule for replacement of the whole series of non-compliant devices will result in achieving timely compliance with the MUTCD. Visual evidence: [Image 5] shows the full text of Section 1B.03, including the Standard requiring replacement with a compliant device (Paragraph 12) and the Option describing the replacement-in-kind exception (Paragraph 13). Citations: - Section 1B.03 Standard §12 (p.6) - Section 1B.03 Option §13 (p.6) - Section 1B.03 Standard §5 (p.5) - Section 6K.01 Standard §15 (p.815) - Section 5A.04 Guidance §6 (p.762) - Section 1D.10 Guidance §1 (p.38) - Page 47

**Gold answer:** The replacement must generally be a compliant device. A non-compliant device may be replaced in kind only when engineering judgment finds that a lone compliant device among adjacent non-compliant devices would confuse users and/or the planned replacement schedule for the whole series will achieve timely compliance.

**Grade:** C=1.00, Comp=1.00, F=1.00, Cit=1.00, Strict full credit=Yes

**Descriptive mean:** 100.0% (Full credit)

**Evaluation confidence:** 82%

**Rationale:** The response received full credit because the audit accepted the substantive answer, coverage of required elements, grounding, and evidence localization without a deduction. The locked benchmark marks the question as answerable, so the response needed to state and support the required MUTCD answer. Correctness rationale: The response states the locked conclusion, rule, value, or interpretation correctly. Completeness rationale: The audit accepted the response as covering all required answer elements. Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 1C.02, Section 1B.07, Section 1B.05, Section 1B.03, Section 1D.09, Figure 1B-1. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: A non-compliant device or application may not be used on a covered public-travel facility without first receiving official experimentation approval from the FHWA Office of Transpo… Adjudication note: Correctly requires prior FHWA experimentation approval. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=1.000 and Context Precision@6=0.333. The visual-routing decision did not match the locked modality requirement for this question. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: No. A non-compliant traffic control device or application may not be deployed on any street, highway, bikeway, or site roadway open to public travel until the jurisdiction has first received official FHWA approval to experiment. This flows from the general federal requirement that all devices on roads open to public travel be in substantial conformance with FHWA standards; installation of the experimental device occurs only after FHWA review and approval in the experimentation process. Standards (mandatory): - "A traffic control device or application that does not comply with the provisions of this Manual shall not be used on any street, highway, bikeway, or site roadway open to public travel (see definition in Section 1C.02) without first receiving official approval to experiment from the FHWA’s Office of Transportation Operations." - "The U.S. Secretary of Transportation, under authority granted by the Highway Safety Act of 1966, decreed that traffic control devices on all streets and highways open to public travel in accordance with 23 U.S.C. 109(d) and 402(a) in each State shall be in substantial conformance with the Standards issued or endorsed by the FHWA." - "Before any highway, site roadway open to public travel (see definition in Section 1C.02), detour, or temporary route is opened to public travel, all traffic control devices necessary for safe operation shall be in place." Support (informational context): - 23 CFR 655.603 also requires traffic control devices on all streets, highways, bikeways, and site roadways open to public travel in each State to be in substantial conformance with standards issued or endorsed by the Federal Highway Administrator. - After an experiment ends, the FHWA Office of Transportation Operations might allow an effective experimental device without safety concerns to remain in use, typically if it is actively being considered for interim approval under Section 1B.07 — this is a post-experiment allowance, not a pre-approval one. - Interim approval allows optional use of a device under agreed conditions until official rulemaking occurs; it is a separate mechanism from experimentation and likewise involves FHWA authorization before use. Visual evidence: [Image 1] Figure 1B-1 shows the required sequence — the requesting jurisdiction submits a request to FHWA, FHWA reviews it, and only after the "Approved? Yes" decision does the jurisdiction install the experimental traffic control device (a "No" decision loops back through responding to FHWA questions and further review). [Image 3] shows the same Figure 1B-1 flowchart on the manual page, confirming installation occurs only after approval. [Image 2] shows Section 1B.05 with the Standard prohibiting use of non-compliant devices without prior FHWA approval to experiment. [Image 5] shows the mandatory contents of a request for permission to experiment, reinforcing that a formal approved request precedes deployment. [Image 4] shows Figure 1B-2, the separate process (including interim approval) for incorporating new devices into the MUTCD after successful experimentation. Citations: - Section 1B.05 Standard §2 (p.6) - Section 1B.03 Standard §1 (p.5) - Section 1D.09 Standard §1 (p.38) - Section 1B.03 Support §2 (p.5) - Section 1B.05 Support §15 (p.9) - Section 1B.07 Support §3 (p.11) - Figure 1B-1 (p.7)

**Gold answer:** No. A non-compliant device or application may not be used on a covered public-travel facility without first receiving official experimentation approval from the FHWA Office of Transportation Operations.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: The final report for an approved experiment must be submitted within 3 months of the experiment's conclusion; if no final report is received and no mitigating circumstances are communicated within 6 months after the experimentation period ends, the experiment is deemed terminated and FHWA approval is rescinded. Similarly, missing two consecutive semi-annual progress reports terminates the experiment and rescinds FHWA's approval, requiring the site(s) to be restored to MUTCD compliance within 3 months. Standards (mandatory): - "The experimenting agency shall submit a final report within 3 months of the conclusion of an approved experiment. If a final report is not received by the FHWA's Office of Transportation Operations, and the experimenting agency fails to notify the FHWA of any mitigating circumstances within 6 months of the end of the approved experimentation period, then the experiment shall be considered terminated and shall constitute rescission of the FHWA's approval to the experimenting agency, requiring restoration of the site(s) to a condition that complies with the provisions of this Manual within 3 months." - "The experimenting agency shall submit a semi-annual progress report for any approved experiment even if no work was performed during the previous reporting period. Failure to submit two consecutive progress reports shall result in termination of the experiment and shall constitute rescission of the FHWA's approval to the experimenting agency, requiring restoration of the site(s) to a condition that complies with the provisions of this Manual within 3 months." - "The required semi-annual progress reports shall be submitted throughout the course of an approved experiment in accordance with the following schedule: A. No later than August 1st for the preceding period of January through June; and B. No later than February 1st for the preceding period of July through December." Visual evidence: [Image 1] Figure 1B-1 depicts the experimentation process, showing that after installing the experimental device the requesting jurisdiction provides semi-annual reports to FHWA Division & HQ and ultimately provides FHWA a copy of the final report. [Image 2] This page view shows the Standard text of Section 1B.05, including the semi-annual reporting schedule (¶12), the two-consecutive-missed-reports termination provision (¶13), and the 3-month final report requirement (¶14). [Image 3] This page view shows Figure 1B-1 in context, illustrating the reporting steps that culminate in delivery of the final report to FHWA. Citations: - Section 1B.05 Standard §14 (p.9) - Section 1B.05 Standard §13 (p.8) - Section 1B.05 Standard §12 (p.8) - Figure 1B-1 (p.7)

**Gold answer:** The final report must be submitted within 3 months after the experiment concludes. Failure to submit two consecutive semi-annual progress reports terminates the experiment and rescinds FHWA approval, requiring restoration of the sites to MUTCD-compliant conditions within 3 months.

**Grade:** C=1.00, Comp=0.75, F=1.00, Cit=1.00, Strict full credit=No

**Descriptive mean:** 93.8% (Strong partial credit)

**Evaluation confidence:** 82%

The official rubric did not award strict full credit because at least one dimension scored below 1.00. The locked benchmark marks the question as answerable, so the response needed to state and support the required MUTCD answer. Correctness rationale: The response states the locked conclusion, rule, value, or interpretation correctly. Completeness rationale: The response omits or underdevelops these locked elements: Protection of internal components or manufacturing methods can still be allowed. | Legally binding statements are required from the relevant agency/developer/supplier for experimentation. Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 1B.05, Section 1B.07, Section 1D.06, Figure 1B-1, Figure 1B-2, p.34. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: The traffic-control message, design, or application must be in the public domain and not protected by patent, trademark, or copyright. Adjudication note: Omits the allowance for proprietary protection of internal components or manufacturing methods. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=0.990 and Context Precision@6=0.833. The visual-routing decision did not match the locked modality requirement for this question. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: A traffic control device design or application must be in the public domain to be eligible for official experimentation or interim approval; express abandonment of all proprietary protections (patents, trademarks, copyrights) satisfies this requirement. In addition, experimentation requests must include a legally-binding statement from the experimenting agency, the manufacturer/developer, and the supplier certifying the device is unprotected and in the public domain, and acknowledging that establishing such protection later results in removal from the MUTCD or cancellation of the interim approval or experimentation authorization. Standards (mandatory): - "A traffic control device design or application shall not be eligible for official experimentation (see Section 1B.05) or interim approval (see Section 1B.07) unless it is in the public domain. Express abandonment of any and all forms of proprietary protection, such as patents, trademarks, or copyrights, related to the design and application of the traffic control device shall satisfy the requirement for the traffic control device to be in the public domain." - "A request for experimentation that involves a new traffic control device or a new application of an existing traffic control device shall include from the agency conducting the experiment, the manufacturer and/or developer of the device, and the supplier of the device, a legally-binding statement certifying that the traffic control device is not protected by a patent, trademark, or copyright in accordance with Section 1D.06, and that the traffic control device is in the public domain and can be used freely in traffic control device design and application without infringement or claim of trade secret misappropriation. The legally-binding statement shall also state that the agency conducting the experiment, the manufacturer and/or developer of the device, and the supplier of the device are aware that if patent, trademark, or copyright protection is established in the future for the device or application, such action will result in its removal from the MUTCD, cancellation of its interim approval, or cancellation of the authorization for experimentation." - The request for permission to experiment shall contain, among other elements, "F. A legally-binding statement that the experimental device or application is in the public domain, in accordance with Paragraph 16 of this Section." Visual evidence: - [Image 1] Figure 1B-1 shows the process for requesting and conducting experimentations for new traffic control devices, beginning with the requesting jurisdiction's submission to FHWA and FHWA review/approval — the process to which the public-domain eligibility condition applies. - [Image 2] Figure 1B-2 shows the process for incorporating new traffic control devices into the MUTCD, including the interim approval pathway that is likewise conditioned on public-domain status. Citations: - Section 1D.06 Standard §2 (p.34) - Section 1B.05 Standard §16 (p.9) - Section 1B.05 Standard §10 (p.8) - Figure 1B-1 (p.7) - Figure 1B-2 (p.9)

**Gold answer:** The traffic-control message, design, or application must be in the public domain and not protected by patent, trademark, or copyright. Legally binding statements are required from the relevant agency/developer/supplier for experimentation. Protection of internal components or manufacturing methods can still be allowed.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: Under Section 1D.01, an effective traffic control device should have five characteristics: it should fulfill a need, command attention, convey a clear and simple meaning, command respect from road users, and give adequate time for proper response. These are Guidance (recommended) provisions, and the Manual adds that design, placement, operation, maintenance, and uniformity should be carefully considered to maximize consistency with these five principles. Guidance (recommended): - "To be effective, a traffic control device should: A. Fulfill a need; B. Command attention; C. Convey a clear, simple meaning; D. Command respect from road users; and E. Give adequate time for proper response." - Design, placement, operation, maintenance, and uniformity should be carefully considered in order to maximize the ability of a traffic control device to be consistent with the five principles; vehicle speed and road-user types should be carefully considered as an element governing design, operation, placement, and location of devices. - The proper use of traffic control devices should provide the road user with the information necessary to safely, efficiently, and lawfully use the streets, highways, pedestrian facilities, and bikeways. - Placement of a device should be within the road user's view so adequate visibility is provided, appropriately positioned with respect to the location, object, or situation to which it applies, with location and legibility such that the road user has adequate time to respond in both day and night conditions (this operationalizes the "adequate time for proper response" principle). - Traffic control devices should be placed and operated in a uniform and consistent manner as part of maintaining uniformity in traffic control. - When developing messages for changeable message signs, Section 1D.01 should be consulted for the principles of an effective traffic control device. Visual evidence: - [Image 5] shows the printed text of Section 1D.01, including the Guidance paragraph listing the five characteristics (A through E) of an effective traffic control device. - [Image 6] shows Section 1D.09, whose placement and uniformity guidance supports the "adequate time for proper response" and "command respect" principles. - [Image 1], [Image 2], [Image 3], [Image 4], [Image 7], and [Image 8] depict signal assemblies, sight distance tables, mounting heights, signal face arrangements, and other sections not directly relevant to the five principles in Section 1D.01. Citations: - Section 1D.01 Guidance §3 (p.31) - Section 1D.01 Guidance §4 (p.31) - Section 1D.01 Guidance §5 (p.31) - Section 1D.09 Guidance §3 (p.38) - Section 1D.09 Guidance §4 (p.38) - Section 2L.04 Guidance §3 (p.513) - Page 72 - Page 79

**Gold answer:** It should fulfill a need, command attention, convey a clear and simple meaning, command respect from road users, and give adequate time for proper response.

Correctness rationale: The response states the locked conclusion, rule, value, or interpretation correctly. Completeness rationale: The audit accepted the response as covering all required answer elements. Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 1D.05, Section 2D.55, Section 9D.12, Table 2A-2, page 33, p.33. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: Fluorescent pink is for incident management, orange is for temporary traffic control, and purple is restricted to vehicles with registered electronic toll collection accounts. Adjudication note: Correct meanings for fluorescent pink, orange, and purple. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=1.000 and Context Precision@6=0.167. The visual-routing decision did not match the locked modality requirement for this question. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: The MUTCD's color code assigns fluorescent pink the general meaning of incident management, orange the meaning of temporary traffic control, and purple the restricted meaning of use only by vehicles with registered electronic toll collection (ETC) accounts. Because these colors carry critical, higher-priority meanings, the MUTCD also mandates that they not be used as background colors on community wayfinding guide signs or shared-use path Destination Guide signs. Standards (mandatory): - "The general meaning of the 13 colors shall be as follows: ... E. Fluorescent Pink—incident management ... I. Orange—temporary traffic control ... J. Purple—restricted to use only by vehicles with registered electronic toll collection (ETC) accounts." - "The standard colors of red, orange, yellow, purple, or the fluorescent versions thereof, fluorescent yellow-green, and fluorescent pink shall not be used as background colors for community wayfinding guide signs, in order to minimize possible confusion with critical, higher-priority regulatory and warning sign color meanings readily understood by road users." - "The standard colors of red, orange, yellow, purple, or the fluorescent versions thereof, fluorescent yellow-green, and fluorescent pink shall not be used as background colors for Destination Guide signs for shared-use paths, in order to minimize possible confusion with critical, higher-priority regulatory and warning sign color meanings readily understood by path users." Visual evidence: - [Image 2] shows Section 1D.05 (Color Code) on page 33, listing the mandatory general meanings of the 13 colors, including Fluorescent Pink—incident management, Orange—temporary traffic control, and Purple—restricted to ETC-account vehicles. - [Image 3] shows Table 2A-2 (Common Uses of Sign Colors), where orange appears as the background for Temporary Traffic Control and Incident Management signs, fluorescent pink as a background for Incident Management signs, and purple as the background for ETC-Account Only signs (with a note that purple use is restricted per Chapter 2F). - [Image 4] shows the community wayfinding sign Standard (Paragraph 19) prohibiting orange, purple, and fluorescent pink (among others) as background colors. - [Image 5] shows the parallel Standard (Paragraph 27) for shared-use path Destination Guide signs prohibiting those same colors as backgrounds. Citations: - Section 1D.05 Standard §2 (p.33) - Section 2D.55 Standard §19 (p.271) - Section 9D.12 Standard §27 (p.1084) - Table 2A-2 (p.43)

**Gold answer:** Fluorescent pink is for incident management, orange is for temporary traffic control, and purple is restricted to vehicles with registered electronic toll collection accounts.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: No. The MUTCD flatly prohibits advertising on traffic control devices or their supports — they must display only messages related to traffic control. The same prohibition applies specifically to changeable message signs (CMS) and their supports and equipment. Standards (mandatory): - "Traffic control devices or their supports shall not bear any advertising message or any other message that is not related to traffic control." - "CMS shall display only traffic operational, regulatory, warning, and guidance information except as otherwise provided in this Chapter. Advertising or other messages not related to traffic control shall not be displayed on a CMS or on its supports or other equipment." - "Traffic control devices, public announcements or notices, and other signs or messages within the highway right-of-way shall be placed only as authorized by a public authority or the official having jurisdiction... for the purpose of regulating, warning, or guiding traffic." Support (informational): - States are encouraged to adopt, through policy or legislation, the provisions of 23 CFR 750.108 that restrict outdoor advertising from resembling traffic control devices. - Traffic control devices are defined as devices whose primary purpose is communicating a regulatory, warning, or guidance message to road users. Citations: - Section 1D.07 Standard §1 (p.34) - Section 2L.02 Standard §1 (p.511) - Section 1D.02 Standard §3 (p.31) - Section 1D.02 Support §6 (p.32) - Section 6A.03 Standard §2 (p.768) - Section 1A.02 Support §1 (p.1)

**Gold answer:** No. They may not bear advertising or any message unrelated to traffic control. Acknowledgment signs, Specific Service signs, and Tourist-Oriented Directional signs are not treated as advertising under this provision.
