Correctness rationale: The response states the locked conclusion, rule, value, or interpretation correctly. Completeness rationale: The audit accepted the response as covering all required answer elements. Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. The response identifies: Section 2D.39, Section 2A.03, Section 2F.12, Section 2G.10, Section 2A.13, Section 2L.01. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: Regulatory signs give notice of traffic laws or regulations. | Warning signs give notice of a situation that might not be readily apparent. | Guide signs show route designations, destinations, directions, distances, services, points of interest, or other geographical, recreational, or cultural information. Adjudication note: Correct functional classification of regulatory, warning, and guide signs. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=1.000 and Context Precision@6=0.167. The visual-routing decision did not match the locked modality requirement for this question. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: The MUTCD classifies signs into three functional categories: regulatory signs give notice of traffic laws or regulations; warning signs give notice of situations that might not be readily apparent; and guide signs convey route, destination, direction, distance, service, and other geographical, recreational, or cultural information. This functional classification is mandatory, and it carries through other provisions—such as how regulatory or warning messages must be displayed when incorporated within guide signs. Standards (mandatory): - "Signs shall be defined by their function as follows: A. Regulatory signs give notice of traffic laws or regulations. B. Warning signs give notice of a situation that might not be readily apparent. C. Guide signs show route designations, destinations, directions, distances, services, points of interest, and other geographical, recreational, or cultural information." - "When regulatory messages are incorporated within a guide sign, they shall be on a rectangular panel with a black legend on a white background. When warning messages are incorporated within a guide sign, they shall be on a rectangular panel with a black legend on a yellow background." - "A combination of guide and regulatory signs shall be used in advance of and at the initial entry point and all intermediate entry points from general-purpose lanes or facilities to contiguous, barrier-separated, and buffer-separated preferential lanes where access between the preferential and general-purpose lanes is restricted to designated locations." Guidance (recommended): - Signs should be individually installed on separate posts or mountings except where one sign supplements another (relevant when regulatory, warning, and guide signs of different functions are located near one another). Support (informational): - A changeable message sign (CMS) is a traffic control device at all times, and the design, format, and display limitations that apply to conventional regulatory, warning, and guidance messages apply equally to messages displayed on a CMS. - Examples of regulatory and warning signs used together at roundabouts and neighborhood traffic circles are shown in Figures 2B-21 through 2B-24, with guide signs for roundabouts addressed separately in Section 2D.39. Visual evidence: - [Image 1] Figure 2A-2 shows examples of heights and lateral placements for signs of different functional classes, including a regulatory STOP sign, a warning sign with advisory speed plaque, a route assembly, and freeway/expressway and overhead guide signs. - [Image 2] Figure 2A-3 illustrates locations for typical regulatory signs (STOP, YIELD) at various intersection configurations. - [Image 3] Figure 2A-4 relates the sign chapters (2B, 2C, and following) that organize signs by their functional classification. - [Image 4] Table 2C-3 provides advance placement guidelines specifically for warning signs, reflecting the distinct function of that class. - [Image 6] The table of contents page shows Chapter 2C, "Warning Signs and Object Markers," confirming the Manual's organization of signs by function. - [Image 7] The color-use table distinguishes Regulatory, Warning, and Guide sign types with characteristic legend/background color combinations for each functional class. - [Image 8] Figure 2B-23 shows an example of regulatory and warning signs applied together at a two-lane roundabout, with guide signs referenced to Section 2D.39. Citations: - Section 2A.03 Standard §1 (p.40) - Section 2F.12 Standard §11 (p.392) - Section 2G.10 Standard §12 (p.422) - Section 2A.13 Guidance §6 (p.57) - Section 2L.01 Support §3 (p.510) - Section 2B.51 Support §6 (p.123) - Figure 2A-2 (p.51) - Figure 2A-3 (p.52) - Figure 2A-4 (p.50) - Table 2C-3 (p.152) - Page 9 - Page 85 - Page 162

**Gold answer:** Regulatory signs give notice of traffic laws or regulations. Warning signs give notice of a situation that might not be readily apparent. Guide signs show route designations, destinations, directions, distances, services, points of interest, or other geographical, recreational, or cultural information.

The response cites: Section 2A.04, Section 1A.05, Section 2D.04, Section 2A.09, Section 2A.08, Section 2D.55. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: Word messages generally read horizontally from left to right. | Place, street, and highway names use initial upper-case letters with lower-case letters Adjudication note: Correct left-to-right orientation but substitutes a community-wayfinding casing rule for the general sign-message casing rule. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=0.832 and Context Precision@6=0.333. The visual-routing decision did not match the locked modality requirement for this question. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: In general, word messages on signs must be aligned horizontally and read left to right, and must use the standard wording shown in the Manual and the "Standard Highway Signs" publication. Casing rules vary by sign type: for example, on community wayfinding guide signs, destination legends must use mixed case (lower-case letters with initial upper-case letters), while all other word messages on those signs must be in all upper-case letters. Standards (mandatory): - "Except as otherwise provided in this Manual, all word messages shall be aligned horizontally across a sign, reading left to right." - "Except as provided in Section 2A.04, all word messages shall use standard wording as shown in this Manual and in the 'Standard Highway Signs' publication (see Section 1A.05)." - "The lettering for destinations on community wayfinding guide signs shall be a combination of lower-case letters with initial upper-case letters (see Section 2D.04). All other word messages on community wayfinding guide signs shall be in all upper-case letters." - "Standardization of these designs does not preclude further improvement by minor modifications to the orientation of symbols (see Section 2A.09), width of borders, or layout of word messages, but all shapes and colors shall be as indicated." Guidance (recommended): - "If a pavement marking word message consists of more than one line of information, it should read in the direction of travel. The first word of the message should be nearest to the road user." (a distinct orientation rule for pavement markings, in contrast to signs) - "Word messages should be as brief as practical and the lettering should be large enough to provide the necessary legibility distance." Visual evidence: [Image 1] Table 2L-3 shows changeable message sign examples composed entirely in upper-case lettering, with a note that these examples are for single-color CMS using all upper-case lettering, while full-matrix CMS with fine pixel spacing should use upper- and lower-case lettering where appropriate. [Image 2] Figure 9D-5 illustrates guide signing on a shared-use path where destination lettering follows the mixed-case convention (lower-case letters with initial upper-case letters) while other word messages are in all upper-case letters. Citations: - Section 2A.08 Standard §1 (p.45) - Section 2A.08 Standard §2 (p.45) - Section 2D.55 Standard §32 (p.271) - Section 2A.04 Standard §8 (p.40) - Section 3B.21 Guidance §2 (p.575) - Section 2D.55 Guidance §28 (p.271) - Table 2L-3 (p.517) - Figure 9D-5 (p.1082)

**Gold answer:** Word messages generally read horizontally from left to right. Place, street, and highway names use initial upper-case letters with lower-case letters; other legends use upper-case letters unless another MUTCD provision specifies otherwise.

Visual evidence: - [Image 1] Figure 2A-1 (p.48) shows Example A, a W16-15P NEW plaque mounted above a No Left Turn regulatory sign as an enhanced-conspicuity treatment when the regulation or condition is new. - [Image 2] Figure 2C-16 (p.195) depicts the NEW (W16-15P) plaque among the supplemental warning plaques, illustrating its standard design (black legend on yellow background). **Gold answer:** The NEW plaque should remain only for a period determined by engineering judgment and no more than 12 months. Strobe lights may not be used to enhance highway-sign conspicuity.

The response identifies: Section 2A.15, Section 6F.02, Section 8B.04, Figure 2A-2, Figure 6F-1, Figure 2M-3. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: The minimum is 5 feet in rural areas and 7 feet in business, commercial, or residential areas where such activity or obstruction is likely, measured as specified from the near pav… Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=0.850 and Context Precision@6=0.333. The visual-routing decision did not match the locked modality requirement for this question. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: For signs installed at the side of the road in rural areas, the minimum mounting height from the bottom of the sign to the elevation of the near edge of the pavement is 5 feet. In business, commercial, or residential areas where parking, bicyclist, or pedestrian movements are likely to occur (or where the sign's view might be obstructed), the minimum height is 7 feet, measured to the top of the curb or, without a curb, to the elevation of the near edge of the traveled way. The same 5-foot/7-foot minimums apply in temporary traffic control zones and to YIELD or STOP signs at passive grade crossings. Standards (mandatory): - "In rural areas, the minimum height, measured vertically from the bottom of the sign to the elevation of the near edge of the pavement, of signs installed at the side of the road shall be 5 feet (see Figure 2A-2)." - "In business, commercial, or residential areas where parking, bicyclist, or pedestrian movements are likely to occur, or where the view of the sign might be obstructed, the minimum height, measured vertically from the bottom of the sign to the top of the curb, or in the absence of curb, measured vertically from the bottom of the sign to the elevation of the near edge of the traveled way, of signs installed at the side of the road shall be 7 feet (see Figure 2A-2)." - For temporary traffic control: "The minimum height, measured vertically from the bottom of the sign to the elevation of the near edge of the pavement, of signs installed at the side of the road in rural areas shall be 5 feet (see Figure 6F-1)," and in business, commercial, or residential areas where parking or pedestrian movements are likely to occur, or where the view of the sign might be obstructed, the minimum height "shall be 7 feet (see Figure 6F-1)." - At passive grade crossings, when a Crossbuck Assembly is on a new support or a YIELD/STOP sign is on a separate support, the mounting height "shall be at least 5 feet in rural areas and shall be at least 7 feet in areas where parking or pedestrian movements are likely to occur." Guidance (recommended): - Except as otherwise provided, signs mounted on portable sign supports that do not meet the minimum mounting heights provided in Part 2 should not be used for a duration of more than 3 days. Visual evidence: - [Image 1] Figure 2A-2 illustrates a rural roadside sign (STOP) with "5 ft MIN." to the bottom of the sign and a roadside sign in a business, commercial, or residential area (Speed Limit) with "7 ft MIN." where parking or pedestrian movements are likely to occur. - [Image 2] Shows Figure 6F-1 with typical temporary traffic control installations: "5 ft MIN." in rural areas (diagrams A and B) and "7 ft MIN." in business, commercial, or residential areas with and without curb (diagrams C and D). - [Image 3] Provides the full-page view of Figure 2A-2, confirming the 5 ft minimum for rural roadside signs and 7 ft minimum for urban/business area signs, along with lateral offset dimensions. - [Image 4] Shows the text of Section 2A.15 Mounting Height, including the Standard provisions establishing the 5-foot rural and 7-foot business/commercial/residential minimums. - [Image 5] Shows Figure 2M-3 for recreational and cultural interest area signs, which applies the same 7 ft minimum in business, commercial, or residential areas and 5 ft minimum in rural areas. Citations: - Section 2A.15 Standard §4 (p.58) - Section 2A.15 Standard §5 (p.58) - Section 6F.02 Standard §4 (p.790) - Section 6F.02 Standard §5 (p.790) - Section 8B.04 Standard §12 (p.1000) - Section 6F.02 Guidance §11 (p.791) - Figure 2A-2 (p.51)

**Gold answer:** The minimum is 5 feet in rural areas and 7 feet in business, commercial, or residential areas where such activity or obstruction is likely, measured as specified from the near pavement edge or curb.

The locked benchmark marks the question as answerable, so the response needed to state and support the required MUTCD answer. Correctness rationale: The response states the locked conclusion, rule, value, or interpretation correctly. Completeness rationale: The audit accepted the response as covering all required answer elements. Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 2A.15, Section 2G.08, Section 2G.10, Section 2A.16, Section 2G.03, Figure 2A-2. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: At least 17 feet, except where nearby structures or special constrained structures qualify for the specific exceptions in Section 2A.15. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 3, which produced MRR=0.333. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=0.525 and Context Precision@6=0.333. The visual-routing decision did not match the locked modality requirement for this question. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: Overhead signs must provide a minimum vertical clearance of 17 feet to the sign, light fixture, or sign bridge over the entire width of the pavement and shoulders, unless the mounting structure or nearby structures have a lesser vertical clearance. Reduced clearances are permitted in limited circumstances, such as near lower structures or in tunnels, and the 17-foot dimension is also applied to certain median-barrier-mounted preferential lane signs that overhang the roadway. Standards (mandatory): - "Overhead signs shall provide a vertical clearance of not less than 17 feet to the sign, light fixture, or sign bridge over the entire width of the pavement and shoulders, except where the structure on which the overhead signs are to be mounted or other structures along the roadway near the sign structure have a lesser vertical clearance." (shown on the Section 2A.15 page, [Image 2]) - "Signs that are post-mounted on a median barrier that overhang any portion of the traveled way shall be mounted with a vertical clearance that complies with that of overhead signs." ([Image 2]) - For the associated supports: "For overhead sign supports, the minimum lateral offset from the edge of the shoulder (or if no shoulder exists, from the edge of the pavement) to the near edge of overhead sign supports (cantilever or sign bridges) shall be 6 feet. Overhead sign supports shall have a barrier or crash cushion to shield them if they are within the clear zone." Options (permitted): - If the vertical clearance of other structures along the roadway near the sign structure is less than 16 feet, the vertical clearance to an overhead sign structure or support may be as low as 1 foot higher than the vertical clearance of the other structures to improve sign visibility ([Image 2]). - In special cases the clearance to overhead signs may be reduced if necessary because of substandard dimensions in tunnels and other major structures such as double-deck bridges ([Image 2]). - Where lateral clearance is limited, preferential lane regulatory signs post-mounted on a median barrier (72 inches or less in width) may be skewed up to 45 degrees or mounted higher, such that the vertical clearance to the bottom of the sign, light fixture, or structural support, whichever is lowest, is not less than 17 feet above any portion of the pavement and shoulders. - The same 17-foot minimum applies where warning signs (Section 2G.08) or preferential lane guide signs (Section 2G.10) on median barriers are mounted higher due to limited lateral clearance. Visual evidence: - [Image 1] Figure 2A-2, Panel H ("Overhead sign"), depicts an overhead street name sign with a "17 ft MIN." vertical clearance to the pavement and a 6 ft minimum lateral offset from the shoulder to the support. - [Image 2] shows the Section 2A.15 Standard text requiring not less than 17 feet of vertical clearance over the entire width of the pavement and shoulders, plus the Options for reduced clearance near lower structures and in tunnels. - [Image 3] and [Image 4] (Figure 2D-10, Sheets 1 and 2) illustrate examples of overhead guide signs mounted on sign bridges at an intersection but do not state clearance dimensions. - [Image 5] (Sections 2E.19–2E.20) notes that vertical clearance of overhead signs is discussed in Section 2A.15 and establishes the 6-foot lateral offset for overhead sign supports on freeways and expressways. Citations: - Section 2A.16 Standard §1 (p.59) - Section 2G.03 Option §14 (p.411) - Section 2G.08 Option §4 (p.419) - Section 2G.10 Option §18 (p.422) - Figure 2A-2 (p.51) - Page 100 - Page 344

**Gold answer:** At least 17 feet, except where nearby structures or special constrained structures qualify for the specific exceptions in Section 2A.15.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: Regulatory signs must be installed at or near where the regulations they convey apply, and they must clearly indicate the requirements and be designed and installed for adequate visibility and legibility to obtain compliance. For nighttime visibility, regulatory signs must be either retroreflective or illuminated (per Section 2A.21). Special contexts (preferential lanes, toll plazas, ramp meters) add placement provisions. Standards (mandatory): - "Regulatory signs shall be used to inform road users of selected traffic laws or regulations and to indicate the applicability of the legal requirements." - "Regulatory signs shall be installed at or near where the regulations apply. The signs shall clearly indicate the requirements imposed by the regulations and shall be designed and installed to provide adequate visibility and legibility in order to obtain compliance." - "Regulatory signs shall be retroreflective or illuminated (see Section 2A.21)." - "If used, overhead Preferential Lane (R3-13 series, R3-14 series, and R3-15 series) regulatory signs shall be installed on the side of the roadway where the entrance to the preferential lane is located and any appropriate adjustments shall be made to the sign message." Guidance (recommended): - Regulatory signs (or regulatory panels within guide signs) indicating restrictions on vehicle type and forms of toll payment at a specific toll plaza lane should be installed over the applicable lane, either on the toll plaza canopy or on a separate structure immediately in advance of the canopy, located so each sign is clearly related to an individual toll lane. Options (permitted): - Where ramp control signals are used to meter traffic on a freeway or expressway entrance ramp, regulatory signs with legends appropriate to the control may be installed adjacent to the ramp control signal faces. Visual evidence: [Image 7] shows the full text of Section 2B.01, including the Standard provisions that regulatory signs shall be installed at or near where regulations apply and shall be retroreflective or illuminated. [Image 3] (Table 2A-4) lists means of retroreflection and illumination of sign elements (e.g., prismatic reflector buttons, LEDs, reflective materials), supporting how the retroreflectivity/illumination requirement can be met. [Image 2] (Figure 2A-1) illustrates conspicuity enhancements for signs, including retroreflective strips on sign supports and LEDs in a sign border, which relate to visibility of regulatory signs such as the STOP sign. [Image 4] (Figure 2B-3) shows examples of regulatory Speed Limit signs and plaques that are subject to these installation and visibility requirements. Citations: - Section 2B.01 Standard §1 (p.67) - Section 2B.01 Standard §2 (p.67) - Section 2B.01 Standard §3 (p.67) - Section 2G.03 Standard §10 (p.409) - Section 2F.04 Guidance §3 (p.383) - Section 2B.61 Option §1 (p.135) - Page 108 - Table 2A-4 (p.62) - Figure 2A-1 (p.48) - Figure 2B-3 (p.86)

**Gold answer:** They must be installed at or near the location where the regulation applies, clearly state the requirement, and provide adequate visibility and legibility. They must be retroreflective or illuminated.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: At an all-way stop intersection, an ALL-WAY (R1-3P) supplemental plaque must be mounted below each STOP sign, and it must have a white legend and border on a red background. Plaques with numbered legends such as 2-WAY, 3-WAY, or 4-WAY are expressly prohibited with STOP signs. Standards (mandatory): - "At intersections where all approaches are controlled by STOP signs (see Section 2B.12), an ALL-WAY (R1-3P) supplemental plaque (see Figure 2B-1) shall be mounted below each STOP sign. The ALL-WAY plaque shall have a white legend and border on a red background." - "Supplemental plaques with legends such as 2-WAY, 3-WAY, 4-WAY, or other numbers of ways shall not be used with STOP signs." Guidance (recommended): - The TRAFFIC FROM LEFT (RIGHT) DOES NOT STOP (W4-4aP) plaque or ONCOMING TRAFFIC DOES NOT STOP (W4-4bP) plaque should be used at intersections where STOP signs control all but one approach, unless the only non-stopped approach is from a one-way street. Options (permitted): - The CROSS TRAFFIC DOES NOT STOP (W4-4P) plaque may be used with a STOP sign when engineering judgment indicates conditions could cause road users to misinterpret the intersection as an all-way stop. - The W4-4aP or W4-4bP plaque may be used when such messages more accurately describe the traffic controls established at the intersection. Visual evidence: [Image 1] shows Figure 2B-1, which depicts the required ALL WAY (R1-3P) plaque with its white legend on a red background alongside the STOP (R1-1) sign and other related plaques. [Image 3] is the full page containing Section 2B.04, showing the Standard text requiring the ALL-WAY plaque and prohibiting numbered-way plaques, together with Figure 2B-1. **Gold answer:** An ALL-WAY (R1-3P) plaque must be mounted below every STOP sign. Numeric-way plaques such as 2-WAY, 3-WAY, or 4-WAY are not permitted with STOP signs.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: A YIELD sign must be used at the entrance to a roundabout to require entering road users to yield the right-of-way to traffic already circulating. YIELD signs at roundabouts must control only the approach roadways — they are expressly prohibited from being used to control the circulatory roadway itself. Standards (mandatory): - "A YIELD sign shall be used to require road users to yield the right-of-way to other traffic at the entrance to a roundabout. YIELD signs at roundabouts shall be used to control the approach roadways and shall not be used to control the circulatory roadway." Guidance (recommended): - On an approach to a circular intersection controlled by a YIELD sign (and at uncontrolled exits), crosswalks should be a minimum of 20 feet from the edge of the circulatory roadway. Options (permitted): - YIELD (word) and YIELD AHEAD (symbol or word) pavement markings may be used on approaches to roundabouts. - At roundabouts where ONE WAY signs have been installed in the central island, Roundabout Circulation (R6-5P) plaques may be placed below the YIELD signs on approaches to supplement the central island signs. - The Vehicular Traffic Warning sign assembly may be installed on an approach to a circular intersection controlled by a YIELD sign where the crosswalk is at least 20 feet in advance of the yield point at the entrance to the circulatory roadway. - The Non-Vehicular Warning sign assembly may likewise be installed on a YIELD-controlled approach where the crosswalk is at least 20 feet in advance of the yield point at the entrance to the circulatory roadway. Visual evidence: - [Image 1] Figure 3D-1 shows YIELD word markings and yield lines placed on the approach roadways at each roundabout entrance (not on the circulatory roadway), with crosswalks dimensioned at a 20 ft minimum from the circulatory roadway edge. - [Image 2] This page presents Sections 3D.04–3D.07, confirming as an Option that YIELD and YIELD AHEAD pavement markings may be used on roundabout approaches. - [Image 3] This page contains Section 2B.51, which permits Roundabout Circulation plaques below the YIELD signs on approaches where ONE WAY signs are installed in the central island. - [Image 4] This page contains Section 2B.10, including the Standard that YIELD signs shall control roundabout approach roadways and shall not control the circulatory roadway. - [Image 5] This page contains Section 3C.09, giving the guidance that crosswalks on YIELD-controlled approaches should be at least 20 feet from the edge of the circulatory roadway. **Gold answer:** A YIELD sign must control each entering approach roadway. It must not be used to control the circulatory roadway.

Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: For the same 8 hours of a typical day, the combined entering volume must be at least 300 units per hour on the major-street approaches and at least 200 units per hour on the minor… | If the major-street 85th-percentile speed exceeds 40 mph, the minimums may be reduced to 70 percent of those values. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=1.000 and Context Precision@6=0.167. The visual-routing decision did not match the locked modality requirement for this question. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: All-Way Stop Control Warrant D (8-Hour Volume) uses two thresholds: the combined motor vehicle, bicycle, and pedestrian volume entering from the major-street approaches must be at least 300 units per hour, and the combined volume entering from the minor-street approaches must be at least 200 units per hour, for each of any 8 hours of a typical day (the same 8 hours for both). When the 85th-percentile approach speed on the major street exceeds 40 mph, these minimum vehicular volume values may be reduced to 70 percent (i.e., approximately 210 and 140 units per hour, respectively). Options (permitted): - All-way stop control may be installed where an engineering study indicates: "The combined motor vehicle, bicycle, and pedestrian volume entering the intersection from the major-street approaches is at least 300 units per hour for each of any 8 hours of a typical day," and "The combined motor vehicle, bicycle, and pedestrian volume entering the intersection from the minor-street approaches is at least 200 units per hour for each of any of the same 8 hours." - "If the 85th-percentile approach speed of the major-street traffic exceeds 40 mph, the minimum vehicular volume warrants may be reduced to 70 percent of the values given in Items A and B in Paragraph 1 of this Section." Visual evidence: - [Image 5] shows Section 2B.16, All-Way Stop Control Warrant D, including the 300 units per hour major-street and 200 units per hour minor-street thresholds in Paragraph 1 and the 70 percent reduction option in Paragraph 2. Citations: - Section 2B.16 Option §2 (p.79) - Page 120

**Gold answer:** For the same 8 hours of a typical day, the combined entering volume must be at least 300 units per hour on the major-street approaches and at least 200 units per hour on the minor-street approaches. If the major-street 85th-percentile speed exceeds 40 mph, the minimums may be reduced to 70 percent of those values.

Guidance (recommended): - Per the Guidance visible on the page containing Section 2B.21 [Image 3]: "An advisory speed plaque (see Section 2C.59) mounted below a warning sign should be used to warn road users of an advisory speed for a roadway condition. Options (permitted): - An Advisory Speed plaque may be used with the PAVEMENT ENDS (W8-3) sign when the change in roadway condition requires a reduced speed. - An Advisory Exit Speed or Advisory Ramp Speed sign may be used based on engineering judgment when the difference between the mainline speed limit and the exit or ramp advisory speed in the vicinity of the departure is 10 mph or less. - A Speed Limit Sign Beacon may be used with a fixed or variable Speed Limit sign, and a flashing beacon (with an appropriate accompanying sign) may indicate that the displayed speed limit is in effect. Visual evidence: [Image 1] Figure 2B-3 shows the R2-1 Speed Limit sign ("SPEED LIMIT 50") and related speed limit signs and plaques, all displaying speeds in multiples of 5 mph. [Image 2] shows Figure 2C-2 with W13-1P advisory speed plaques (35 MPH) mounted below curve warning signs, illustrating the proper device for advisory speeds. [Image 3] shows the Section 2B.21 text, including the Standard that the R2-1 sign shall display the established limit in multiples of 5 mph and the Guidance that a Speed Limit sign should not be used to warn of an advisory speed. [Image 4] is the full page of Figure 2B-3, again showing R2-1 and related signs with 5-mph-multiple speeds. [Image 5] shows enhanced conspicuity examples including a Speed Limit 35 sign with flags and an "EXIT 25 MPH" advisory sign, illustrating the distinction between regulatory and advisory speed displays. Citations: - Section 2B.21 Standard §13 (p.85) - Section 2G.26 Standard §3 (p.457) - Section 2C.28 Option §2 (p.173) - Section 2C.12 Option §5 (p.160) - Section 4S.04 Option §3 (p.753) - Figure 2B-3 (p.86) - Page 126 - Page 127

**Gold answer:** It must display the speed limit established by law, ordinance, regulation, or an authorized agency based on an engineering study, in multiples of 5 mph. It should not be used for an advisory speed; an advisory speed plaque below an appropriate warning sign should be used instead.

## T027

**Question:** How should the advance placement of warning signs be determined?

**Grade:** C=1.00, Comp=1.00, F=0.95, Cit=1.00, Strict full credit=No

**Descriptive mean:** 98.8% (Strong partial credit)

**Evaluation confidence:** 76%

**Rationale:** The response received 98.8% as the descriptive mean of the four official item scores. The official rubric did not award strict full credit because at least one dimension scored below 1.00. The locked benchmark marks the question as answerable, so the response needed to state and support the required MUTCD answer. Correctness rationale: The response states the locked conclusion, rule, value, or interpretation correctly. Completeness rationale: The audit accepted the response as covering all required answer elements. Faithfulness rationale: The audit records an unsupported, overstated, or source-inconsistent element. The package retains the official deduction without inventing a new source citation. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 2C.04, Section 6H.02, Section 6B.04, Table 2C-3, Table 6B-1, Figure 2C-2. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: Warning signs should be placed to provide adequate perception-response time. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 4, which produced MRR=0.250. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=0.501 and Context Precision@6=0.333. The visual-routing decision did not match the locked modality requirement for this question. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: Advance placement of warning signs should be determined by providing drivers adequate Perception-Response Time (PRT), using Table 2C-3 distances (based on posted or 85th-percentile speed and the condition being warned of) applied with engineering judgment. In temporary traffic control (TTC) zones, signs should be placed at varying distances depending on roadway type, condition, and posted speed, using Table 6B-1 — roughly 100 feet on low-speed urban streets, 4 to 8 times the speed limit on urban streets, 8 to 12 times the speed limit on rural highways, and ½ mile or more on freeways and expressways. Placement effectiveness should also be periodically evaluated under both day and night conditions. Standards (mandatory): - No Standard provisions appear in the evidence for this question. Guidance (recommended): - The effectiveness of the placement of warning signs should be periodically evaluated under both day and night conditions; Table 2C-3 provides guidelines for advance placement distances keyed to posted or 85th-percentile speed and Condition A (speed reduction and lane changing in heavy traffic) or Condition B (deceleration to a listed advisory speed). - Table 2C-3 distances range from 225 ft (Condition A at 20 mph) up to 1,600 ft (at 85 mph); for warning signs with less than 6-inch legend or more than four words, a minimum of 100 feet should be added to the advance placement distance to provide adequate legibility. - In TTC zones, where highway conditions permit, warning signs should be placed in advance of the transition and activity areas at varying distances depending on roadway type, condition, and posted speed (see Table 6B-1); where a series of two or more advance warning signs is used, the closest sign should be placed approximately 100 feet for low-speed urban streets to 1,000 feet or more for freeways and expressways. - On urban streets, the effective placement of the nearest warning sign to the TTC zone, in feet, should range from 4 to 8 times the speed limit in mph, with the high end used when speeds are relatively high; with two or more signs on higher-speed streets, the advance warning area should extend a greater distance. - On rural highways, the effective placement of the first warning sign should be substantially longer—from 8 to 12 times the speed limit in mph—and the advance warning area should extend 1,500 feet or more for open highway conditions. - On freeways and expressways, typical distances should be longer because drivers are conditioned to uninterrupted flow; advance warning sign placement should extend as far as ½ mile or more. Visual evidence: - [Image 1] Table 2C-3 shows advance placement distances by posted or 85th-percentile speed (20–85 mph) for Condition A and Condition B advisory speeds, with footnotes on sign legibility adjustments and minimum 100-ft spacing between signs. - [Image 2] Table 6B-1 (Warning Sign Minimum Spacing) lists distances between signs (A, B, C dimensions) by road type: 100 feet for low-speed urban, 350 feet for high-speed urban, 500 feet for rural, and 1,000/1,500/2,640 feet for expressways/freeways. - [Image 3] Page 193 shows Section 2C.04 (Placement of Warning Signs), including the Guidance that distances in Table 2C-3 should be applied with engineering judgment, and reproduces Table 2C-3. - [Image 4] Page 839 shows Section 6H.02 (Position of Advance Warning Signs) with Guidance on placing warning signs in advance of transition and activity areas per Table 6B-1. - [Image 5] Page 197 shows Figure 2C-2, an example of horizontal alignment warning sign placement, noting that Table 2C-3 should be consulted for advance placement distance guidelines. - [Image 6] Page 812 shows Section 6B.04 (Advance Warning Area) with the 4–8 times (urban) and 8–12 times (rural) speed-limit placement guidance and the ½-mile-or-more freeway/expressway guidance. Citations: - Section 2C.04 Guidance §5 (p.152) - Section 2C.04 Guidance §804 (p.153) - Section 6H.02 Guidance §1 (p.798) - Section 6B.04 Guidance §3 (p.771) - Section 6B.04 Guidance §4 (p.771) - Section 6B.04 Guidance §6 (p.771) - Table 2C-3 (p.152) - Table 6B-1 (p.773) - Page 193 - Page 839 - Page 197 - Page 812

**Gold answer:** Warning signs should be placed to provide adequate perception-response time. Table 2C-3 is an aid, but its distances should be applied with engineering judgment and can be adjusted for roadway features, other signing, and visibility.

Standards (mandatory): - "The Chevron Alignment sign shall be a vertical rectangle. No border shall be used on the Chevron Alignment sign." - "If used, Chevron Alignment signs shall be installed on the outside of a turn or curve, in line with and at approximately a right angle to approaching traffic. Chevron Alignment signs shall be installed at a minimum height of 4 feet, measured vertically from the bottom of the sign to the elevation of the near edge of the traveled way." - "The use of the Chevron Alignment (W1-8) sign (see Figures 2C-1 and 2C-2) to provide additional emphasis and guidance for a change in horizontal alignment shall be in accordance with the information shown in Table 2C-4." Guidance (recommended): - The Chevron Alignment signs should be visible for a sufficient distance to provide the road user with adequate time to react to the change in alignment. - When the Hairpin Curve sign or the 270-degree Loop sign is installed, either a One-Direction Large Arrow (W1-6) sign or Chevron Alignment (W1-8) signs should be installed on the outside of the turn or curve. Options (permitted): - Chevron Alignment signs may be used instead of or in addition to standard delineators. Visual evidence: [Image 1] shows Table 2C-5, which gives typical Chevron Alignment sign spacing on horizontal curves by advisory speed and curve radius (e.g., 40 feet for 15 mph or less, up to 200 feet for more than 60 mph). [Image 2] shows the full text of Section 2C.08 on page 158, including the Standard provisions on shape, border, placement, orientation, and 4-foot minimum mounting height. [Image 3] shows Figure 3G-1 (delineator placement on a curve), noting that Chevron Alignment signs (W1-8) may be used instead of or in addition to standard delineators per Section 2C.08. [Image 5] shows the table of contents entry locating Section 2C.08, Chevron Alignment Sign (W1-8), on page 158. **Gold answer:** They must be vertical rectangles with no border, installed on the outside of the turn or curve, aligned approximately at a right angle to approaching traffic, and mounted at least 4 feet above the near edge of the traveled way.

**Rationale:** The response received full credit because the audit accepted the substantive answer, coverage of required elements, grounding, and evidence localization without a deduction. The locked benchmark marks the question as answerable, so the response needed to state and support the required MUTCD answer. Correctness rationale: The response states the locked conclusion, rule, value, or interpretation correctly. Completeness rationale: The audit accepted the response as covering all required answer elements. Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 2L.05, Table 2L-2, p.514, p.516, p.515. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: A unit of information is one answer to one question that a driver can use to make a decision. | It should contain no more than four words Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=0.990 and Context Precision@6=0.833. The visual-routing decision did not match the locked modality requirement for this question. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: A unit of information is a single answer to a single question that a driver can use to make a decision (e.g., "What happened?" → "MAJOR CRASH"). Per MUTCD Guidance, a unit of information should contain no more than four words. This is a recommended (Guidance) provision, not a mandatory Standard. Guidance (recommended): - "A unit of information, which is a single answer to a single question that a driver can use to make a decision, should not be more than four words." - Related principles for CMS messages: "No more than three units of information should be displayed in a message phase"; "No more than four units of information should be in a message when the traffic operating speeds are 35 mph or more"; "No more than five units of information should be in a message when the traffic operating speeds are less than 35 mph"; and "Only one unit of information should appear on each line of the CMS." Options (permitted): - "A unit of information consisting of more than one word may be displayed on more than one line. An additional CMS at a downstream location may be used for the purpose of allowing the entire message to be read twice." Visual evidence: [Image 1] Table 2L-2 illustrates the concept with a four-unit example message, pairing driver questions with answers — "What happened?" (MAJOR CRASH), "Where?" (AT EXIT 12), "Who is the advisory for?" (Drivers heading TO NEW YORK), and "What is advised?" (USE ROUTE 46) — each counting as one unit of information, and shows how these can be arranged into a two-phase message. Images 2–5 (pages 551, 16, 85, and 557) do not contain material relevant to defining a unit of information. Citations: - Section 2L.05 Guidance §1 (p.514) - Section 2L.05 Guidance §7 (p.516) - Section 2L.05 Support §2 (p.515) - Section 2L.05 Option §9 (p.516) - Table 2L-2 (p.515)

**Gold answer:** A unit of information is one answer to one question that a driver can use to make a decision. It should contain no more than four words; total message length should be governed by the number of information units and the size of the CMS.

## T031

**Question:** What directional relationship do yellow center line markings and white lane line markings communicate?

**Grade:** C=1.00, Comp=1.00, F=1.00, Cit=1.00, Strict full credit=Yes

**Descriptive mean:** 100.0% (Full credit)

**Evaluation confidence:** 88%

**Rationale:** The response received full credit because the audit accepted the substantive answer, coverage of required elements, grounding, and evidence localization without a deduction. The locked benchmark marks the question as answerable, so the response needed to state and support the required MUTCD answer. Correctness rationale: The response states the locked conclusion, rule, value, or interpretation correctly. Completeness rationale: The audit accepted the response as covering all required answer elements. Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 3B.04, Section 3B.05, Section 3B.01, Section 3B.06, Section 9E.13, Figure 3B-1. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: Yellow center lines separate lanes traveling in opposite directions. | White lane lines separate lanes traveling in the same direction. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=0.951 and Context Precision@6=0.833. The visual-routing decision did not match the locked modality requirement for this question. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: Yellow center line markings communicate the separation of traffic lanes moving in **opposite** directions of travel, while white lane line markings communicate the separation of traffic lanes moving in the **same** direction of travel. In other words, seeing yellow on your left means opposing traffic is on the other side; seeing white means adjacent traffic flows the same way you do. The pattern of the yellow line (broken vs. solid) further communicates whether passing is permitted or prohibited. Standards (mandatory): - "Center line pavement markings, when used, shall be the pavement markings used to delineate the separation of traffic lanes that have opposite directions of travel on a roadway and shall be yellow." - "When used, lane line pavement markings delineating the separation of traffic lanes that have the same direction of travel shall be white." - "The center line markings on two-lane, two-way roadways shall be one of the following as shown in Figure 3B-1: A. Two-direction passing zone markings consisting of a normal width broken yellow line where crossing the center line markings for passing with care is permitted for traffic traveling in either direction; B. One-direction no-passing zone markings consisting of a double yellow line, one of which is a normal width broken yellow line and the other is a normal width solid yellow line, where crossing the center line markings for passing with care is permitted for the traffic traveling adjacent to the broken line, but is prohibited for traffic traveling adjacent to the solid line; or C. Two-direction no-passing zone markings consisting of two normal width solid yellow lines where crossing the center line markings for passing is prohibited for traffic traveling in either direction." - "Except where a reversible lane (see Section 3B.04) or a two-way left-turn lane (see Section 3B.05) is present, the center line markings on undivided two-way roadways with four or more lanes for moving motor vehicle traffic always available shall be the two-direction no-passing zone markings consisting of normal width double solid yellow lines as shown in Figure 3B-2." Options (permitted): - On shared-use paths wide enough for two lanes, a solid yellow center line may be used to separate the two directions of travel where passing or traveling to the left of the line is not permitted, and a broken yellow center line may be used where passing is permitted. - Solid white lane line markings may be used: (A) on approaches to intersections to separate contiguous through lanes, (B) to separate through traffic lanes from auxiliary lanes such as an added uphill truck lane, and (C) on approaches to crosswalks across multi-lane roadways. Visual evidence: [Image 1] shows a multi-lane, two-way roadway with double solid yellow lines separating the two opposing directions (arrows pointing opposite ways on either side of the yellow) and broken white lane lines separating same-direction lanes. [Image 2] shows three-lane, two-way applications where the yellow center line (broken or solid) separates opposing traffic and a white lane line separates the two same-direction lanes. [Image 3] illustrates changing the direction of a center lane, with yellow two-direction no-passing markings delineating the boundary between opposing flows. [Image 4] (Figure 3B-1) shows two-lane, two-way roadways where broken and solid yellow center lines communicate both direction separation and passing permissions. [Image 5] shows the same four-or-more-lane, two-way applications with yellow center lines between opposing arrows and white lines between same-direction lanes. [Image 6] shows the three-lane, two-way applications with the text of Sections 3B.02 and 3B.03. [Image 7] shows a freeway lane drop where all lanes travel the same direction and are separated by white lane lines, with yellow only on the left edge. [Image 8] shows a two-way street where tubular markers supplement the yellow center line dividing opposing traffic and white lane lines dividing same-direction lanes. Citations: - Section 3B.01 Standard §1 (p.540) - Section 3B.06 Standard §1 (p.546) - Section 3B.01 Standard §4 (p.540) - Section 3B.01 Standard §6 (p.540) - Section 9E.13 Option §1 (p.1106) - Section 3B.06 Option §8 (p.548) - Figure 3B-1 (p.540) - Figure 3B-2 (p.541) - Figure 3B-3 (p.542) - Figure 3B-5 (p.545)

**Gold answer:** Yellow center lines separate lanes traveling in opposite directions. White lane lines separate lanes traveling in the same direction.

**Model answer:** # Answer Direct Answer: On roadways with center line markings, no-passing-zone markings are mandatory at horizontal or vertical curves wherever the passing sight distance falls below the minimum in Table 3B-1 for the 85th-percentile speed or the speed limit (e.g., 450 feet at 25 mph up to 1,200 feet at 70 mph). When successive no-passing zones are separated by less than 400 feet, the no-passing zone markings should be connected to join the zones. Standards (mandatory): - "On roadways with center line markings, no-passing zone markings shall be used at horizontal or vertical curves where the passing sight distance is less than the minimum shown in Table 3B-1 for the 85th-percentile speed or the speed limit." - "On two-way, two-lane or three-lane roadways where center line markings are installed, no-passing zones shall be established at vertical and horizontal curves and other locations where an engineering study indicates that passing must be prohibited because of inadequate sight distances or other special conditions." - "No-passing zones shall be marked by either the one-direction no-passing zone pavement markings or the two-direction no-passing zone pavement markings described in Section 3B.01 and shown in Figures 3B-1 and 3B-3." - "No-passing zone markings shall be used on: A. Two-way roadways at lane-reduction transitions (see Section 3B.12), B. Approaches to obstructions that must be passed on the right (see Section 3B.13), C. Approaches to grade crossings (see Section 8C.02), and D. Approaches to crosswalks." Guidance (recommended): - "Where the distance between successive no-passing zones is less than 400 feet, no-passing zone markings should connect the zones." Visual evidence: - [Image 1] Figure 3B-4 illustrates the method of locating no-passing zone limits at vertical and horizontal curves, with the zone beginning at point "a" where sight distance (measured between points 3.5 feet above the pavement) first becomes less than the minimum and ending at point "b" where sight distance again exceeds the minimum. - [Image 2] Table 3B-1 lists the minimum passing sight distances that trigger no-passing zone markings, ranging from 450 feet at 25 mph to 1,200 feet at 70 mph. - [Image 3] Page 584 shows the same table and explains that the upstream end of a no-passing zone (point "a") is where sight distance first drops below the Table 3B-1 value and the downstream end (point "b") is where it again exceeds the minimum, along with the 400-foot connection guidance. - [Image 4] Page 585 (Figure 3B-4) shows profile and plan views of no-passing zones at vertical and horizontal curves, noting that zones in opposite directions might or might not overlap depending on alignment. - [Image 5] Page 583 contains Section 3B.03 with the Standard provisions listing where no-passing zone markings shall be used, including at curves where an engineering study indicates passing must be prohibited due to inadequate sight distances. - [Image 6] Page 1051 (Figure 8C-1) shows an example of no-passing zone application on an approach to a grade crossing, including the optional W14-3 NO PASSING ZONE sign. Citations: - Section 3B.03 Standard §4 (p.543) - Section 3B.03 Standard §3 (p.543) - Section 3B.03 Standard §2 (p.542) - Section 3B.03 Standard §1 (p.542) - Section 3B.03 Guidance §7 (p.543) - Section 2B.36 Support §3 (p.102) - Figure 3B-4 (p.544) - Table 3B-1 (p.543) - Page 584 - Page 585 - Page 583 - Page 1051

**Gold answer:** They are required on roadways with center line markings where passing sight distance is below the minimum in Table 3B-1 for the relevant speed. If successive no-passing zones are less than 400 feet apart, the markings should connect the zones.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: Crosswalk markings must be white, and transverse lines are limited to a width of not less than 6 inches nor greater than 24 inches. Figure 3C-1 further shows the minimum crosswalk width of 6 feet (8 feet where the posted speed limit is 40 mph or greater at a non-intersection crosswalk), and dimensional ranges for high-visibility designs such as longitudinal bar, ladder, and bar pair crosswalks. Standards (mandatory): - "The transverse lines used to establish the limits of the ladder crosswalk shall not be less than 6 inches or greater than 24 inches in width." - The visible page containing Section 3C.03 also states: "Crosswalk markings shall be white. When used, transverse lines shall not be less than 6 inches or greater than 24 inches in width," and that the minimum width of a marked crosswalk shall be 6 feet, or 8 feet at a non-intersection crosswalk where the posted speed limit is 40 mph or greater (see [Image 2]). Guidance (recommended): - Transverse line crosswalk markings should be limited to locations controlled by traffic control signals or on approaches controlled by STOP or YIELD signs. - Where aesthetic surface treatments are used, a gap of at least one-half the width of the white transverse line, but not less than 6 inches, should separate the white crosswalk lines from the aesthetic treatment (such as unmarked pavement or a black contrast line). Options (permitted): - High-visibility crosswalk markings may be used where additional conspicuity is desired over transverse line crosswalk markings. Visual evidence: - [Image 1] Figure 3C-1 depicts the dimensional limits: transverse lines 6–24 inches wide with a 6 ft minimum crosswalk width; longitudinal bars 12–60 inches long (not to exceed 2.5 times the bar width) and 12–24 inches wide; ladder designs combining both; and bar pairs of 8–12 inch bars, with a note that the minimum crosswalk width shall be 8 feet where the posted speed limit is 40 mph or greater at a non-intersection crosswalk. - [Image 2] shows Section 3C.03 (Design of Crosswalk Markings) with the Standard that crosswalk markings shall be white, transverse lines shall be 6–24 inches wide, and minimum crosswalk widths of 6 feet (8 feet at high-speed non-intersection locations), along with Figure 3C-1. - [Image 3] shows Sections 3C.04 and 3C.05, confirming that the transverse line design consists of two parallel transverse lines and that high-visibility designs (longitudinal bar, ladder, bar pair) may be used for added conspicuity. - [Image 4] shows the table of contents locating Chapter 3C (Crosswalk Markings), Sections 3C.01–3C.12. - [Image 5] shows Section 3H.03 guidance on contrast gaps between white crosswalk lines and aesthetic surface treatments, including the minimum 6-inch gap recommendation. Citations: - Section 3C.07 Standard §4 (p.592) - Section 3C.04 Guidance §1 (p.591) - Section 3H.03 Guidance §11 (p.623) - Section 3C.05 Option §1 (p.591) - Section 3C.01 Support §1 (p.589) - Section 3C.01 Support §2 (p.589) - Figure 3C-1 (p.590) - Page 631 - Page 632 - Page 664

**Gold answer:** Crosswalk markings must be white. Where transverse lines are used, each line must be at least 6 inches and no more than 24 inches wide. Except for certain ramp-terminal cases, the marked crosswalk should be at least 6 feet wide.

The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: No. Under Section 3D.04, a yield line at a roundabout entrance is an Option, not a requirement — it "may be used to indicate the point behind which vehicles are required to yield at the entrance to a roundabout" (see [Image 3]). What is mandatory at roundabout entrances is the YIELD sign itself, per the Standard in Section 2B.10; the pavement markings (yield line, YIELD word marking, dotted edge line extension) are recommended or permitted, not required. Standards (mandatory): - "A YIELD sign shall be used to require road users to yield the right-of-way to other traffic at the entrance to a roundabout. YIELD signs at roundabouts shall be used to control the approach roadways and shall not be used to control the circulatory roadway." - "YIELD signs shall not be placed on all of the approaches to an intersection, except at roundabouts." Guidance (recommended): - Where a white edge line is used for the circulatory roadway, it should be: "A. A solid line adjacent to the splitter island, and B. A wide dotted line across the lane(s) entering the roundabout" (see Figure 3D-1) — this is recommended practice, not a yield line requirement. Options (permitted): - Per Section 3D.04 (Option): "A yield line (see Section 3B.19) may be used to indicate the point behind which vehicles are required to yield at the entrance to a roundabout (see Figures 3D-1 and 3D-2)." (text visible in [Image 3]) - "YIELD (word) (see Figure 3D-1) and YIELD AHEAD (symbol or word) pavement markings may be used on approaches to roundabouts." - "A yellow edge line may be placed around the inner (left-hand) edge of the circulatory roadway (see Figure 3D-1) and may be used to channelize traffic." Visual evidence: - [Image 1] Figure 3D-1 shows roundabout approach markings, with the YIELD word marking and yield line elements labeled "Optional" and the "Wide dotted white extension of circulatory roadway edge line" called out. - [Image 2] Figure 3D-2 illustrates a one-lane roundabout where yield-related markings (yield lines and YIELD word markings) are flagged with the "Optional" asterisk in the legend. - [Image 3] shows the text of Section 3D.04, confirming the yield line appears only under the "Option" heading ("may be used"), not under a Standard. - [Image 4] is the full-page view of Figure 3D-1, again labeling the yield line and YIELD word markings as "Optional." - [Image 5] shows the Chapter 3D text including Section 3D.03, where the wide dotted line across entering lanes is stated as Guidance ("should"). - [Image 6] shows Figure 3D-3 (Sheet 1), where yield line markings on approaches carry the optional asterisk in the legend. Citations: - Section 2B.10 Standard §5 (p.78) - Section 2B.10 Standard §6 (p.78) - Section 3D.03 Guidance §2 (p.595) - Section 3D.05 Option §1 (p.605) - Section 3D.03 Option §4 (p.605) - Figure 3D-1 (p.596) - Figure 3D-2 (p.597) - Page 646

**Gold answer:** No. A yield line may be used to indicate the point behind which vehicles are required to yield at a roundabout entrance; it is an Option, not a universal requirement.

## T035

**Question:** Does satisfying a traffic signal warrant automatically require installation of a traffic control signal?

**Grade:** C=1.00, Comp=0.85, F=1.00, Cit=1.00, Strict full credit=No

**Descriptive mean:** 96.2% (Strong partial credit)

**Evaluation confidence:** 76%

**Rationale:** The response received 96.2% as the descriptive mean of the four official item scores. The official rubric did not award strict full credit because at least one dimension scored below 1.00. The locked benchmark marks the question as answerable, so the response needed to state and support the required MUTCD answer. Correctness rationale: The response states the locked conclusion, rule, value, or interpretation correctly. Completeness rationale: The response omits or underdevelops these locked elements: it is not conclusive justification. | A warrant is a threshold that triggers analysis Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 4B.03, Section 4C.01, Section 2B.12, Section 4C.08, p.651, p.79. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: A signal can also be installed where no warrant is met if an engineering study documents why it is the best solution. | Installation must be based on an engineering study considering safety, operation, alternatives, and the site. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=0.933 and Context Precision@6=0.500. The visual-routing decision did not match the locked modality requirement for this question. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: No. The MUTCD explicitly states that satisfying one or more signal warrants does not by itself require installing a traffic control signal. Warrant satisfaction is only one factor in an engineering study, and agencies should consider alternatives to signalization even where warrants are met. Standards (mandatory): - "The satisfaction of a traffic signal warrant or warrants shall not in itself require the installation of a traffic control signal." - Analogously for stop control: "The satisfaction of an all-way stop control warrant or warrants shall not in itself require the installation of all-way stop control at an unsignalized intersection." Guidance (recommended): - "Since road user delay and the frequency of some types of crashes are sometimes higher under traffic signal control than under STOP sign control, consideration should be given to providing alternatives to traffic control signals even if one or more of the signal warrants (see Chapter 4C) has been satisfied." - Where a signal is installed based on projected (estimated) traffic volumes, except when Warrant 8 is the basis, an engineering study should be done within 1 year of putting the signal into steady (stop-and-go) operation to determine if the signal is justified; if not justified, the signal should be taken out of steady operation or removed. Support (informational): - The safe and efficient movement of all road users is the primary consideration in the engineering study; satisfaction of a warrant is one relevant factor "but it is not intended to be the only factor or even the overriding consideration." Agencies can also install a signal where no warrants are met, but only after an engineering study documents the rationale that a signal is the best solution. - The Crash Experience warrant illustrates that warrants define conditions for *considering* a signal (where crash severity and frequency are the principal reasons to consider installation), not for mandating one. Visual evidence: [Image 8] shows Section 4B.03, Alternatives to Traffic Control Signals, which lists alternative treatments (e.g., warning signs, roundabouts, pedestrian hybrid beacons, speed reduction measures, flashing beacons) that should be considered even when signal warrants are satisfied. Citations: - Section 4C.01 Standard §3 (p.651) - Section 2B.12 Standard §5 (p.79) - Section 4B.03 Guidance §1 (p.649) - Section 4C.01 Guidance §12 (p.652) - Section 4C.01 Support §20 (p.653) - Section 4C.08 Support §1 (p.661) - Page 690

**Gold answer:** No. A warrant is a threshold that triggers analysis; it is not conclusive justification. Installation must be based on an engineering study considering safety, operation, alternatives, and the site. A signal can also be installed where no warrant is met if an engineering study documents why it is the best solution.

**Descriptive mean:** 100.0% (Full credit)

**Evaluation confidence:** 88%

**Rationale:** The response received full credit because the audit accepted the substantive answer, coverage of required elements, grounding, and evidence localization without a deduction. The locked benchmark marks the question as answerable, so the response needed to state and support the required MUTCD answer. Correctness rationale: The response states the locked conclusion, rule, value, or interpretation correctly. Completeness rationale: The audit accepted the response as covering all required answer elements. Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 4A.03, Section 4F.01, Section 4F.02, Section 4F.09, Figure 4E-2, p.644. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: The driver must yield to pedestrians lawfully in an associated crosswalk, vehicles lawfully in the intersection, and for left/U-turns, opposing vehicles close enough to be an imme… | The driver may generally proceed straight, turn right or left, or make a U-turn unless another control modifies the movement. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=1.000 and Context Precision@6=0.167. The visual-routing decision did not match the locked modality requirement for this question. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: A driver facing a steady CIRCULAR GREEN signal indication is permitted to proceed straight through, turn right or left, or make a U-turn, unless the movement is restricted by other traffic control devices such as lane-use signs, turn prohibitions, lane markings, or separate turn signal indications. The driver must yield to pedestrians lawfully within an associated crosswalk and to other vehicles lawfully within the intersection; drivers turning left or making a U-turn to the left must additionally yield to oncoming vehicles that are close enough to constitute an immediate hazard. Standards (mandatory): - "Vehicular traffic facing a CIRCULAR GREEN signal indication is permitted to proceed straight through or turn right or left or make a U-turn movement except as such movement is modified by lane-use signs, turn prohibition signs, lane markings, roadway design, separate turn signal indications, or other traffic control devices." - "Such vehicular traffic, including vehicles turning right or left or making a U-turn movement, shall yield the right-of-way to: (a) Pedestrians lawfully within an associated crosswalk, and (b) Other vehicles lawfully within the intersection." - "In addition, vehicular traffic turning left or making a U-turn movement to the left shall yield the right-of-way to other vehicles approaching from the opposite direction so closely as to constitute an immediate hazard during the time when such turning vehicle is moving across or within the intersection." - Related requirement: "A steady CIRCULAR GREEN signal indication shall be displayed only when it is intended to permit traffic to proceed in any direction that is lawful and practical." Options (permitted): - A steady straight-through GREEN ARROW signal indication may be used instead of a CIRCULAR GREEN signal indication to discourage wrong-way turns on approaches intersecting a one-way street, an interchange exit ramp, approaches with unique geometric design prohibiting turns, or approaches with pre-signals where adjacent lanes are controlled separately. Support (informational): - Turns made on a CIRCULAR GREEN signal indication constitute the "permissive only mode" for both left-turning and right-turning traffic, in which turns are made after yielding to pedestrians, if any, and/or opposing traffic, if any. Visual evidence: [Image 2] Figure 4E-2 shows typical arrangements of signal faces containing the circular green (G) indication in vertical, horizontal, and single-section configurations for movements that do not control turning movements. Citations: - Section 4A.03 Standard §1 (p.644) - Section 4F.01 Standard §3 (p.682) - Section 4F.01 Option §5 (p.682) - Section 4F.02 Support §2 (p.683) - Section 4F.09 Support §2 (p.693) - Figure 4E-2 (p.678)

**Gold answer:** The driver may generally proceed straight, turn right or left, or make a U-turn unless another control modifies the movement. The driver must yield to pedestrians lawfully in an associated crosswalk, vehicles lawfully in the intersection, and for left/U-turns, opposing vehicles close enough to be an immediate hazard.

Correctness rationale: The response gives the main conclusion but loses precision on one or more required details. Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The response provides some evidence localization, but the audit judged it incomplete, imprecise, or partly incorrect. The response cites: Section 4F.17, Section 2C.35, Section 4I.06, Section 4H.11, Section 4N.03, Section 4J.03. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: Its duration must be determined using engineering practices, with guidance of 3 to 6 seconds. | The yellow change interval warns that permission to proceed or a protected movement is ending. | Pre-yellow displays such as vehicular countdowns or flashing green indications are prohibited, except for permitted advance-warning beacons. Adjudication note: Correct function, engineering basis, and duration guidance; omits the prohibition on pre-yellow displays. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=1.000 and Context Precision@6=0.333. The visual-routing decision did not match the locked modality requirement for this question. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: The yellow change interval's sole function is to warn approaching traffic that its permission to proceed is ending, after which vehicles will be directed to stop (or, for protected/permissive turns, must complete the turn permissively). Its duration must be determined using engineering practices, with a recommended range of 3 to 6 seconds (longer values reserved for higher-speed approaches). Pre-yellow vehicular countdown displays may NOT be used: the MUTCD expressly prohibits signal displays intended to provide a "pre-yellow warning" at signalized locations, because research has found they increase crash frequency. Standards (mandatory): - "The duration of the yellow change interval shall be determined using engineering practices." - For pedestrian hybrid beacons: "The duration of the steady yellow change interval shall be determined using engineering practices in accordance with the provisions in Section 4F.17." - For emergency-vehicle hybrid beacons: "The duration of the steady yellow change interval shall be determined using engineering practices in accordance with the provisions in Section 4F.17." - As shown on the Section 4F.17 page [Image 2]: "Except for Warning Beacons mounted on advance warning signs on the approach to a signalized location (see Section 2C.35), signal displays that are intended to provide a 'pre-yellow warning' interval, such as flashing green signal indications, vehicular countdown displays, or other similar displays, shall not be used at a signalized location." Guidance (recommended): - "A yellow change interval should have a minimum duration of 3 seconds and a maximum duration of 6 seconds (see Section 4F.17). The longer intervals should be reserved for use on approaches with higher speeds." Visual evidence: - [Image 1] Figure 4I-4 shows the yellow change interval (of at least 3 seconds) in its possible relationships with the pedestrian buffer interval and red clearance interval. - [Image 2] shows Section 4F.17 in full, including the Support statement of the interval's exclusive warning function, the Standard requiring engineering practices for its duration, the 3–6 second Guidance, and the Standard prohibiting pre-yellow warning displays such as vehicular countdown displays. - [Image 3] shows Figure 4I-4 in context in Section 4I.06, illustrating how the yellow change interval relates to pedestrian intervals. - [Image 4] shows related Standards that the yellow change interval shall not be shortened or omitted during preemption and priority control transitions. - [Image 5] shows Section 4H.11 applying the Section 4F.17 duration provisions to bicycle signal phases and explaining the interval's warning function for bicyclists. Citations: - Section 4F.17 Support §1 (p.708) - Section 4F.17 Standard §3 (p.708) - Section 4H.11 Support §3 (p.717) - Section 4N.03 Standard §4 (p.743) - Section 4J.03 Standard §10 (p.730) - Section 4N.03 Guidance §5 (p.743) - Figure 4I-4 (p.724) - Page 749

**Gold answer:** The yellow change interval warns that permission to proceed or a protected movement is ending. Its duration must be determined using engineering practices, with guidance of 3 to 6 seconds. Pre-yellow displays such as vehicular countdowns or flashing green indications are prohibited, except for permitted advance-warning beacons.
