## T038

**Question:** What walking speed is generally used to calculate pedestrian clearance time, and what alternative speed may be used with an extended push-button feature?

**Grade:** C=1.00, Comp=1.00, F=1.00, Cit=1.00, Strict full credit=Yes

**Descriptive mean:** 100.0% (Full credit)

**Evaluation confidence:** 88%

**Rationale:** The response received full credit because the audit accepted the substantive answer, coverage of required elements, grounding, and evidence localization without a deduction. The locked benchmark marks the question as answerable, so the response needed to state and support the required MUTCD answer. Correctness rationale: The response states the locked conclusion, rule, value, or interpretation correctly. Completeness rationale: The audit accepted the response as covering all required answer elements. Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 4I.07, Section 4I.06, Section 4K.05, Figure 4I-4, Figure 2B-27, Page 765. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: Up to 4 feet per second may be used to evaluate sufficiency where an extended push-button press provides slower pedestrians a longer clearance time. | The general walking speed is 3.5 feet per second. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=1.000 and Context Precision@6=0.833. The visual-routing decision did not match the locked modality requirement for this question. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: The pedestrian clearance time is generally calculated using a walking speed of 3.5 feet per second, measured from the curb or edge of pavement to the far side of the traveled way or a suitable median. Where an extended push-button press feature is installed to give slower pedestrians a longer clearance time, a walking speed of up to 4 feet per second may be used to evaluate the sufficiency of the clearance time. A slower speed (less than 3.5 ft/s) should be considered where slower pedestrians or wheelchair users routinely use the crosswalk. Guidance (recommended): - "Except as provided in Paragraph 8 of this Section, the pedestrian clearance time should be sufficient to allow a pedestrian crossing in the crosswalk who left the curb or edge of pavement at the end of the WALKING PERSON (symbolizing WALK) signal indication to travel at a walking speed of 3.5 feet per second to at least the far side of the traveled way or to a median of sufficient width for pedestrians to wait." - "Where pedestrians who walk slower than 3.5 feet per second, or pedestrians who use wheelchairs, routinely use the crosswalk, a walking speed of less than 3.5 feet per second should be considered in determining the pedestrian clearance time." - The total of the walk interval and pedestrian clearance time should be sufficient for a pedestrian leaving the pedestrian detector (or a point 6 feet behind the curb face or pavement edge) at the beginning of the WALK indication to cross at 3 feet per second; any additional time needed should be added to the walk interval. Options (permitted): - "A walking speed of up to 4 feet per second may be used to evaluate the sufficiency of the pedestrian clearance time at locations where an extended push button press function has been installed to provide slower pedestrians an opportunity to request and receive a longer pedestrian clearance time." Passive pedestrian detection may also be used to automatically adjust the clearance time based on the pedestrian's actual walking speed or actual clearance of the crosswalk. - The additional time provided by an extended push button press may be added to either the walk interval or the pedestrian change interval. - Pedestrians may be provided with additional features such as increased crossing time, audible beaconing, or a speech push button information message as a result of an extended push button press. Visual evidence: [Image 1] Figure 4I-4 illustrates the pedestrian intervals, showing that the calculated pedestrian clearance time (see Section 4I.07) spans the pedestrian change interval and buffer interval. [Image 3] Figure 2B-27 shows the R10-32P plaque ("PUSH BUTTON FOR 2 SECONDS FOR EXTRA CROSSING TIME") associated with the extended push-button press feature. ## T039

Completeness rationale: The audit accepted the response as covering all required answer elements. Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 4I.04, Section 9E.12, Figure 4I-1, Figure 4I-4, Figure 9E-12, Page 759. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: It is mandatory at a crosswalk when the pedestrian change interval is more than 7 seconds. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=1.000 and Context Precision@6=0.667. The visual-routing decision did not match the locked modality requirement for this question. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: A countdown display is mandatory on all pedestrian signal heads at crosswalks where the pedestrian change interval is more than 7 seconds. It is also mandatory for the crosswalk crossing an approach where a bicycle box extends across more than one motor-vehicle approach lane. Where the pedestrian change interval is 7 seconds or less, a countdown display is optional. Standards (mandatory): - "All pedestrian signal heads used at crosswalks where the pedestrian change interval is more than 7 seconds shall include a pedestrian change interval countdown display in order to inform pedestrians of the number of seconds remaining in the pedestrian change interval." - "Countdown pedestrian signals (see Section 4I.04) for the crosswalk or pedestrian crossing movement that crosses the approach shall accompany bicycle boxes that extend across more than one approach lane for motor vehicles. Countdown pedestrian signals used with bicycle boxes shall display the pedestrian change interval countdown without the need for actuation." - "Countdown pedestrian signals shall consist of Portland orange numbers that are at least 6 inches in height on a black opaque background. The countdown pedestrian signal shall be located immediately adjacent to the associated UPRAISED HAND (symbolizing DONT WALK) pedestrian signal head indication (see Figure 4I-1)." - "The countdown pedestrian signal shall display the number of seconds remaining until the termination of the pedestrian change interval (flashing UPRAISED HAND). Countdown displays shall not be used during the walk interval. Countdown displays shall not be used during the red clearance interval of a concurrent vehicular phase that is ending simultaneously with or after the end of the pedestrian phase." Options (permitted): - "Pedestrian signal heads used at crosswalks where the pedestrian change interval is 7 seconds or less may include a pedestrian change interval countdown display in order to inform pedestrians of the number of seconds remaining in the pedestrian change interval." Visual evidence: [Image 1] Figure 4I-4 shows the countdown displayed during the flashing UPRAISED HAND (pedestrian change interval) and notes "The countdown display is optional for Pedestrian Change Intervals of 7 seconds or less." [Image 2] Figure 9E-12 shows a bicycle box across multiple lanes with the callout "Pedestrian signal with countdown display (required where bicycle box crosses more than one lane)." [Image 3] Page 759 shows Figure 4I-1 depicting typical pedestrian signal indications with the countdown display located adjacent to the UPRAISED HAND. [Image 4] Page 761 contains the Section 4I.04 text establishing the option for change intervals of 7 seconds or less and the standards for countdown display design and operation. [Image 5] Page 1148 repeats the bicycle-box layout showing countdown pedestrian signals required where the bicycle box crosses more than one lane. [Image 6] Page 765 shows Figure 4I-4 illustrating the pedestrian intervals and countdown timing relationships. Citations: - Section 4I.04 Standard §1 (p.720) - Section 9E.12 Standard §8 (p.1105) - Section 4I.04 Standard §4 (p.720) - Section 4I.04 Standard §6 (p.720) - Section 4I.04 Option §2 (p.720) - Figure 4I-4 (p.724) - Figure 9E-12 (p.1107) - Page 759 - Page 761 - Page 1148 - Page 765

**Gold answer:** It is mandatory at a crosswalk when the pedestrian change interval is more than 7 seconds.

Correctness rationale: The response states the locked conclusion, rule, value, or interpretation correctly. Completeness rationale: The audit accepted the response as covering all required answer elements. Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 4J.03, Section 4J.02, Figure 4J-3, Page 770, Page 771, Page 769. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: Flashing circular yellow, then steady circular yellow, then both steady circular red during the walk interval, then alternating flashing circular red during the pedestrian change… Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=0.975 and Context Precision@6=0.833. The visual-routing decision did not match the locked modality requirement for this question. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: After a pedestrian actuation, the beacon must display a flashing circular yellow, then a steady circular yellow, then both steady circular red indications during the pedestrian walk interval, then alternating flashing circular red indications during the pedestrian change interval, and finally revert to dark. Between actuations the beacon must remain dark (not illuminated). Figure 4J-3 illustrates this six-interval sequence, with optional steady red clearance and buffer intervals permitted. Standards (mandatory): - "Following an actuation by a pedestrian, a pedestrian hybrid beacon face shall display a flashing CIRCULAR yellow signal indication, followed by a steady CIRCULAR yellow signal indication, followed by both steady CIRCULAR RED signal indications during the pedestrian walk interval, followed by alternating flashing CIRCULAR RED signal indications during the pedestrian change interval (see Figure 4J-3). Upon ... termination of the pedestrian change interval, the pedestrian hybrid beacon faces shall revert to a dark (not illuminated) condition." - "Pedestrian hybrid beacon indications shall be dark (not illuminated) during periods between actuations." - "A pedestrian hybrid beacon face shall consist of three signal sections, with a CIRCULAR YELLOW signal indication centered below two horizontally-aligned CIRCULAR RED signal indications (see Figure 4J-3)." Guidance (recommended): - If the pedestrian hybrid beacon is coordinated as part of a signal system, it should remain in the dark condition after a pedestrian actuation is received until the point in the background cycle when the predetermined duration of the flashing yellow interval needs to be initiated to achieve the appropriate coordinated offset. - If installed within a signal system, the pedestrian hybrid beacon should be coordinated. Options (permitted): - If a minimum dark time between activations has been set on the controller, the beacon may remain in the dark condition after a pedestrian actuation has been received until the minimum dark time has been provided. - Per the note in Figure 4J-3, an optional steady red clearance interval may be added after Interval 3 (steady yellow), and an optional short buffer interval (alternating flashing red while the pedestrian signal heads display a steady UPRAISED HAND) may be added after Interval 5. Visual evidence: [Image 1] Figure 4J-3 depicts the six-interval sequence — (1) dark until activated, (2) flashing yellow upon activation, (3) steady yellow, (4) steady red during the pedestrian walk interval, (5) alternating flashing red during the pedestrian change interval, and (6) dark again until activated — with a note allowing optional clearance and buffer intervals. [Image 2] Page 770 shows the same figure and the Section 4J.03 Standard establishing the mandatory sequence following pedestrian actuation. [Image 3] Page 771 contains the continuation of Section 4J.03, including the dark-condition requirement upon termination of the change interval, coordination guidance, and the optional steady red clearance and flashing red buffer intervals. [Image 4] Page 769 shows Section 4J.02 requiring the beacon face to consist of a circular yellow indication centered below two horizontally-aligned circular red indications. [Image 5] Page 20 is a table of contents listing Chapter 4J sections and provides no substantive detail on the sequence. Citations: - Section 4J.03 Standard §2 (p.730) - Section 4J.03 Standard §1 (p.729) - Section 4J.02 Standard §2 (p.728) - Section 4J.03 Guidance §7 (p.730) - Section 4J.02 Guidance §4 (p.729) - Section 4J.03 Option §8 (p.730) - Figure 4J-3 (p.729)

**Gold answer:** Flashing circular yellow, then steady circular yellow, then both steady circular red during the walk interval, then alternating flashing circular red during the pedestrian change interval, followed by return to dark.

Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 4K.04, Section 6C.03, Section 4K.01, Figure 4I-2, Figure 4I-3, p.734. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: A locator tone must be incorporated | The push button must have a high-contrast vibrotactile arrow on the button, aligned parallel to travel on the associated crosswalk. Adjudication note: Correct arrow and locator-tone requirements; omits the normal 0.15-second duration and 1-second repetition. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=0.956 and Context Precision@6=0.667. The visual-routing decision did not match the locked modality requirement for this question. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: Each accessible pedestrian push button must have a vibrotactile arrow on the button itself that clearly indicates which crosswalk signal it actuates; the arrow must have high visual contrast and be aligned parallel to the direction of travel on the associated crosswalk. A locator tone is mandatory in the accessible pedestrian signal equipment to help pedestrians with vision disabilities find the vibrotactile arrow and the push button, although the tone may be deactivated during DONT WALK periods if a qualifying passive detection system is used. Standards (mandatory): - "To enable pedestrians with vision disabilities to distinguish and locate the appropriate push button at an accessible pedestrian signal location, and to help them align with the crosswalk, each push button shall clearly indicate by means of a vibrotactile arrow which crosswalk signal is actuated by the push button. Vibrotactile arrows shall be located on the button of the push button assembly, shall have high visual contrast (light on dark or dark on light), and shall be aligned parallel to the direction of travel on the associated crosswalk." - "A locator tone shall be incorporated into the accessible pedestrian signal equipment to help pedestrians with vision disabilities locate the vibrotactile arrow, and the associated push button if a push button is provided." Guidance (recommended): - In temporary traffic control zones, if a pushbutton is used to provide equivalent TTC information to pedestrians with vision disabilities, the pushbutton should be equipped with a locator tone to notify pedestrians with vision disabilities that a special accommodation is available and to help them locate the pushbutton. Options (permitted): - The push button locator tone may default to a deactivated mode while the steady UPRAISED HAND (symbolizing DONT WALK) indication is displayed for the associated crosswalk, provided a passive pedestrian detection system activates the locator tone at all times (other than when the audible walk indication is active) that a pedestrian is present within a 12-foot radius from the push button location; where pedestrian facilities such as sidewalks are present, the passive detection requirement may be reduced to apply only to pedestrians on those facilities within the 12-foot radius. Visual evidence: [Image 1] shows the preferred (A), acceptable (B), and less-desirable (C) push button location areas on a corner, illustrating the placement context in which the push button's vibrotactile arrow must align parallel to the associated crosswalk. [Image 2] shows typical push button locations for four curb-ramp configurations (perpendicular, parallel, shared landing, and continuous surface), indicating where buttons are placed relative to ramps and crosswalks so pedestrians can align with the crossing. Citations: - Section 4K.04 Standard §1 (p.734) - Section 4K.04 Standard §2 (p.734) - Section 4K.04 Support §3 (p.734) - Section 4K.04 Option §5 (p.734) - Section 6C.03 Guidance §8 (p.781) - Section 4K.01 Support §11 (p.731) - Figure 4I-2 (p.721) - Figure 4I-3 (p.723)

**Gold answer:** The push button must have a high-contrast vibrotactile arrow on the button, aligned parallel to travel on the associated crosswalk. A locator tone must be incorporated; normally it is 0.15 seconds or less and repeats at 1-second intervals when the audible walk indication is not active, subject to stated exceptions.

The response identifies: Section 4S.01, Section 4I.06, Section 4L.03, Section 4L.01, Section 4L.02, Figure 4L-1. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: They are normally dark and operate only upon pedestrian actuation, stopping after the predetermined period or after a detected pedestrian clears. | When active, they use 75 rapid-flashing sequences per minute. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=1.000 and Context Precision@6=0.833. The visual-routing decision did not match the locked modality requirement for this question. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: RRFBs must remain dark until actuated by a pedestrian, then flash for a predetermined period (or, with passive detection, until the pedestrian clears the crosswalk). All RRFB units serving a given crosswalk must start and stop flashing simultaneously. When flashing, each unit's two yellow indications must use a rapid flash rate of 75 flashing sequences per minute — a specific exception to the normal beacon flash rate. Standards (mandatory): - "The RRFB shall be normally dark, shall initiate operation only upon pedestrian actuation, and shall cease operation at a predetermined time after the pedestrian actuation or, with passive detection, after the pedestrian clears the crosswalk." - "All RRFB units associated with a given crosswalk (including those with an advance crossing sign, if used) shall, when activated, simultaneously commence operation of their rapid flashing indications and shall cease operation simultaneously." - "When activated, the two yellow indications in each RRFB unit shall flash in a rapidly flashing sequence. As a specific exception to the requirements for the flash rate of beacons provided in Paragraph 3 of Section 4S.01, RRFBs shall use a much faster flash rate and shall provide 75 flashing sequences per minute." Guidance (recommended): - "The minimum duration of a predetermined period of operation of the RRFBs following each actuation should be based on the procedures for the timing of pedestrian clearance times for pedestrian signals (see Section 4I.06)." Options (permitted): - "A pedestrian-activated and/or bicyclist-activated rectangular rapid flashing beacon (RRFB) may be used to provide supplemental emphasis to pedestrian, school, and trail warning signs at marked crosswalks across uncontrolled approaches." - "If used at intersections with two crosswalks on an uncontrolled approach, post-mounted RRFBs may be installed to face only one direction of travel at the first crosswalk that traffic encounters (see Figure 4L-1)." Visual evidence: - [Image 1] Figure 4L-1 shows an example RRFB installation at uncontrolled, marked crosswalks at an intersection, with Note 1 stating that when activated, the RRFBs on both approaches shall simultaneously commence and cease operation of their rapid flashing indications. - [Image 2] The full page view of the Section 4L.03 Standard text shows the normally-dark/actuation-only requirement, the simultaneous start-and-stop requirement, the 75-flashing-sequences-per-minute rate, and the detailed 800-millisecond left/right flashing sequence (steps A–G). - [Image 3] The continuation page shows the remainder of the flashing sequence (steps H–L) and the Standard that the flash rate of each individual RRFB indication shall not be more than 5 flashes per second, to avoid frequencies that might cause seizures. - [Image 4] The full page view containing Figure 4L-1 reiterates in the figure notes that RRFBs on both approaches shall simultaneously commence and cease operation. - [Image 5] The full page view of Sections 4L.01–4L.02 shows the Option that a pedestrian- and/or bicyclist-activated RRFB may be used at marked crosswalks across uncontrolled approaches. Citations: - Section 4L.03 Standard §1 (p.738) - Section 4L.03 Standard §2 (p.738) - Section 4L.03 Standard §6 (p.738) - Section 4L.03 Guidance §3 (p.738) - Section 4L.01 Option §1 (p.736) - Section 4L.02 Option §11 (p.738) - Figure 4L-1 (p.737) - Page 779 - Page 780 - Page 778 - Page 777

**Gold answer:** They are normally dark and operate only upon pedestrian actuation, stopping after the predetermined period or after a detected pedestrian clears. All RRFB units for the crosswalk begin and end together. When active, they use 75 rapid-flashing sequences per minute.

Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: Road-user and worker safety and accessibility must be treated as integral, high-priority considerations from planning through design and construction, including motorists, bicycli… Adjudication note: Correct high-priority lifecycle and user coverage; rail-transit coordination is not stated in the direct answer. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: The MUTCD establishes that road user and worker safety and accessibility should be an integral, high-priority element of every project involving temporary traffic control, from planning through design and construction. This priority extends to all motorists, bicyclists, pedestrians (including those with disabilities), and workers at all times, and TTC operations should minimize inconvenience, delay, and crash potential to affected traffic. These are Guidance (recommended) provisions; no mandatory Standard on this priority appears in the evidence. Guidance (recommended): - "Road user and worker safety and accessibility in TTC zones should be an integral and high-priority element of every project from planning through design and construction. Similarly, maintenance and utility work should be planned and conducted with the safety and accessibility of all motorists, bicyclists, pedestrians (including those with disabilities), and workers being considered at all times. If the TTC zone includes a grade crossing, early coordination with the railroad company or light rail transit agency should take place." - The seven fundamental principles of TTC include: general plans or guidelines should be developed to provide safety for motorists, bicyclists, pedestrians, workers, and enforcement/emergency officials; the basic safety principles governing permanent roadway design should also govern TTC zone design; a TTC plan appropriate to the project's complexity should be prepared and understood by all responsible parties before the site is occupied; and road user movement should be inhibited as little as practical. - "Temporary traffic control operations should minimize the inconvenience, delay, and crash potential to affected traffic. Prior notice should be given to affected public or private agencies, emergency services, businesses, railroad companies or transit agencies, and road users before the free movement of road users or rail traffic is infringed upon or blocked." Support (context, non-normative): - When the normal function of the roadway is suspended, TTC planning provides for continuity of movement of motor vehicle, bicycle, and pedestrian traffic (including accessible passage), transit operations, and access (and accessibility) to property and utilities. - Temporary traffic control planning at grade crossings similarly provides for continuity of operations, including pedestrian and bicyclist movement and access to property/utilities. - The overall purpose of the MUTCD is to establish uniform national criteria for traffic control devices that meet the needs and expectancy of road users on all facilities, including pedestrian and bicycle facilities. Visual evidence: [Image 6] shows the full text of Section 6A.02 "Fundamental Principles of Temporary Traffic Control," including the high-priority safety and accessibility Guidance in paragraph 01 and the seven fundamental principles in paragraph 02. [Image 8] shows Section 8A.13 "Temporary Traffic Control Zones," including the Guidance that TTC operations should minimize inconvenience, delay, and crash potential to affected traffic. [Image 5] shows the table of contents for Part 6, confirming Chapter 6C is devoted to "Pedestrian and Worker Safety," including accessibility considerations. [Image 7] shows Section 6L.01, whose Support notes that accessible pedestrian signals provide non-visual information so pedestrians with vision disabilities can cross safely along an alternate route in TTC zones. Citations: - Section 6A.02 Guidance §1 (p.766) - Section 6A.02 Guidance §2 (p.768) - Section 8A.13 Guidance §7 (p.992) - Section 6A.01 Support §3 (p.765) - Section 8A.13 Support §1 (p.992) - Section 1A.01 Support §1 (p.1) - Page 807 - Page 1033 - Page 22 - Page 865

**Gold answer:** Road-user and worker safety and accessibility must be treated as integral, high-priority considerations from planning through design and construction, including motorists, bicyclists, pedestrians with disabilities, workers, and coordination with rail transit where applicable.

The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: All workers within the right-of-way who are within a TTC zone—during both daytime and nighttime activity—must wear high-visibility safety apparel meeting ANSI/ISEA 107-2015 Performance Class 2 or 3 requirements, with the appropriate class selected by an employer-designated safety person. This requirement extends to uniformed law enforcement directing traffic and, with limited exceptions, to firefighters and other emergency responders; certain responders may instead wear ANSI/ISEA 207-2006 public safety vests. Standards (mandatory): - "For daytime and nighttime activity, all workers, including emergency responders, within the right-of-way who are within the TTC zone shall wear high-visibility safety apparel that meets the Performance Class 2 or 3 requirements of the ANSI/ISEA 107-2015 publication entitled 'American National Standard for High-Visibility Safety Apparel and Headwear,' or equivalent revisions, except as provided in Paragraph 4 of this Section. A person designated by the employer to be responsible for worker safety shall make the selection of the appropriate class of garment." - "When uniformed law enforcement personnel are used to direct traffic, to investigate crashes, or to handle lane closures, obstructed roadways, and disasters, high-visibility safety apparel as described in this Section shall be worn by the law enforcement personnel." - "Except as provided in Paragraph 6 of this Section, firefighters or other emergency responders working within the right-of-way shall wear high-visibility safety apparel as described in this Section." Guidance (recommended): - For flagger wear during nighttime activity, high-visibility safety apparel meeting the Performance Class 3 requirements of ANSI/ISEA 107-2015 (or equivalent revision), and labeled as meeting the ANSI 107-2015 standard performance for Class 3 risk exposure, should be worn. - All on-scene responders and news media personnel at traffic incidents should constantly be aware of their visibility to oncoming traffic and wear high-visibility apparel. Options (permitted): - Emergency and incident responders and law enforcement personnel within the TTC zone may wear high-visibility safety apparel that meets the performance requirements of ANSI/ISEA 207-2006 ("American National Standard for High-Visibility Public Safety Vests"), or equivalent revisions, and labeled as ANSI 207-2006, in lieu of ANSI/ISEA 107-2015 apparel. Visual evidence: [Image 5] shows the Part 6 table of contents listing Section 6C.05 "High-Visibility Safety Apparel" within Chapter 6C, Pedestrian and Worker Safety. [Image 6] shows the full text of Section 6C.05, including the Standard, Option, and Guidance paragraphs on apparel requirements. The remaining images ([Image 1], [Image 2], [Image 3], [Image 4], [Image 7], [Image 8]) depict signs, symbols, and mounting methods and do not bear directly on apparel requirements. Citations: - Section 6C.05 Standard §1 (p.782) - Section 6C.05 Standard §3 (p.782) - Section 6C.05 Standard §5 (p.782) - Section 6C.05 Option §4 (p.782) - Section 6C.05 Guidance §7 (p.783) - Section 6O.01 Guidance §7 (p.854) - Page 22 - Page 823

**Gold answer:** Workers, including emergency responders, within the right-of-way and TTC zone must wear ANSI/ISEA 107-2015 Performance Class 2 or 3 apparel, or an equivalent revision, subject to specified responder exceptions. The employer's designated safety person selects the appropriate class.

Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=1.000 and Context Precision@6=0.167. The visual-routing decision did not match the locked modality requirement for this question. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: Flagger qualifications are addressed as Guidance in Section 6D.01. Because flaggers are responsible for public safety and have more public contact than any other highway workers, they should be trained in traffic control practices and public contact techniques, and should be able to demonstrate five specific abilities: clear communication, quick maneuvering, control of signaling devices, applying traffic control practices under stress, and recognizing dangerous situations in time to warn workers. There is no mandatory (Standard) provision on flagger qualifications themselves. Guidance (recommended): - Because flaggers are responsible for public safety and make the greatest number of contacts with the public of all highway workers, they should be trained in proper traffic control practices and public contact techniques. - Flaggers should be able to satisfactorily demonstrate: (A) ability to receive and communicate specific instructions clearly, firmly, and courteously; (B) ability to move and maneuver quickly in order to avoid danger from errant vehicles; (C) ability to control signaling devices (such as paddles and flags) in order to provide clear and positive guidance to drivers approaching a TTC zone in frequently changing situations; (D) ability to understand and apply proper traffic control practices, sometimes in stressful or emergency situations; and (E) ability to recognize dangerous traffic situations and warn workers in sufficient time to avoid injury. - Related context: if manual traffic control is needed, it should be provided by qualified flaggers or uniformed law enforcement officers. Visual evidence: [Image 5] shows the printed MUTCD page containing Section 6D.01, Qualifications for Flaggers, with the five lettered abilities (A–E) listed under Guidance. [Image 6] shows related flagger procedure and flagger station provisions (Sections 6D.05 and 6D.06) that a qualified flagger must be able to apply. [Image 2] and [Image 4] illustrate typical applications where a flagger (red symbol) controls one-lane, two-way traffic. [Image 1] shows the legend defining the flagger symbol used in the typical application diagrams. Citations: - Section 6D.01 Guidance §1 (p.783) - Section 6O.01 Guidance §16 (p.855) - Page 824 - Page 827 - Figure 6B-3 (p.777) - Figure 6L-2 (p.829) - Table 6P-2 (p.859)

**Gold answer:** A flagger should communicate instructions clearly and courteously; move quickly to avoid danger; control paddles or flags to give clear guidance; understand and apply traffic-control practices under stress; and recognize dangerous situations and warn workers in time.

The response identifies: Section 6E.01, Section 6H.17, Section 6E.02, Section 6E.03, Section 6E.04, Section 6E.05. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: Methods include flaggers, flag transfer, a pilot car, temporary traffic signals, or stop/yield control. | Movements from both ends must be coordinated, except for a short, low-volume, mutually visible work zone that can self-regulate. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=0.947 and Context Precision@6=0.500. The visual-routing decision did not match the locked modality requirement for this question. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: When traffic in both directions must use a single lane for a limited distance, the MUTCD requires that movements from each end be coordinated, except that on short work spaces on low-volume roads with full mutual visibility the movement may be self-regulating. Permitted control methods include flaggers (one or two, depending on visibility), temporary traffic control signals, and — as explanatory cross-reference — the techniques described in Section 6E.01 for two-lane highway work. Standards (mandatory): - "Except as provided in Paragraph 4 of this Section, when traffic in both directions must use a single lane for a limited distance, movements from each end shall be coordinated." - For the related situation of two-lane, two-way traffic on one roadway of a normally-divided highway: "opposing vehicular traffic shall be separated with either temporary traffic barriers (concrete safety-shape or approved alternate), channelizing devices, Narrow Two-Way Traffic (W6-4) signs on flexible supports (see Section 6H.17), or a temporary raised island throughout the length of the two-way operation. The use of markings and complementary signing, by themselves, shall not be used." Options (permitted): - When a one-lane, two-way TTC zone is short enough for a flagger to see from one end of the zone to the other, traffic may be controlled by either a single flagger or by a flagger at each end of the section. - If the work space on a low-volume street or road is short and road users from both directions can see traffic approaching from the opposite direction through and beyond the worksite, the movement through the one-lane, two-way constriction may be self-regulating. - Traffic control signals may be used to control vehicular traffic movements in one-lane, two-way TTC zones (see Figure 6P-12 and Chapter 4O). Support (informational): - Techniques for controlling vehicular traffic under one-lane, two-way conditions are described in Section 6E.01. Visual evidence: - [Image 1] Figure 6P-12 (Typical Application 12) illustrates a one-lane, two-way operation controlled by temporary traffic control signals, with W3-3 signal-ahead warning signs, R10-6 STOP HERE ON RED signs, temporary markings, and tapered channelizing devices at each end. - [Image 2] Figure 6P-14 (Typical Application 14, Haul Road Crossing) shows the same one-lane, two-way constriction controlled two alternative ways: "A – Using temporary traffic control signals" (W3-3, R10-6) and "B – Using flaggers" (W20-7 flagger symbol sign and a flagger station 30 ft in advance), demonstrating the permitted control methods. - [Image 3] The full page view of Chapter 6E shows Section 6E.01 (coordination Standard and self-regulating Option), Section 6E.02 (Flagger Method), Section 6E.03 (Flag Transfer Method), and Section 6E.04 (Pilot Car Method) as the family of control methods for one-lane, two-way operation. - [Image 5] The full page view of Figure 6L-1 illustrates use of a STOP/SLOW Automated Flagger Assistance Device (AFAD) with gates, R1-1/W20-8 STOP/SLOW signs, and WAIT ON STOP / GO ON SLOW plaques at each end of a one-lane, two-way section, showing another way flagger-based control can be implemented. Citations: - Section 6E.01 Standard §1 (p.787) - Section 6E.01 Option §4 (p.787) - Section 6E.02 Option §2 (p.787) - Section 6E.05 Option §1 (p.788) - Section 6N.14 Standard §2 (p.850) - Section 6N.09 Support §6 (p.846) - Figure 6P-12 (p.883) - Figure 6P-14 (p.887) - Page 828 - Page 868

**Gold answer:** Movements from both ends must be coordinated, except for a short, low-volume, mutually visible work zone that can self-regulate. Methods include flaggers, flag transfer, a pilot car, temporary traffic signals, or stop/yield control.

Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: The assembly or S5-1 sign must be at or as near as practicable to the point where the reduced zone begins. | A School (S1-1) sign must be placed in advance of the first school-speed-limit sign in each direction. | The downstream end is normally marked with an END SCHOOL SPEED LIMIT (S5-3) sign, with an END SCHOOL ZONE (S5-2) allowed when the two zones end together. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: A School Speed Limit assembly (or S5-1 School Speed Limit When Flashing sign) must be placed at, or as near as practicable to, the point where the reduced school speed limit zone begins. A School (S1-1) sign is required in advance of the first School Speed Limit assembly or S5-1 sign encountered in each direction. The downstream end of the zone is normally marked with an END SCHOOL SPEED LIMIT (S5-3) sign, although an END SCHOOL ZONE (S5-2) sign may substitute where the reduced speed zone ends at the same point as the designated school zone. Standards (mandatory): - "A School Speed Limit assembly (see Figure 7B-1) or a School Speed Limit When Flashing (S5-1) sign (see Figure 7B-1) shall be used to indicate the speed limit where a reduced school speed limit zone has been established based upon an engineering study or where a reduced school speed limit is specified for such areas by statute. The School Speed Limit assembly or School Speed Limit When Flashing sign shall be placed at or as near as practicable to the point where the reduced school speed limit zone begins (see Figures 7B-2 and 7B-4)." - "If a reduced school speed limit zone has been established, a School (S1-1) sign shall be installed in advance (see Table 2C-3 for advance placement guidelines) of the first School Speed Limit sign assembly or S5-1 sign that is encountered in each direction as traffic approaches the reduced school speed limit zone (see Figures 7B-2 and 7B-4)." - "Except as provided in Paragraph 4 of this Section, the downstream end of an authorized and posted reduced school speed limit zone shall be identified with an END SCHOOL SPEED LIMIT (S5-3) sign (see Figures 7B-1, 7B-2, and 7B-4)." - "The static School Speed Limit assembly shall consist of a top plaque (S4-3P) with the legend SCHOOL, a Speed Limit (R2-1) sign, and a bottom plaque (S4-1P, S4-2P, S4-4P, or S4-6P) indicating the specific periods of the day and/or days of the week that the special school speed limit is in effect (see Figure 7B-1)." - "The School Speed Limit assembly shall be either a static sign assembly, a blank-out sign, or a changeable message sign (see Chapter 2L)." Options (permitted): - "The downstream end of a designated school zone may be identified with an END SCHOOL ZONE (S5-2) sign (see Figures 7B-1 and 7B-2)" — this S5-2 sign may be used instead of the S5-3 where the reduced school speed limit zone ends at the same point as the designated school zone. Visual evidence: - [Image 1] Figure 7B-2 shows the layout for a school zone with a school speed limit and school crossing, with the S1-1 School sign in advance, the School Speed Limit assembly (S4-3P/R2-1/S4-1P) at the start of the zone, and END SCHOOL SPEED LIMIT (S5-3) or END SCHOOL ZONE (S5-2) signs at the downstream end. - [Image 2] Figure 7B-4 shows the same sequence for a school zone with higher fines only for speeding: advance S1-1 sign, School Speed Limit assembly at the zone start, and S5-3 or S5-2 signs marking the end. - [Image 3] Figure 7B-1 illustrates the School Speed Limit assembly components (S4-3P SCHOOL plaque, R2-1 Speed Limit sign, S4-1P/S4-2P/S4-4P/S4-6P bottom plaques) as well as the S5-3 END SCHOOL SPEED LIMIT and S5-2 END SCHOOL ZONE signs. - [Image 4] Page 1019 contains Section 7B.05 Standards §1–§3 requiring placement at the zone beginning, an advance S1-1 sign, and the S5-3 end sign. - [Image 5] Page 1021 contains Section 7B.05 Standards §6–§7 defining the assembly composition and permitted formats. - [Image 6] Page 1014 shows Figure 7B-1 with the sign assemblies referenced in the requirements. - [Image 7] Page 1013 contains Section 7B.02 Option §7 permitting the END SCHOOL ZONE (S5-2) sign at the downstream end of a designated school zone. Citations: - Section 7B.05 Standard §1 (p.978) - Section 7B.05 Standard §2 (p.978) - Section 7B.05 Standard §3 (p.978) - Section 7B.05 Standard §7 (p.980) - Section 7B.05 Standard §6 (p.980) - Section 7B.02 Option §7 (p.976) - Figure 7B-2 (p.975) - Figure 7B-4 (p.979) - Figure 7B-1 (p.973) - Page 1019 - Page 1021 - Page 1014 - Page 1013

**Gold answer:** The assembly or S5-1 sign must be at or as near as practicable to the point where the reduced zone begins. A School (S1-1) sign must be placed in advance of the first school-speed-limit sign in each direction. The downstream end is normally marked with an END SCHOOL SPEED LIMIT (S5-3) sign, with an END SCHOOL ZONE (S5-2) allowed when the two zones end together.

Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: If green pavement is used, it must fill the full limits of the bicycle box. | The box must be at least 10 feet from its upstream edge near the motor-vehicle stop line to its downstream edge near the crosswalk or intersection, must include at least one bicyc… Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The visual-routing decision did not match the locked modality requirement for this question. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: A bicycle box must be at least 10 feet deep (measured from the upstream edge nearest the motor-vehicle stop line to the downstream edge nearest the crosswalk/intersection) and must contain at least one bicycle symbol marking. Operationally, turns on red must be prohibited from the lane where the box is placed, signal change/clearance intervals must be recalculated if the stop line is relocated upstream, and countdown pedestrian signals are required where the box extends across more than one approach lane. Green-colored pavement is optional, but if used it must cover the full limits of the bicycle box, since bicycle boxes are one of the applications to which green pavement is expressly limited. Standards (mandatory): - "If used, the distance from the upstream edge of the bicycle box that is nearest to the stop line for motor vehicles to the downstream edge of the bicycle box that is nearest the crosswalk or intersection shall be at least 10 feet. At least one bicycle symbol marking (see Figure 9E-12) shall be used in the bicycle box." - "If used, green-colored pavement shall be used in the full limits of the bicycle box." - "If used, green-colored pavement shall be limited to: A. Bicycle lanes…, B. Extensions of bicycle lanes through intersections…, C. Extensions of bicycle lanes through areas where motor vehicles enter a mandatory turn lane…, D. Two-stage bicycle turn boxes…, E. Bicycle Boxes (see Section 9E.12), and F. As a background for bicycle detector symbols…" - Additional operational Standards visible on the Section 9E.12 page: "Where an existing stop line for motor vehicles is relocated upstream to install a new bicycle box, the yellow change and red clearance intervals (see Section 4F.17) shall be recalculated and if necessary, reprogrammed to accommodate the length of the bicycle box."; "Countdown pedestrian signals … shall accompany bicycle boxes that extend across more than one approach lane for motor vehicles. Countdown pedestrian signals used with bicycle boxes shall display the pedestrian change interval countdown without the need for actuation."; and "Turns on red shall be prohibited from the lane where a bicycle box is placed." Guidance (recommended): - If green-colored pavement is used within separated bicycle lanes on an independent alignment, it should be used only at entrances to those facilities from roadways open to public travel or at conflict, weaving, or crossing locations. - From the Section 9E.12 page shown in the images: a bicycle lane should be used on the approach to a bicycle box; a bicycle box should not be contiguous with a crosswalk; and a stop line on the downstream end of the box should mark where bicycles are required to stop. Options (permitted): - Green-colored pavement may be installed for the entire length of a bicycle lane or bicycle lane extension or for only a portion (or portions) of it (this partial-application option applies to lanes/extensions; within a bicycle box, green must be full-limits per the Standard above). - From the Section 9E.12 page shown in the images: "Green-colored pavement may be used in a bicycle box." Visual evidence: [Image 1] Figure 9E-12 (Sheet 1) depicts a single-lane bicycle box with the "10 ft MIN." dimension between the intersection stop line and advance stop line, bicycle symbol markings, optional green pavement, and accompanying R3-7R and R4-4 signs. [Image 2] shows Figure 9E-12 (Sheet 2), a bicycle box across multiple lanes with the required countdown pedestrian signals, the NO TURN ON RED (R10-11) sign, and the STOP HERE ON RED (R10-6a) sign with EXCEPT BICYCLES (R3-7bP) plaque. [Image 3] repeats Sheet 1 of Figure 9E-12 with the same 10-foot minimum dimension and green-pavement labeling. [Image 4] shows the Section 9E.12 text page, including the Standards on 10-foot minimum depth, signal-interval recalculation, countdown pedestrian signals, and the prohibition of turns on red, plus the Option that green-colored pavement may be used in a bicycle box. [Image 5] shows Section 3H.06, whose Standard limits green-colored pavement to enumerated bicycle facility applications including Bicycle Boxes. Citations: - Section 9E.12 Standard §6 (p.1105) - Section 9E.12 Standard §13 (p.1106) - Section 3H.06 Standard §2 (p.626) - Section 3H.06 Guidance §5 (p.626) - Section 3H.06 Option §7 (p.626) - Section 3H.06 Support §1 (p.625) - Figure 9E-12 (p.1106) - Page 1146 - Page 1147 - Page 1148 - Page 666

**Gold answer:** The box must be at least 10 feet from its upstream edge near the motor-vehicle stop line to its downstream edge near the crosswalk or intersection, must include at least one bicycle symbol, and turns on red must be prohibited from the lane containing the box. If green pavement is used, it must fill the full limits of the bicycle box.

## T049

**Question:** According to the MUTCD, what exact percentage reduction in crashes is guaranteed by installing a STOP sign or all-way stop control?

**Grade:** C=1.00, Comp=1.00, F=1.00, Cit=1.00, Strict full credit=Yes

**Descriptive mean:** 100.0% (Full credit)

**Evaluation confidence:** 88%

**Rationale:** The response received full credit because the audit accepted the substantive answer, coverage of required elements, grounding, and evidence localization without a deduction. The locked benchmark marks the question as unanswerable, and the audit accepted the response as handling the unsupported premise without fabricating a MUTCD rule. Correctness rationale: The response states the locked conclusion, rule, value, or interpretation correctly. Completeness rationale: The audit accepted the response as covering all required answer elements. Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 2B.11, Section 2B.13, Section 2B.14, Section 2B.15, Section 2B.16, p.78. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: The automated gold comparison did not isolate a high-overlap clause. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=0.893 and Context Precision@6=0.667. The visual-routing decision did not match the locked modality requirement for this question. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: The MUTCD does not state or guarantee any percentage reduction in crashes from installing a STOP sign or all-way stop control — the premise of the question is not supported by the evidence. Instead, the Manual uses crash-frequency thresholds (numbers of reported crashes over 12- or 36-month periods) as factors in warrants and guidance, and it requires that decisions be based on engineering judgment or an engineering study, with the expectation only that the crashes be "of a type susceptible to correction" by the control — not that a specific reduction is assured. Guidance (recommended): - Minor-road stop control should be considered when, among other conditions, "Crash records indicate that: 1. For a four-leg intersection, there are three or more reported crashes in a 12-month period or six or more reported crashes in a 36-month period... 2. For a three-leg intersection, there are three or more reported crashes in a 12-month period or five or more reported crashes in a 36-month period. The crashes should be susceptible to correction by installation of minor-road stop control." No percentage reduction is stated or guaranteed. Options (permitted): - All-way stop control may be installed where an engineering study indicates, for a four-leg intersection, "five or more reported crashes in a 12-month period or six or more reported crashes in a 36-month period that were of a type susceptible to correction by the installation of all-way stop control" (four or more in 12 months, or five or more in 36 months, for a three-leg intersection). This is a crash-count warrant, not a promised crash reduction. - Other all-way stop warrants (sight distance, transition to signal or yield control at a circular intersection, and 8-hour volume) likewise permit installation based on study findings and contain no crash-reduction percentage. Citations: - Section 2B.11 Guidance §1 (p.78) - Section 2B.13 Option §1 (p.79) - Section 2B.14 Option §1 (p.79) - Section 2B.15 Option §1 (p.79) - Section 2B.16 Option §1 (p.79)

**Gold answer:** The MUTCD does not specify this information. A correct response should state that the requested value or requirement cannot be determined from the manual rather than inventing an answer.

The speed limits displayed shall be in multiples of 5 mph." - "Speed zones (other than statutory speed limits) shall only be established on the basis of an engineering study that has been performed in accordance with traffic engineering practices. The engineering study shall consider the roadway context." ILEV—inherently low emission vehicle." Guidance (recommended): - "The Inherently Low Emission Vehicle (ILEV) (R3-10a) sign (see Figure 2G-1) should be used when it is permissible for a properly labeled and certified ILEV, regardless of the number of occupants, to use an HOV lane." Note that eligibility hinges on being "properly labeled and certified," not on fuel economy. Standards (mandatory): - "The abbreviations shown in Table 1D-3 shall not be used in connection with traffic control devices because of their potential to be misinterpreted by road users." Visual evidence: [Image 1] shows Table 1D-3, "Unacceptable Abbreviations," where the row for "DLY" lists the intended word as "Delay" and the common misinterpretation as "Daily." **Gold answer:** It is intended to mean 'Delay' but can be misinterpreted as 'Daily.'

Guidance (recommended): - "Shapes that are exclusive to a particular sign (such as an octagon for STOP, a pennant for NO PASSING ZONE, or a circle for Railroad Advance) should not be obscured by another sign mounted on the back of the same assembly protruding or extending beyond the edge of the sign with the exclusive shape." Recommended alternatives include installing the signs on separate mountings, increasing the size of the exclusive-shape sign, or increasing its mounting height. Visual evidence: - [Image 1] Table 2A-1 shows the shape-to-sign pairings, listing Octagon with Stop (R1-1), Circle with Grade Crossing Advance Warning (W10-1), and Pennant with No Passing Zone (W14-3), all marked with the exclusivity asterisks. - [Image 2] Figure 2A-1 illustrates a STOP sign as an octagon (example G, LEDs in border), consistent with the octagon's exclusive use for the Stop sign. - [Image 4] Page 231 (Section 2C.53) confirms the pennant shape requirement, stating the NO PASSING ZONE (W14-3) sign shall be a pennant-shaped isosceles triangle with its longer axis horizontal and pointing to the right. Citations: - Section 2A.05 Standard §1 (p.42) - Section 2A.05 Guidance §3 (p.42) - Table 2A-1 (p.42) - Page 83 - Page 231

**Gold answer:** Octagon: STOP; circle: Grade Crossing Advance Warning; pennant: NO PASSING ZONE.

Options (permitted): - "Warning signs used for TTC incident management situations may have a black legend and border on a fluorescent pink background." - "Guide signs used in TTC incident management situations may have a black legend and border on a fluorescent pink background." - "Detour signs in TTC incident management situations may have a black legend and border on a fluorescent pink background." - "Warning and guide signs used for TTC traffic incident management situations may have a black legend and border on a fluorescent pink background (see Figure 6O-1)." **Model answer:** Direct Answer: For white-on-red signs, Table 2A-5 specifies minimum maintained retroreflectivity of W ≥ 35 for the white and R ≥ 7 for the red (in cd/lx/m², measured at an observation angle of 0.2° and an entrance angle of -4.0°), applicable across all sheeting types. As an additional criterion (footnote 4), the sign must maintain a minimum contrast ratio ≥ 3:1, computed as white retroreflectivity divided by red retroreflectivity. Agencies are required to use an assessment or management method designed to maintain signs at or above these levels. Standards (mandatory): - "Public agencies or officials having jurisdiction shall use an assessment or management method that is designed to maintain sign retroreflectivity at or above the minimum levels in Table 2A-5." Guidance (recommended): - Except for signs identified in Paragraph 5, one or more of the methods described in "Maintaining Traffic Sign Retroreflectivity" (FHWA-SA-07-020, Revised 2013), or a method developed based on an engineering study, should be used to maintain sign retroreflectivity at or above the Table 2A-5 minimums; signs identified as being below the minimum levels should be replaced. Options (permitted): - Table 2A-5 values for White on Red: "W ≥ 35; R ≥ 7," with footnote 4: "Minimum sign contrast ratio ≥ 3:1 (white retroreflectivity ÷ red retroreflectivity)." - The table's values "are in units of cd/lx/m² measured at an observation angle of 0.2° and an entrance angle of -4.0°." Visual evidence: [Image 1] shows Table 2A-5, whose "White on Red" row lists W ≥ 35; R ≥ 7 spanning all beaded and prismatic sheeting columns, with the Additional Criteria column referencing footnote 4 (contrast ratio ≥ 3:1). [Image 3] (full page view of Table 2A-5's page) confirms the same White on Red row and footnote 4 defining the 3:1 white-to-red contrast ratio. Citations: - Table 2A-5 (p.62) - Section 2A.22 Option §3 (p.63) - Section 2A.22 Option §4 (p.63) - Section 2A.22 Standard §2 (p.62) - Section 2A.22 Guidance §4 (p.62)

Visual evidence: [Image 1] shows Table 4D-2, "Minimum Sight Distance for Signal Visibility," with the row for 45 mph listing a minimum sight distance of 460 feet, and a note that distances are derived from stopping sight distance plus an assumed queue length for shorter cycle lengths (60 to 75 seconds). [Image 2] shows the full page 670 context of Section 4D.06 containing Table 4D-2 with the same 45 mph / 460 feet value. ## TB020

**Question:** What is the key difference between automation Levels 3 and 4 in Table 5A-1 regarding a human response to a request to intervene?

**Grade:** C=1.00, Comp=1.00, F=1.00, Cit=1.00, Strict full credit=Yes

**Descriptive mean:** 100.0% (Full credit)

Correctness rationale: The response states the locked conclusion, rule, value, or interpretation correctly. Completeness rationale: The audit accepted the response as covering all required answer elements. Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 5A.03, Table 5A-1, Page 801, p.760. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: At Level 4, the ADS performs the task even if the human driver does not respond appropriately. | At Level 3, the ADS performs the dynamic driving task within its operational design domain with the expectation that the human driver will respond appropriately to a request to in… Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=1.000 and Context Precision@6=0.167. The visual-routing decision matched the locked modality requirement. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer. The question required 1 locked visual entity or entities. Exact visual hit=1.00, visual recall=1.00, and visual precision=1.00.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: The key difference is the reliance on the human driver as a fallback. At Level 3, the ADS performs the Dynamic Driving Task within its ODD "with the expectation that the human driver will respond appropriately to a request to intervene," whereas at Level 4 the ADS continues to perform the task "even if a human driver does not respond appropriately to a request to intervene." In other words, Level 4 does not depend on the human driver taking over when requested; Level 3 does. Standards (mandatory): - None. Table 5A-1 and the associated definitions are informational (Support), not normative requirements. Visual evidence: [Image 1] Table 5A-1 shows Level 3 as "The driving mode specific sustained performance by an ADS of all aspects of the Dynamic Driving Task within a given ODD with the expectation that the human driver will respond appropriately to a request to intervene," and Level 4 as the same sustained ADS performance "even if a human driver does not respond appropriately to a request to intervene"; both levels fall under the Automated Driving System (ADS) category. [Image 2] Page 801 reproduces Table 5A-1 with the same Level 3 and Level 4 descriptions, along with the Section 5A.03 definitions (e.g., ADS describes a Level 3, 4, or 5 driving automation system). Citations: - Table 5A-1 (p.760) - Section 5A.03 Support §1 (p.760) - Page 801

**Gold answer:** At Level 3, the ADS performs the dynamic driving task within its operational design domain with the expectation that the human driver will respond appropriately to a request to intervene. At Level 4, the ADS performs the task even if the human driver does not respond appropriately.
